Can deleterious mutations surf deterministic population waves? A functional law of large numbers for a spatial model of Muller’s ratchet
João Luiz de Oliveira Madeira, Marcel Ortgiese, Sarah Penington

Abstract
The spatial Muller’s ratchet is a model introduced by Foutel-Rodier and Etheridge [ 34 ] to study the impact of cooperation and competition on the fitness of an expanding asexual population. The model is an interacting particle system consisting of particles performing symmetric random walks that reproduce and die with rates that depend on the local number of particles. For each particle, we keep track of the number of deleterious mutations that it carries, and after each birth event, with some positive probability, the offspring particle can acquire an additional mutation that gives it a lower reproduction rate than its parent. We show that under an appropriate scaling, the process converges weakly to the solution of an infinite system of partial differential equations (PDEs), confirming non-rigorous computations of Foutel-Rodier and Etheridge in [ 34 ] . In the PDE limit, when the reaction term of the system of PDEs is monostable, we establish bounds on the ratio between the density of particles with a given number of mutations and the density of particles without mutations. If the reaction term satisfies a Fisher-KPP condition, we can also rigorously determine the spreading speed of the population into an empty habitat. Finally, by considering the PDE limit of a form of tracer dynamics, we answer the question of whether deleterious mutations can surf population waves in this setting.



Keywords:
Muller’s ratchet; spatial birth-death processes; hydrodynamic limits; non-local partial differential equations; gene surfing.

1
Introduction

In this article, we rigorously establish the scaling limit of a spatial reaction-diffusion system of interacting particles with countably infinitely many mutation types. The system is a generalisation of a model introduced by Foutel-Rodier and Etheridge in
[
34
]
to investigate the impact of cooperation and competition on the propagation of deleterious mutations through an asexual population expanding in space, where individual birth and death rates depend on the local population density. Before formally defining the interacting particle system in Section
2
, in this section we outline the biological motivation for the model, as well as the (non-rigorous) derivations in
[
34
]
that we prove in this article.

1.1
Motivation

As a central mechanism of evolution, when an individual passes its genetic material to its offspring, a mutation may occur and the genetic material is modified. This modification may have an effect on the fitness of the individual to survive and reproduce: a mutation may be deleterious (reducing fitness), advantageous (increasing fitness), or neutral (having no effect on fitness).

Although fitness is a key factor in determining the survival probability of an individual, in a spatially structured population this probability also depends on the local population density
[
34
]
. Higher density may either decrease survival probability due to competition or increase it due to cooperation.

It is known that rare neutral mutations can propagate across large regions during range expansion, a phenomenon called
gene surfing
, first described by Edmonds et al.
[
26
]
. Roughly speaking, if competition dominates over cooperation and so the per-capita growth rate is high at low population densities, then a neutral mutation appearing at the front of an expanding population (i.e. at the leading edge of the expanding population) can spread locally with high probability, since the population density at the front is usually low. Since colonisation of new habitat is driven by individuals near the front, such mutations can become prevalent over large spatial regions
[
34
]
.

Gene surfing of neutral mutations has been studied through simulations of discrete particle systems, partial differential equations (PDEs), and stochastic PDEs (see
[
26
,
39
,
70
]
), and rigorously in PDE-based models
[
35
,
70
]
. We also refer the reader to
[
70
]
for a discussion on the possible different definitions of gene surfing of neutral mutations. In contrast, the gene surfing of deleterious mutations has received less attention.

To address the question of whether deleterious mutations can also surf, Foutel-Rodier and Etheridge introduced the
spatial Muller’s ratchet

[
34
]
, a spatial extension of a mechanism first proposed by Muller
[
61
]
to explain the evolution of recombination and sexual reproduction. In asexual reproduction, chromosomes are inherited as indivisible units, so the number of mutations along an ancestral lineage can only increase. Since the majority of mutations in biological systems are deleterious
[
6
]
, this leads to a gradual decline in overall population fitness
[
28
]
. In Muller’s ratchet models, when all individuals in the most-adapted class in the population acquire at least one additional deleterious mutation, the minimum mutational load in the population increases — this is known as a ‘click’ of the ratchet. Such models may help us to understand the accumulation of deleterious mutations in microorganisms, in mitochondrial DNA, and in the Y chromosome in mammals (see e.g.
[
42
,
2
,
46
]
and references therein).
This mechanism may also explain the evolutionary advantage of sexual reproduction, where recombination may
reduce mutation load along lineages, thereby preventing further clicks of the ratchet. From a mathematical perspective, Muller’s ratchet has been extensively studied in populations without spatial structure
[
36
,
66
,
38
,
28
,
57
]
.

The model in
[
34
]
shares the same key feature of deleterious mutations, but also includes a spatial component and density dependent birth and death rates. We now describe the model. Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
denote a scaling parameter. The population is subdivided into demes indexed by
L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, where
L_{N}>0
is a space renormalisation parameter. For
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
,
t\geq 0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, let
{\eta}^{N}_{k}(t,x)
indicate the number of particles (representing individuals in the population) carrying exactly
k
mutations living in deme
x
at time
t
, and let
\|{\eta}^{N}(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{\eta}_{k}^{N}(t,x)
. Each particle (independently) migrates at rate
m_{N}
, jumping to one of the two neighbouring demes with equal probability. Moreover, a particle located in deme
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
at time
t\geq 0
and carrying
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
mutations gives birth to a new particle at rate

r(1-s)^{k}\Bigg(B\frac{\|{\eta}^{N}(t-,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}}{N}+1\Bigg),

and dies at rate

r\frac{\|{\eta}^{N}(t-,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}}{N}\Bigg(B\frac{\|{\eta}^{N}(t-,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}}{N}+1\Bigg),

where
r\in(0,\infty)
is a Malthusian growth parameter,
s\in(0,1)
represents the impact on fitness of each deleterious mutation, and the parameter
B\in[0,\infty)
captures the relative effect of cooperation and competition in the local population dynamics. Here, larger values of
B
indicate a greater role of cooperation in the dynamics. Let
{\mu}\in(0,1)
. After a birth event, an offspring particle is added to the same deme as its parent: with probability
1-{\mu}
, the offspring inherits the same number of mutations as its parent, and with probability
{\mu}
, it accumulates an additional mutation.

Foutel-Rodier and Etheridge
consider a scaling regime in which
L_{N}
is of order
N
and
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{m_{N}}{L_{N}^{2}}=m\in(0,\infty)
. By performing a (non-rigorous) generator calculation, they conjecture that the number of particles in each deme (rescaled by
1/N
) converges as
N\rightarrow\infty

to the solution of the following system of PDEs: For each
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, for
t>0
,

\displaystyle{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}_{t}u_{k}=\frac{m}{2}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}u_{k}+r(B\|u\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}+1)(u_{k}(1-s)^{k}(1-{\mu})+\mathds{1}_{\{k\geq 1\}}u_{k-1}(1-s)^{k-1}{\mu}-u_{k}\|u\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}).

(1.1)

Foutel-Rodier and Etheridge also predict
[
34
, Equation (9)]
that the (critical) spreading speed into an empty habitat of a population governed by the dynamics of (
1.1
) depends on the parameter
B\in[0,\infty)
, and is given by
c^{*}>0
, where:

c^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\sqrt{2mr(1-{\mu})}&\textrm{if}&\displaystyle B\leq\frac{2}{1-{\mu}},\\
\displaystyle\frac{B(1-{\mu})+2}{2}\sqrt{\frac{mr}{B}}&\textrm{if}&\displaystyle B>\frac{2}{1-{\mu}}.\end{array}\right.

(1.2)

The change in the expression for the spreading speed at
B=\frac{2}{1-{\mu}}
reflects a transition from
pulled
to
pushed
expansion waves
[
70
,
35
]
. Heuristically, in the case of pulled waves, the critical speed is the same as for the system of PDEs linearised around the state
0
, i.e. the wave is pulled by its leading edge. On the other hand, in pushed waves, the critical speed is determined not only by the leading edge, but by the whole front, i.e. the wave is pushed from behind
[
35
]
. Pulled expansion waves are observed when the highest per-capita growth rate is achieved at very low population density, i.e. in particular when the population dynamics is of
Fisher-KPP
type
[
70
]
. On the other hand, when the per-capita growth rate is negative at very low population densities, i.e. when the dynamics displays a
strong Allee
effect, pushed expansion waves are observed
[
70
]
. When the maximum growth rate is achieved at an intermediate population density, but the population still exhibits growth even at low population densities, then the dynamics shows a
weak Allee
effect; in this case, the wave can be either pulled or pushed, depending on the model parameters. From (
1.2
), Foutel-Rodier and Etheridge conclude that for
B\in[0,2/(1-{\mu}))
, the dynamics of the system of PDEs (
1.1
) exhibits pulled behaviour, while for
B>2/(1-{\mu})
, the system exhibits pushed behaviour.

For an expanding population, in the PDE limit scaling, Foutel-Rodier and Etheridge also state that, under a weak selection–low mutation regime, i.e. assuming that
s,{\mu}\ll 1
, the stationary solution
\hat{u}=(\hat{u})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
of the system of PDEs (
1.1
) with
\hat{u}_{0}\neq 0
satisfies

\frac{\hat{u}_{k}}{\|\hat{u}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}}\approx\exp\left(-\frac{{\mu}}{s}\right)\frac{({\mu}/s)^{k}}{k!}\quad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

(1.3)

From a mathematical perspective, there are several challenges in rigorously proving the conjectures of Foutel-Rodier and Etheridge in
[
34
]
. First, since we allow for the existence of particles carrying an arbitrarily large number of mutations, we must keep track of infinitely many types of particles. Moreover, there are no
a priori
bounds on the number of particles per deme, and both birth and death rates are unbounded. Furthermore, the fact that particles at the same deme with different number of mutations interact with each other leads to ‘non-local interactions’ in both the interacting particle system and the system of PDEs (
1.1
).

In fact, it is highly non-trivial to show that the particle systems even exists (when started with an infinite number of particles), not only for the reasons mentioned above, but also because the system turns out to be non-monotone.
We address this question in our companion article
[
56
]
, where we construct a general version of the interacting particle system introduced in
[
34
]
rigorously. Moreover, we prove moment bounds that will also be essential in this article. In this article, we use the results in
[
56
]
and rigorously derive the scaling limit of the general spatial Muller’s ratchet model, and we confirm several of the conjectures of
[
34
]
. In particular, we prove convergence to the limiting PDE system under appropriate scaling assumptions, derive the spreading speed in the Fisher-KPP regime (that is, for
B\leq 1/(1-{\mu})
), and establish an evolution equation for the mutational profile that agrees with (
1.3
) in the weak selection–low mutation regime. Finally, we also address the question of gene surfing via a construction of tracer dynamics.

Structure of the article.
In Section
2
, we define the spatial Muller’s ratchet model, and in Sections
2.1
–
2.4
we state the main results of this article. In Section
2.5
, we briefly review some techniques commonly used to establish hydrodynamic limit results for interacting particle systems, and explain why they are not directly applicable to our setting. In Section
2.6
, we informally present the key ideas behind the proofs of our results. In Section
3
, we review some concepts that will be used in the article, including the notion of solutions to the (infinite) system of PDEs. The main results of our companion article
[
56
]
that will be used in this article are stated in Section
3.1
.

The proofs of our results can be found in Sections
4
–
8
. In Section
4
, we use a Green’s function representation of the random walk semigroup to establish regularity properties of the population density. We use these properties to derive tightness in the space of sequences of Radon measures in Section
5
. Tightness of the process in a suitable generalisation of the usual
L_{p}
space is established in Section
6
. Regularity and uniqueness properties of limiting solutions to a system of PDEs are established in Section
7
. In Section
8
, we use a Feynman-Kac formula to determine the asymptotic behaviour of the limiting system of PDEs. Appendix
0.A
contains standard results and technical estimates used in Sections
4
–
8
.

Notation.
Throughout the article we will use the following notation. Let
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
denote the space of summable real-valued sequences, i.e.

{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}={\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}\left(\mathbb{N}_{0}\right)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{z=\left(z_{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}:\,\|z\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}|z_{k}|<\infty\right\}.

Moreover, let
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
denote the subset of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
consisting of the summable sequences with non-negative entries. For any

K\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
[\![K]\!]\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\{1,2,\ldots,K\}
. Let
{\lambda}
be the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for any
d\in\mathbb{N}
. Let

\mathscr{C}_{c}(\mathbb{R})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{{\phi}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}:\,{\phi}\textrm{ is continuous and has compact support}\right\}.

Let
\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})
be the space of non-negative locally finite Radon measures on
\mathbb{R}
. For
{\varphi}\in\mathscr{C}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
and
{\rho}\in\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})
, we let

\langle{\rho},{\varphi}\rangle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\varphi}(x)\,{\rho}(dx).

We equip
\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})
with the vague topology to turn this space into a Polish space, and let
d_{\textrm{vague}}
denote a corresponding metric.

For a complete and separable metric space
(\mathcal{S},d_{\mathcal{S}})
, we let
\mathscr{D}([0,\infty),\mathcal{S})
denote the space of
\mathcal{S}
-valued càdlàg paths, and we equip
\mathscr{D}([0,\infty),\mathcal{S})
with the
J_{1}
-Skorokhod metric.

For a metric space
(\mathcal{S},d_{\mathcal{S}})
, we let
\mathscr{C}(\mathcal{S};\mathbb{R})
denote the space of continuous real-valued functions on
\mathcal{S}
. Let
\mathscr{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
denote the set of continuously differentiable real-valued functions defined on
\mathbb{R}
. Let
\mathscr{C}^{1,2}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
denote the set of real-valued functions on
(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
that are continuously differentiable in the first coordinate and twice continuously differentiable in the second coordinate.

For a set
\mathcal{S}
, suppose
f,g:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow(0,\infty)
are functions on
\mathcal{S}
. We write
f\lesssim g
to indicate that there exists a constant
C>0
such that for every
x\in\mathcal{S}
,
f(x)\leq Cg(x)
. If the constant
C
depends on a parameter
p
, then we write
f\lesssim_{p}g
. We say that sequences of positive real numbers
(a_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(b_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfy
a_{N}={\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}(b_{N})
as
N\rightarrow\infty
if

0<\liminf_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{a_{N}}{b_{N}}\leq\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{a_{N}}{b_{N}}<\infty.

For
z\in\mathbb{R}
, we let
z^{+}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=z\vee 0
.

2
Model definition and main results

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
be a scaling parameter and
L_{N}>0
a space renormalisation parameter depending on
N
. The model consists of particles moving on the rescaled one-dimensional lattice
L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, where we call each point
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
a
deme
. Each particle (representing an individual in the population that carries a unique chromosome) is characterised by two features: the number of deleterious mutations that it carries and its spatial location.
For each
t\geq 0
,
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, let
{\eta}^{N}_{k}(t,x)
denote the number of particles at deme
x
carrying exactly
k
mutations at time
t
. Note that for each deme
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, we can characterise the set of particles at this deme at time
t
by the sequence
{\eta}^{N}(t,x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=({\eta}^{N}_{k}(t,x))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
. The total number of particles living at deme
x
at time
t
is given by

\|{\eta}^{N}(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{\eta}^{N}_{k}(t,x)
.

Let
m_{N}>0
be the migration rate, and let
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be a sequence of fitness parameters, where
s_{k}\geq 0
denotes the fitness of a particle carrying
k
mutations. Let
q_{+},q_{-}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
be non-negative functions, and let
{\mu}\in[0,1]
be the mutation probability. We will precisely state our assumptions on
m_{N}
,
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
q_{+}
and
q_{-}
later in this section. The spatial Muller’s ratchet process
({\eta}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}=({\eta}_{k}^{N}(t,x):\,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z})_{t\geq 0}
can be described informally as follows:

•

Migration events
: For each
t\geq 0
and
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, each particle living at deme
x
at time
t
independently jumps at rate
m_{N}
to a uniformly chosen deme from
\{x-L_{N}^{-1},\,x+L_{N}^{-1}\}
.

•

Reproduction events
: For each
t\geq 0
,
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, each particle carrying
k
mutations at deme
x
at time
t
reproduces independently at rate
\displaystyle s_{k}q_{+}(\|{\eta}^{N}(t-,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}/N)
. After such an event, a new offspring particle will be added at deme
x
. With probability
1-{\mu}
, the offspring particle will carry
k
mutations, and with probability
{\mu}
it will carry
k+1
mutations. Note that in both cases, the parent particle remains alive and keeps the same number of mutations.

•

Death events
: For each
t\geq 0
and
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, each particle at deme
x
at time
t
dies independently at rate
\displaystyle q_{-}(\|{\eta}^{N}(t-,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}/N)
, regardless of its number of mutations. When a particle dies, it is simply removed from the process.

We will now state our conditions on
m_{N}
,
L_{N}
,
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
q_{+}
and
q_{-}
. The migration rate
m_{N}
and the space renormalisation parameter
L_{N}
will satisfy the following assumptions.

Assumption 1

(Space renormalisation)
.

The positive parameters
m_{N}
and
L_{N}
are such that

(i)

\displaystyle\frac{m_{N}}{L_{N}^{2}}\rightarrow m\in(0,\infty)
as
N\rightarrow\infty
.

(ii)

L_{N}={\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}(N)
as
N\rightarrow\infty
.

We assume that all the mutations in our model are deleterious, i.e. that
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is decreasing, so that any mutation decreases the rate of reproduction. More precisely, we assume:

Assumption 2

(Fitness parameters)
.

The sequence of fitness parameters
\left(s_{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

s_{0}=1
.

(ii)

s_{k}\geq 0
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

(iii)

\left(s_{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is monotonically non-increasing, i.e.
s_{k}\geq s_{k+1}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

(iv)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}s_{k}=0
.

Now, since in our model we do not impose
a priori
bounds on the number of particles per deme, we make the following assumptions on the birth and death rates.

Assumption 3

(Birth and death polynomial rates)
.

Suppose that
q_{+},q_{-}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
are polynomials such that
0\leq\deg q_{+}<\deg q_{-}
.

By Assumption
3
, the leading coefficient of the polynomial
q_{+}
is non-negative, and the leading coefficient of
q_{-}
is strictly positive. Note also that the scaling parameter
N
can be thought of as being proportional to the local carrying capacity of the population. Indeed, since
\deg q_{+}<\deg q_{-}
, when the number of particles at a deme is much larger than
N
, then death events happen at a higher rate than birth events, providing a local regulation mechanism for the population density.

The initial configurations of particles in
{{\eta}}^{N}
will be chosen in such a way that they converge as
N\rightarrow\infty
to a function
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
satisfying the following assumptions. Let
{\lambda}
denote the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
.

Assumption 4

(Initial condition)
.

Suppose
f=\left(f_{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

f
is continuous
{\lambda}
-almost everywhere, i.e. there exists
\mathcal{N}^{(1)}\subset\mathbb{R}
such that
{\lambda}(\mathcal{N}^{(1)})=0
and
f
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathcal{N}^{(1)}
.

(ii)

f\in L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
, i.e.
\esssup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\|f(x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}<\infty
.

(iii)

There exists
\mathcal{N}^{(2)}\subset\mathbb{R}
such that
{\lambda}(\mathcal{N}^{(2)})=0
and

\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\;\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathcal{N}^{(2)}}\;\sum_{j\geq k}f_{j}(x)=0.

We will clarify what we mean by
L_{p}
spaces of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
-valued functions in Section
3
. For a function
f=(f_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
satisfying Assumption
4
, for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we will define the initial condition of our process
{{\eta}}^{N}
as the configuration
\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}=({\eta}^{N}_{k}(x))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\in(\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}})^{L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}
such that for all
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

{{\eta}}^{N}_{k}(x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\lfloor L_{N}N\int_{x-\frac{1}{2}L_{N}^{-1}}^{x+\frac{1}{2}L_{N}^{-1}}f_{k}(y)\,dy\right\rfloor.

(2.1)

For
f
satisfying Assumption
4
, for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the initial configuration
\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}\in(\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}})^{L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}
given by (
2.1
) satisfies

\sup_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\;\|{\eta}^{N}(x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}<\infty\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\;\sup_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\;\sum_{j\geq k}\;{\eta}^{N}_{j}(x)=0.

(2.2)

In particular, for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the initial configuration
\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
satisfies
\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}\in\mathcal{S}^{N}
, where

\mathcal{S}^{N}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Bigg\{\boldsymbol{{\xi}}=({\xi}_{k}(x))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,x\in L^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\in(\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}})^{L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}:\;|||\boldsymbol{{\xi}}|||_{\mathcal{S}^{N}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sum_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\|{\xi}(x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}}{(1+|x|)^{2}}<\infty\Bigg\}.

(2.3)

We define a metric
d_{\mathcal{S}^{N}}
on
\mathcal{S}^{N}
by

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{S}^{N}}:\mathcal{S}^{N}\times\mathcal{S}^{N}

\displaystyle\rightarrow[0,\infty)

(2.4)

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{{\zeta}},\boldsymbol{{\xi}})

\displaystyle\mapsto d_{\mathcal{S}^{N}}(\boldsymbol{{\zeta}},\boldsymbol{{\xi}})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=|||\boldsymbol{{\zeta}}-\boldsymbol{{\xi}}|||_{\mathcal{S}^{N}}=\sum_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\|{\zeta}(x)-{\xi}(x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}}{(1+|x|)^{2}}.

Then the space
(\mathcal{S}^{N},d_{\mathcal{S}^{N}})
is a complete and separable metric space (see Proposition
3.1
below). In our companion article
[
56
]
,
we prove that, under Assumptions
2
and
3
, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, for an initial configuration
\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
satisfying (
2.2
), there exists a càdlàg
\mathcal{S}^{N}
-valued strong Markov process
({\eta}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
, with
{\eta}^{N}(0)=\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
almost surely, given by the unique weak limit of a sequence of
\mathcal{S}^{N}
-valued Markov processes, and such that
({\eta}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
evolves according to the informal description at the start of this section (see
[
56
, Theorem 2.2]
for a full statement of this result; we will formally state a version of the result that will suffice for our purposes in this article in Theorem
3.2
below). We also refer the reader to
[
56
, Section 3.3]
for an explanation of why (
2.2
) is required. We call the process
({\eta}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}=({\eta}^{N}_{k}(t,x))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z},\,t\geq 0}
the
spatial Muller’s ratchet
.

For
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, let
\boldsymbol{e}_{k}^{(x)}\in(\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}})^{L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}
denote the configuration consisting of a single particle carrying exactly
k
mutations at deme
x
. In order to define the infinitesimal generator of the Markov process
({\eta}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
, let

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{*}(\mathcal{S}^{N};\mathbb{R})

(2.5)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Bigg\{{\phi}\in\mathscr{C}(\mathcal{S}^{N};\mathbb{R}):\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z},\\
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\end{subarray}}\sup_{\boldsymbol{{\zeta}}\in\mathcal{S}^{N}}\Bigg(\Big|{\phi}\Big(\boldsymbol{{\zeta}}+\boldsymbol{e}^{(x)}_{k}\Big)-{\phi}(\boldsymbol{{\zeta}})\Big|+\mathds{1}_{\{{\zeta}_{k}(x)>0\}}\Big|{\phi}\Big(\boldsymbol{{\zeta}}-\boldsymbol{e}^{(x)}_{k}\Big)-{\phi}(\boldsymbol{{\zeta}})\Big|

\displaystyle+\mathds{1}_{\{{\zeta}_{k}(x)>0\}}\sum_{a=1}^{2}\Big|{\phi}\Big(\boldsymbol{{\zeta}}+\boldsymbol{e}^{(x+(-1)^{a}L_{N}^{-1})}_{k}-\boldsymbol{e}^{(x)}_{k}\Big)-{\phi}(\boldsymbol{{\zeta}})\Big|\Bigg)(1+|x|)^{2(1+\deg q_{-})}<\infty\Bigg\}.

The set
\mathscr{C}_{*}(\mathcal{S}^{N};\mathbb{R})
can be thought of as a set of functions whose discrete derivatives decrease polynomially fast in distance from the origin.
By
[
56
, Theorem 2.2]
, for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the infinitesimal generator
\mathcal{L}^{N}
of
({\eta}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
satisfies, for all
{\phi}\in\mathscr{C}_{*}(\mathcal{S}^{N};\mathbb{R})
,

\mathcal{L}^{N}{\phi}(\boldsymbol{{\xi}})=\frac{m_{N}}{2}\mathcal{L}^{N}_{m}{\phi}(\boldsymbol{{\xi}})+\mathcal{L}^{N}_{r}{\phi}(\boldsymbol{{\xi}})\quad\forall\boldsymbol{{\xi}}\in\mathcal{S}^{N},

(2.6)

where
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{m}
corresponds to the migration process and
\mathcal{L}^{N}_{r}
corresponds to the birth-death process and are defined as follows: for any
{\phi}\in\mathscr{C}_{*}(\mathcal{S}^{N},\mathbb{R})
and
\boldsymbol{{\xi}}=({\xi}_{k}(x))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}^{N}
,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}^{N}_{m}{\phi})(\boldsymbol{{\xi}})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sum_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\;\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\;\sum_{z\in\{-L_{N}^{-1},L_{N}^{-1}\}}{\xi}_{k}(x)\left({\phi}\Big(\boldsymbol{{\xi}}+\boldsymbol{e}_{k}^{(x+z)}-\boldsymbol{e}_{k}^{(x)}\Big)-{\phi}(\boldsymbol{{\xi}})\right),

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}^{N}_{r}{\phi})(\boldsymbol{{\xi}})

\displaystyle\quad\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sum_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Bigg(q_{+}\left(\frac{\|{\xi}(x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}}{N}\right)\Big(s_{k}(1-{\mu}){\xi}_{k}(x)+\mathds{1}_{\{k\geq 1\}}s_{k-1}{\mu}{\xi}_{k-1}(x)\Big)\left({\phi}\Big(\boldsymbol{{\xi}}+\boldsymbol{e}_{k}^{(x)}\Big)-{\phi}(\boldsymbol{{\xi}})\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+q_{-}\left(\frac{\|{\xi}(x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}}{N}\right){\xi}_{k}(x)\left({\phi}\Big(\boldsymbol{{\xi}}-\boldsymbol{e}_{k}^{(x)}\Big)-{\phi}(\boldsymbol{{\xi}})\right)\Bigg).

(2.7)

2.1
Functional law of large numbers

We now prepare to state our main result concerning convergence of the particle system to a system of PDEs. For
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we define the approximate density process
(u^{N}_{k}(t,\cdot))_{t\geq 0}
given by
u^{N}_{k}(t,\cdot):\mathbb{R}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
for
t\geq 0

by setting

u^{N}_{k}(t,x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{{\eta}^{N}_{k}(t,x)}{N}\quad\textrm{ for all }x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z},

(2.8)

and linearly interpolating
u^{N}_{k}(t,\cdot)
between the demes
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
. Since
({\eta}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is an
\mathcal{S}^{N}
-valued càdlàg process, we have that for every
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and any
t\geq 0
,
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}u^{N}_{k}(t,x)
is almost surely finite.

Our main result will describe the limiting behaviour of the system defined above as
N\rightarrow\infty
. To deal with the infinitely many types of particles in the system, we will interpret

((u^{N}_{k}(t,\cdot))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{t\geq 0}
as a Markov process taking values in the space of sequences of non-negative Radon measures
\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
.

By the definition of the state space
\mathcal{S}^{N}
in (
2.3
), the definition of
u^{N}
in (
2.8
), and the fact that
({\eta}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is an
\mathcal{S}^{N}
-valued process, we conclude that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
t\geq 0
, almost surely, for any compact set
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{R}
we have
u^{N}_{k}(t,\cdot)\in\mathscr{C}(\mathcal{K};\mathbb{R})
. Therefore, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
t\geq 0
,
(u^{N}_{k}(t,x))_{x\in\mathbb{R}}
is almost surely the density of a Radon measure with respect to the Lebesgue measure. With a slight abuse of notation, we denote this measure by
u^{N}_{k}(t)
, and for
{\phi}\in\mathscr{C}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
, we let

\left\langle u^{N}_{k}(t),{\phi}\right\rangle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\phi}(x)u^{N}_{k}(t,x)\,dx.

(2.9)

For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, since
({\eta}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is an
\mathcal{S}^{N}
-valued càdlàg process, we have
(u^{N}_{k}(t))_{t\geq 0}\in\mathscr{D}\Big([0,\infty),\Big(\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R}),d_{\textrm{vague}}\Big)\Big)
almost surely, where we recall that
d_{\textrm{vague}}
is a metric on
\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})
which induces the vague topology. Recall that
\Big(\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R}),d_{\textrm{vague}}\Big)
is a complete and separable metric space (see e.g.
[
16
, Proposition III.1.9.14]
).

The process
(u^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}=((u^{N}_{k}(t))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{t\geq 0}
can then be interpreted as a càdlàg process with sample paths in
\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, i.e. the space of sequences of non-negative Radon measures. In order to characterise the convergence of
u^{N}
in
\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
as
N\rightarrow\infty
, we introduce the metric
d
given by

\displaystyle{d}:\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}\times\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow[0,2]

(2.10)

\displaystyle(u,v)

\displaystyle\mapsto d(u,v)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-k}\Big(d_{\textrm{vague}}\left(u_{k},v_{k}\right)\wedge 1\Big).

Then
(\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}},d)
is a complete and separable metric space equipped with the product topology (see e.g. the comment before Proposition 3.4.6 in
[
29
]
). In particular, a sequence
(v^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}=((v^{n}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of elements of
\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
converges to
v=(v_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
in the topology of
(\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}},d)
if and only if
v^{n}_{k}\rightarrow v_{k}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
in
(\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R}),d_{\textrm{vague}})
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

In
[
34
]
, Foutel-Rodier and Etheridge
derived non-rigorously via a generator calculation
that for the sequence of initial conditions
(\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
given by (
2.1
),
as
N\rightarrow\infty
, the sequence of processes
(u^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
should converge to the solution
u=(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
of an infinite system of PDEs given by

\displaystyle{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}_{t}u_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{2}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}u_{k}+F_{k}(u)\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,t>0,

(2.11)

\displaystyle u(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=f(\cdot),

where
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
is the function in (
2.1
), and
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
is given by, for all
u=(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
,

F_{k}(u)=q_{+}(\|u\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})\left(s_{k}(1-{\mu})u_{k}+\mathds{1}_{\{k\geq 1\}}s_{k-1}{\mu}u_{k-1}\right)-q_{-}(\|u\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})u_{k}\quad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

(2.12)

Note that, in the system of PDEs above,
\displaystyle\frac{m}{2}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}u_{k}
corresponds to migration of particles carrying exactly
k
mutations;
q_{+}(\|u\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})s_{k}(1-{\mu})u_{k}
is the rescaled rate at which particles carrying exactly
k
mutations give birth to new particles carrying exactly
k
mutations;
q_{-}(\|u\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})u_{k}
is the rescaled rate at which particles carrying
k
mutations die; and
q_{+}(\|u\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})s_{k-1}{\mu}u_{k-1}
is the rescaled rate at which particles carrying exactly
k-1
mutations give birth to new particles carrying
k
mutations (when
k\geq 1
).
Our main result rigorously confirms the conjectured convergence to the system of PDEs.

Theorem 2.1
.

Suppose that
(m_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(L_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
q_{+}
,
q_{-}
and
f
satisfy Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
. For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, define
\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
as in (
2.1
), and let
({\eta}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
denote the càdlàg
\mathcal{S}^{N}
-valued strong Markov process with generator
\mathcal{L}^{N}
defined in (
2.6
) and (
2
) with
{\eta}^{N}(0)=\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
almost surely.
Then, as
N\rightarrow\infty
, the approximate density process
(u^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
defined in (
2.8
) and (
2.9
) converges in distribution on
\mathscr{D}\left([0,\infty),(\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}},d)\right)
with respect to the
J_{1}
-topology to a continuous-time
\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
-valued process
(u(t))_{t\geq 0}=((u_{k}(t))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{t\geq 0}
, which satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
t\geq 0
,
u_{k}(t)
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, with density denoted by
\left(u_{k}(t,x)\right)_{x\in\mathbb{R}}
.

(ii)

The family of sequences of densities
(u(t,\cdot))_{t\geq 0}=((u_{k}(t,\cdot))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{t\geq 0}
is a non-negative mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
).

(iii)

The family of sequences of densities
(u(t,\cdot))_{t\geq 0}=((u_{k}(t,\cdot))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{t\geq 0}
is such that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
(u_{k}(t,\cdot))_{t\geq 0}
is equal almost everywhere on
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
to a map
\hat{u}_{k}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
such that
\hat{u}_{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{1,2}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
.

Finally,
(u(t,\cdot))_{t\geq 0}
is the unique weak solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) satisfying conditions (i)-(iii).

We will carefully define what it means to say that
u
is a weak solution or a mild solution of (
2.11
) in Section
3
. We will refer to the weak solution
u=(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in\mathscr{D}([0,\infty),(\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}},d))
to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) satisfying conditions (i)-(iii) of Theorem
2.1
as the unique continuous mild solution to (
2.11
).

Remark 2.2
.

We note that the arguments used in the proof of Theorem
2.1
remain valid under more general assumptions. In particular, Assumption
2
(iii) is not essential for our proof of the functional law of large numbers. Furthermore, instead of requiring the per-capita birth and death rates
q_{+},q_{-}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
to be polynomials satisfying Assumption
3
, it suffices that
q_{+}
and
q_{-}
are non-negative locally Lipschitz functions such that:

(1)

\liminf_{u\to\infty}\displaystyle\frac{q_{-}(u)}{u\,q_{+}(u)+1}>0
.

(2)

\lim_{u\to\infty}q_{-}(u)=\infty
.

(3)

There exists
u^{*}\in(0,\infty)
for which
q_{-}(u^{(1)})>q_{-}(u^{(2)})
whenever
u^{(1)}>u^{(2)}>u^{*}
.

(4)

There exists a non-negative polynomial
p:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
such that
q_{-}(u)\leq p(u)
, for all
u\in[0,\infty)
.

The same arguments also yield the functional law of large numbers for the spatial Muller’s ratchet defined on the rescaled
d
-dimensional lattice
L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for any spatial dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}
.

2.2
The asymptotic behaviour of the limiting process

In
[
34
]
, Foutel-Rodier and Etheridge state (without rigorous proofs) that for the spatial Muller’s ratchet model, if the initial proportions of individuals carrying each number of mutations in a population spreading into an empty habitat were the same proportions as an equilibrium point of the system of PDEs (
2.11
) (i.e. if the population were in selection–mutation equilibrium), then the proportions of individuals carrying each number of mutations would subsequently be the same in the bulk as in the front of the range expansion. Using this hypothesis, they determine (non-rigorusly) the spreading speed of the population into an empty habitat.

Our next main results establish rigorous versions of these conjectures under certain conditions on the reaction term (
2.12
) of the system of PDEs (
2.11
). To introduce these conditions, we first recall the terminology usually used to describe one-dimensional reaction-diffusion equations. Consider the PDE

{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}_{t}U=\frac{m}{2}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}U+g(U)\quad\textrm{for }t>0,

(2.13)

where
g\in\mathscr{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
,
g(0)=g(1)=0
and
\int_{0}^{1}g(U)\,dU>0
. We say that the reaction term
g
is
monostable
if
g^{\prime}(0)>0
,
g^{\prime}(1)<0
and
g(x)>0
for all
x\in(0,1)
(see e.g.
[
35
]
). We say that the reaction term
g
is of
Fisher-KPP type
if
g
is monostable and
g(U)\leq g^{\prime}(0)U
for all
U\in(0,1)
. From a population dynamics point of view, the Fisher-KPP condition corresponds to the setting in which competition dominates over cooperation. In our next definition, we adapt these concepts to the system of PDEs (
2.11
).

Definition 2.3

(Monostable and Fisher-KPP reaction terms)
.

We say that the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
defined in (
2.12
) is
monostable
if the sequence of fitness parameters
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
satisfies Assumption
2
and is strictly decreasing, the mutation rate satisfies
{\mu}\in(0,1)
, and the functions
q_{+},q_{-}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
satisfy Assumption
3
and the following conditions:

(i)

q_{+}(U)\geq q_{-}(U)\quad\forall\,U\in[0,1]
.

(ii)

q_{+}(U)>0\;\forall\,U\in[0,1]
.

(iii)

q_{+}(1)=q_{-}(1)
.

(iv)

q^{\prime}_{+}(1)<q^{\prime}_{-}(1)
.

(v)

(1-{\mu})q_{+}(0)-q_{-}(0)>0
.

Finally, if additionally to the conditions above, we also have

(1-{\mu})q_{+}(U)-q_{-}(U)\leq(1-{\mu})q_{+}(0)-q_{-}(0)\quad\forall\,U\in[0,1],

(2.14)

then we say that the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is of
Fisher-KPP type
.

Note that if the reaction term
g(U)=U(q_{+}(U)-q_{-}(U))
of the one-dimensional PDE

{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}_{t}U=\frac{m}{2}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}U+U(q_{+}(U)-q_{-}(U))

(2.15)

is monostable, then conditions (i), (iii) and (iv) of Definition
2.3
are automatically satisfied. Moreover, if the reaction term of (
2.15
) is monostable, then
q_{+}(0)>q_{-}(0)
, and therefore condition (v) of Definition
2.3
must hold for sufficiently small
{\mu}
. We also must have
q_{+}(U)>0
for all
U\in(0,1)
, but the additional condition
q_{+}(1)>0
in condition (ii) is not implied by the monostability of the reaction term of (
2.15
); this is a technical assumption required in our proofs. This condition, however, is not restrictive, since it is reasonable to assume in biological models that the per-capita reproduction and death rates are both strictly positive in high population density.

In order to determine the evolution of the ratios between the proportions of particles carrying a particular number of mutations, we will need some control on these ratios in the initial condition. This will be our next assumption. Recall that we denote the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
by
{\lambda}
.

Assumption 5

(Control on the initial prevalence of mutations)
.

Let
f=\left(f_{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
be a function satisfying Assumption
4
, and the following additional conditions:

(i)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\esssup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\,\|f(x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\leq 1
.

(ii)

{\lambda}(\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\,f_{0}(x)>0\})>0
.

(iii)

There exists a sequence
\left(\hat{{\pi}}_{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
such that for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
{\lambda}
-almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

0\leq f_{k}(x)\leq\hat{{\pi}}_{k}f_{0}(x).

For
{\mu}\in(0,1)
, let
({\alpha}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}=\Big({\alpha}_{k}({\mu},(s_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})\Big)_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be given by
{\alpha}_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=1
and

{\alpha}_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{{\mu}s_{i-1}}{(1-{\mu})(1-s_{i})}\quad\;\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.16)

Since, by Assumption
2
,
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}s_{k}=0
, we have that
({\alpha}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
. Moreover, under the assumption that
s_{k}>0
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, which is required in Definition
2.3
for the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
defined in (
2.12
) to be monostable, we also have
{\alpha}_{k}>0
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Remark 2.4
.

We claim that, at least heuristically, it is straightforward to see that the sequence
({\alpha}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
defined in (
2.16
) gives the proportions of the population carrying different numbers of mutations at equilibrium. Indeed, suppose that the polynomials
q_{+}
and
q_{-}
satisfy Assumption
3
and are strictly positive on
(0,\infty)
. Let
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be the reaction term defined in (
2.12
), and let
u^{(\mathrm{eq})}=(u^{(\mathrm{eq})}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
be a stationary solution of (
2.11
) such that
u^{(\mathrm{eq})}_{0}\neq 0
and
F(u^{(\mathrm{eq})})=0
.
From the definition of
F_{0}
in (
2.12
), we obtain

q_{-}(\|u^{(\mathrm{eq})}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})=(1-{\mu})q_{+}(\|u^{(\mathrm{eq})}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}).

Using this identity together with the definition of the reaction term
F
, (
2.16
) and the fact that we assume
q_{+}
and
q_{-}
to be strictly positive on
(0,\infty)
, it follows that
u^{(\mathrm{eq})}_{k}={\alpha}_{k}u^{(\mathrm{eq})}_{0}\;\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Thus, up to normalisation,
({\alpha}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
gives the equilibrium distribution of the different numbers of mutations.

Let

\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\displaystyle\min_{U\in[0,1]}q_{+}(U)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\displaystyle\max_{U\in[0,1]}q_{+}(U).

(2.17)

By Definition
2.3
(ii), if the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is monostable, then
\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}\geq\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}>0
. We can now state our result determining the prevalence of mutations during a range expansion.

Theorem 2.5
.

Suppose that
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
q_{+}
and
q_{-}
satisfy Assumptions
2
and
3
, that
{\mu}\in(0,1)
, that
m>0
, that the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
defined in (
2.12
) is monostable in the sense of Definition
2.3
, and that
f
satisfies Assumption
5
. Let
u=(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be the unique continuous mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
). Define
({\alpha}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
as in (
2.16
), and
\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}
,
\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}
as in (
2.17
). Then there exist functions
\underline{{\pi}}=\left(\underline{{\pi}}_{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
and
\overline{{\pi}}=\left(\overline{{\pi}}_{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
such that the following limits hold in
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
:

\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\underline{{\pi}}(T)=\Bigg({\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}\right)^{k}\Bigg)_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\overline{{\pi}}(T)=\Bigg({\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}\right)^{k}\Bigg)_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}},

and such that
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
T>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\underline{{\pi}_{k}}(T)u_{0}(T,x)\leq u_{k}(T,x)\leq\overline{{\pi}_{k}}(T)u_{0}(T,x).

(2.18)

Remark 2.6
.

(i)

One can always take
\underline{{\pi}}_{0}(T)=\overline{{\pi}}_{0}(T)=1
for all
T\geq 0
, and so (
2.18
) holds trivially for
k=0
.

(ii)

If
\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}=\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}
, i.e. if
q_{+}
is a constant function, Theorem
2.5
shows that in the PDE limit, the ratio of particles carrying exactly
k
mutations to particles without mutations converges uniformly (in space) as
T\rightarrow\infty
to
{\alpha}_{k}
, where
{\alpha}_{k}
is given by (
2.16
). This is the case for the system corresponding to the classical Fisher-KPP equation, with
q_{+}(U)=1
and
q_{-}(U)=U
for all
U\in[0,1]
.

We highlight again that by combining the definition of
{\alpha}_{k}
in (
2.16
), the fact that
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}s_{k}=0
by Assumption
2
, and the fact that Definition
2.3
(ii) implies that
0<\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}\leq\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}
, we see that both sequences

\left({\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}\right)^{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\left({\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}\right)^{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}

are elements of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
. Phenomenologically, Theorem
2.5
implies that in the PDE limit, the ratio between the local population density with
k
mutations and the local population density with
0
mutations can be uniformly bounded in some compact interval contained in
(0,\infty)
at large times. Although this result is not the same as the conjecture from
[
34
]
described at the start of this subsection, it indicates that the proportion of the population carrying a certain number of mutations is not badly behaved during a range expansion. It also implies that, in the PDE limit, if the population without mutations spreads into an empty habitat with some speed, then the population with mutations also spreads into the empty habitat with the same speed. Using these consequences of Theorem
2.5
, we can determine the spreading speed for the PDE limit in the case when the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is of Fisher-KPP type.

Theorem 2.7
.

Suppose that
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
q_{+}
and
q_{-}
satisfy Assumptions
2
and
3
, that
{\mu}\in(0,1)
, that
m>0
, that the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
defined in (
2.12
) is of Fisher-KPP type in the sense of Definition
2.3
, and that
f
satisfies Assumption
5
. Further, assume that there exists
R>0
such that the support of the initial condition satisfies
\supp f\subset(-\infty,R]
. Let

c^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sqrt{2m\Big((1-{\mu})q_{+}(0)-q_{-}(0)\Big)},

and let
u=(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
denote the unique continuous mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) with initial condition
f
. Then

\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\,\sup_{x\geq c^{*}T}\|u(T,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}=0.

Moreover, there exists a sequence
({\nu}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
with
{\nu}_{k}>0
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that for any
{\varepsilon}\in(0,c^{*})
,

\liminf_{T\rightarrow\infty}\,\inf_{x\in[0,(c^{*}-{\varepsilon})T]}\,u_{k}(T,x)\geq{\nu}_{k}\quad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

We will apply this result to some biologically relevant examples in Section
2.3
below. The following result about the long-term behaviour of the spatial Muller’s ratchet
{\eta}^{N}
for large
N
is a consequence of Theorems
2.1
and
2.5
.

Theorem 2.8
.

Suppose that
(m_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(L_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
q_{+}
,
q_{-}
and
f
satisfy Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
and
5
,and that the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
defined in (
2.12
) is monostable in the sense of Definition
2.3
. For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, define
\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
as in (
2.1
), and let
({\eta}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
denote the càdlàg
\mathcal{S}^{N}
-valued strong Markov process with generator
\mathcal{L}^{N}
defined in (
2.6
) and (
2
) with
{\eta}^{N}(0)=\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
almost surely. Let
(u^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}=((u^{N}_{k}(t))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{t\geq 0}
denote the approximate density process defined in (
2.8
) and (
2.9
). Then for any compact interval
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathbb{R}
, any
K\in\mathbb{N}
and any
{\delta},{\varepsilon}>0
, there exists
T_{{\delta},K}>0
such that for any
T\geq T_{{\delta},K}
, there exists
N_{{\delta},{\varepsilon},\mathcal{I},K,T}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for
N\geq N_{{\delta},{\varepsilon},\mathcal{I},K,T}
and
k\leq K
,

\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\Bigg({\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}\right)^{k}-{\delta}\Bigg)u^{N}_{0}(T)(\mathcal{I})\leq u^{N}_{k}(T)(\mathcal{I})\leq\Bigg({\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}\right)^{k}+{\delta}\Bigg)u^{N}_{0}(T)(\mathcal{I})\Bigg)\geq 1-{\varepsilon},

where the sequence
({\alpha}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is given by (
2.16
), and
\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}
and
\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}
are given by (
2.17
).

2.3
Application to different settings of population dynamics

In this subsection, we show how our results apply to different models of population growth. Since our definition of the spatial Muller’s ratchet
{\eta}^{N}
is rather general, it can be used to model asexual populations under different biological assumptions. For instance, taking

q_{+}(U)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=r(BU+1)\quad\textrm{ and }\quad q_{-}(U)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=r(BU+1)U\quad\forall U\geq 0,\textrm{ for some }r>0\textrm{ and }B\geq 0,

(2.19)

we recover the same birth and death rates as in the model introduced by Foutel-Rodier and Etheridge in
[
34
]
(recall that we described this model in Section
1.1
). Phenomenologically,
r
indicates the Malthusian growth factor, and
B
reflects the dependence of the growth rate on the local population density. Note that the value of the population density
U
at which the maximum of the function

[0,\infty)\ni U\mapsto q_{+}(U)-q_{-}(U)=r(1-U)(BU+1)

is attained depends on the parameter
B
. If
0\leq B\leq 1
, then the function attains its maximum when
U=0
, i.e. the maximum per-capita growth rate is attained at
U=0
. We say that in this case the population dynamics (neglecting, for now, the effect of deleterious mutations) satisfies a Fisher-KPP condition
[
35
]
. Biologically, this means that competition prevails over cooperation in determining the population dynamics.

For
B>1
, the function
[0,\infty)\ni U\mapsto q_{+}(U)-q_{-}(U)
attains its maximum at
U=\frac{B-1}{2B}>0
. In this case, we say the population (neglecting the effect of deleterious mutations) exhibits a weak Allee effect, since the maximum per-capita growth rate is attained at an intermediate population density rather than at low population density
[
34
]
. From a biological point of view, this scenario corresponds to the case where both competition and cooperation affect the population dynamics.

Our Theorem
2.1
shows that for
q_{+}
and
q_{-}
given by (
2.19
), under Assumptions
1
and
2
on
(m_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(L_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
and suitable assumptions on the initial condition,
the spatial Muller’s ratchet
{\eta}^{N}
converges as
N\rightarrow\infty
to the solution of the corresponding system of PDEs (
2.11
) (which is the same as (
1.1
) in the special case
s_{k}=(1-s)^{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
). Since the rates
q_{+}
and
q_{-}
satisfy Definition
2.3
(i)-(iv) for any
B\geq 0
,
r>0
and
{\mu}\in(0,1)
, we have from Theorem
2.5
quantitative bounds on the proportions of the population carrying
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
mutations in the solution of the system of PDEs (
2.11
) at large times, for suitable initial conditions. The assumptions for Theorem
2.7
, which
determines the spreading speed, require that the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
defined in (
2.12
) is of Fisher-KPP type in the sense of Definition
2.3
, i.e. that for all
U\in[0,1]
,

(1-{\mu})q_{+}(U)-q_{-}(U)=r(1-{\mu}-U)(BU+1)\leq(1-{\mu})q_{+}(0)-q_{-}(0)=r(1-{\mu}).

(2.20)

Inequality (
2.20
) holds for all
U\in[0,1]
if and only if
0\leq B\leq\frac{1}{1-{\mu}}
. We note that for
B\in\left(1,\frac{1}{1-{\mu}}\right]
, the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is of Fisher-KPP type but, as noted above, the population dynamics, neglecting the effect of mutations, exhibits a weak Allee effect.

If
0\leq B\leq\frac{1}{1-{\mu}}
, then by Theorem
2.7
, the spreading speed of a population evolving according to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) into an empty habitat, under suitable assumptions on the initial condition, is given by

c^{*}=\sqrt{2mr(1-{\mu})}.

This confirms the calculations in
[
34
]
for
B\leq\frac{1}{1-{\mu}}
(recall (
1.2
)), with a more general choice of fitness parameters
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
and mutation rate
{\mu}
than considered in
[
34
]
. Theorem
2.7
also implies that under the same assumptions,
c^{*}
is the spreading speed of the fraction of the population carrying
k
mutations, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
(see Figure
1
).

Figure 1:
Numerical simulation of the system of PDEs (
2.11
) with Fisher–KPP type dynamics with parameters
m=3
,
q_{+}(U)=1
and
q_{-}(U)=U
for all
U\geq 0
,
{\mu}=0.025
, and
s_{k}=0.95^{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. The horizontal axis indicates spatial position
x
, and the vertical axis shows population density. The time the snapshot is taken is denoted by
T
. Colours indicate the density of population carrying different numbers of mutations, i.e.
u_{k}(T,\cdot)
for
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,4\}
as shown in the legend of the
T=0
figure. The simulation illustrates that the fractions of the population carrying positive numbers of mutations invade previously uncolonised habitat at the same speed as the fraction carrying no mutations. We emphasise that, as will be shown in Theorem
2.9
, this phenomenon occurs due to a mutation–selection equilibrium rather than gene surfing of deleterious mutations.

Another interesting example is to take

q_{+}(U)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=U(B+1)\quad\textrm{and}\quad q_{-}(U)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=U^{2}+B\quad\forall\,U\geq 0,\textrm{ for some }B\in\left(0,1/2\right).

Then the function
U\mapsto q_{+}(U)-q_{-}(U)=(1-U)(U-B)
is negative for
U\in[0,B)
. We say that the population (neglecting the effect of deleterious mutations) exhibits a strong Allee effect
[
35
]
. Biologically, this means that cooperation is fundamental for population growth,
as the population shrinks at low population densities. In this case, our Theorem
2.1
again shows the convergence of the spatial Muller’s ratchet to the solution of the corresponding system of PDEs (
2.11
). However, the asymptotic behaviour of solutions of (
2.11
) in this case is not determined by Theorems
2.5
and
2.7
, because the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
given by (
2.12
) is not monostable in the sense of Definition
2.3
. Determining the asymptotic behaviour in this case is an interesting open problem.

We can also consider the effect of different choices of fitness parameters
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
. We first consider the model introduced in
[
34
]
, where the sequence of fitness parameters is given by
s_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(1-s)^{k}

\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, for some fixed
s\in(0,1)
. In this case, when a new mutation is acquired, the fitness of a chromosome always decreases by a factor
(1-s)
. In other words, the effect of each mutation is independent of the genetic background. Biologically, we say there is no
epistasis
. As explained in Section
1.1
, in the case
s,{\mu}\ll 1
, i.e. under a weak selection–low mutation regime, Foutel-Rodier and Etheridge derive non-rigorously that the stationary solution of the system of PDEs (
1.1
) can be approximated by (
1.3
). To formalise the weak selection–low mutation regime, let
(s^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},({\mu}^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(0,\infty)
and
s,{\mu}\in(0,\infty)
be such that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}ns^{(n)}=s\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}n{\mu}^{(n)}={\mu}.

(2.21)

Then, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, let
s^{(n)}_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(1-s^{(n)})^{k}
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large that
s^{(n)},{\mu}^{(n)}\in(0,1)
, we can let
s_{k}=s^{(n)}_{k}\;\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
{\mu}={\mu}^{(n)}
determine the sequence of fitness parameters and the mutation probability for the spatial Muller’s ratchet and the system of PDEs (
2.11
). In this case, the (rescaled) stationary solution of (
2.11
) defined in (
2.16
) is given by
{\alpha}^{(n)}=({\alpha}^{(n)}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, where
{\alpha}^{(n)}_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=1
and

{\alpha}^{(n)}_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left(\frac{{\mu}^{(n)}}{1-{\mu}^{(n)}}\right)^{k}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{(1-s^{(n)})^{i-1}}{(1-(1-s^{(n)})^{i})}\quad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.22)

If the polynomials
q_{+}
and
q_{-}
satisfy Assumption
3
and Definition
2.3
(i)-(v), then Theorem
2.5
shows for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large, under suitable assumptions on the initial condition, for a population evolving according to the system of PDEs (
2.11
), at large times, the ratio of population density carrying
k
mutations to the population density carrying
0
mutations is uniformly bounded between quantities close to
{\alpha}^{(n)}_{k}(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}})^{k}
and
{\alpha}^{(n)}_{k}(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}})^{k}
. In particular, as noted in Remark
2.6
(ii), in the special case where
q_{+}
is constant, the ratio converges uniformly to
{\alpha}^{(n)}_{k}
. We will prove in Lemma
0.A.13
in the appendix that
({\alpha}^{(n)}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
converges as
n\rightarrow\infty

in
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
to
(\hat{{\alpha}}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
given by

\hat{{\alpha}}_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{({\mu}/s)^{k}}{k!}\quad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

(2.23)

In particular, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\frac{{\alpha}^{(n)}_{k}}{\|{\alpha}^{(n)}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}}=\exp\left(-\frac{{\mu}}{s}\right)\frac{({\mu}/s)^{k}}{k!},

(2.24)

which agrees with the right-hand side of (
1.3
). In other words, roughly speaking, in the weak selection–low mutation regime, our Theorem
2.5
shows that the approximate stationary solution given in
[
34
]
governs the proportions of the population carrying different numbers of mutations at large times in solutions of (
2.11
).

Our Assumption
2
also allows for other choices of fitness parameters. For instance, one could take
s_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{k+1}\;\forall k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. In this case, the addition of another mutation to a genotype with
k
mutations leads to a reduction of fitness by a factor
s_{k+1}/s_{k}=k/(k+1)
, which depends on the previous number of mutations
k
. In biological terms, we say that the model allows some degree of epistasis, which can be important in some biological applications
[
37
]
.

2.4
Tracer dynamics and gene surfing

Our final result answers the question that motivated this article: can deleterious mutations surf population waves? Although we are not yet able to approach this question in the stochastic setting, we can answer it when we restrict ourselves to deterministic waves, i.e. when considering the limiting system of PDEs (
2.11
). We will use the idea of
tracer dynamics
introduced by Hallateschek and Nelson in
[
39
]
and formalised by Garnier and co-authors in
[
35
]
in the deterministic setting.

Roughly speaking, in the particle system setting, the idea of tracer dynamics is to label some subset of particles at time
0
. The label does not affect the mutation probability or the migration, reproduction and death rates. When a labelled particle reproduces, its offspring particle is also a labelled particle. The aim is to track the number of labelled particles at each deme over time, and use this to infer information about the typical history of particles in the system.
In order to translate this idea to the context of the system of PDEs (
2.11
), we take
f^{*}=(f^{*}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
, and we think of
f^{*}_{k}
as indicating the initial population density that carries exactly
k
mutations and is labelled. As usual, we will take
f=(f_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
and think of
f_{k}
as indicating the initial population density that carries exactly
k
mutations. We make the following assumptions about
f^{*}
.

Assumption 6

(Tracer dynamics initial condition)
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
satisfy Assumptions
4
and
5
. Let
f^{*}=(f^{*}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
satisfy the following conditions:

(i)

f^{*}\in L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
, i.e.
\esssup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\|f^{*}(x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}<\infty
.

(ii)

f^{*}
is continuous almost everywhere, i.e. there exists
\mathcal{N}\subset\mathbb{R}
such that
{\lambda}(\mathcal{N})=0
and
f^{*}
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathcal{N}
.

(iii)

f_{k}^{*}(x)\leq f_{k}(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Note that condition (iii) guarantees that the initially labelled population is a subset of the total initial population. For
t\geq 0
,
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we indicate the density of the labelled population carrying exactly
k
mutations at position
x
at time
t
by
u^{*}_{k}(t,x)
. Suppose
f
and
f^{*}
satisfy Assumption
6
. Then we can see from the definition of the labelled set in the particle system setting that
u^{*}=(u^{*}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
should satisfy the system of PDEs

\displaystyle{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}_{t}u^{*}_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{2}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}u^{*}_{k}+F^{*}_{k}(u,u^{*})\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,t\geq 0,

(2.25)

\displaystyle u^{*}(0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=f^{*}(\cdot),

where
u:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
is the unique continuous mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) satisfying conditions (i)-(iii) of Theorem
2.1
, and the reaction term
F^{*}=(F^{*}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}\times{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
is given by, for all
u=(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
and
u^{*}=(u^{*}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
,

F^{*}_{k}(u,u^{*})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=q_{+}(\|u\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})\left(s_{k}(1-{\mu})u^{*}_{k}+\mathds{1}_{\{k\geq 1\}}s_{k-1}{\mu}u^{*}_{k-1}\right)-q_{-}(\|u\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})u^{*}_{k}\quad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

(2.26)

The definition of
F^{*}=(F^{*}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
reflects the fact that in the particle system with tracer dynamics, the per-capita birth and death rates of labelled particles are determined by the local density of the total population, but only labelled particles can give birth to other labelled particles.
In order to study gene surfing of deleterious mutations, we label all of the initial population that carries at least one mutation, and determine the behaviour of
u^{*}(T,\cdot)
as
T\rightarrow\infty
.

Theorem 2.9
.

Suppose that
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
q_{+}
and
q_{-}
satisfy Assumptions
2
and
3
, that
{\mu}\in(0,1)
and
m>0
, and that
f
and
f^{*}
satisfy Assumption
6
, and that the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
defined in (
2.12
) is monostable in the sense of Definition
2.3
. Then there exists a unique mild solution
u^{*}=(u^{*}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
to the system of PDEs (
2.25
) satisfying the following conditions:

(i)

u^{*}\in\mathscr{C}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
.

(ii)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|u^{*}(t,\cdot)\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}<\infty
for all
T>0
.

(iii)

u^{*}_{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{1,2}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Moreover, if
f^{*}_{0}(\cdot)\equiv 0
, then

\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\|u^{*}(T,\cdot)\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}=0.

We will recall the definition of continuous mild solutions to the system of PDEs (
2.25
) in Section
8.4
, and the definition of the norm
\|\cdot\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}
in Section
3.2
. Theorem
2.9
complements the description of the asymptotic behaviour of the solution of (
2.11
) given by Theorem
2.5
(in the monostable case). It shows that for a population expanding according to (
2.11
), although (by Theorem
2.5
) there is a positive fraction of the population carrying deleterious mutations in both the front and the bulk of the expanding population, this fraction descends from the expanding subpopulation without mutations and is not the result of surfing of previously existing deleterious mutations; see Figure
2
for a simulation of this phenomenon. Therefore, we can say that deleterious mutations cannot surf deterministic population waves.

Figure 2:
Numerical simulation of a population with Fisher–KPP type dynamics, governed by the systems of PDEs (
2.11
) and (
2.25
), using the same parameters as in Figure
1
. The horizontal axis indicates spatial position
x
, and the vertical axis shows population density. The time the snapshot is taken is denoted by
T
. Colours indicate the density of population carrying different numbers of mutations, i.e.
u_{k}(T,\cdot)
for
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,4\}
as shown in the legend of the
T=0
figure. Shaded regions indicate the labelled fraction of the population. At time
T=0
, only the subpopulation without mutations is unlabelled. As shown in Theorem
2.9
, the labelled population, descended from the initial mutant subpopulation, does not propagate with the expansion wave.

2.5
Related work

The study of the convergence of interacting particle systems to reaction–diffusion partial differential equations has been a very active area of research in probability theory since at least the 1980s. For spatial birth–death processes, there are several approaches to proving convergence to a hydrodynamic limit, depending on the assumptions on the initial condition, the spatial domain, and the dynamics of the discrete model. In this subsection, we briefly review some of this literature and explain why the methods developed previously cannot be directly applied to the proof of Theorem
2.1
.

Roughly speaking, the literature on hydrodynamic scaling limits of spatial birth–death processes can be divided into five main categories:

(A)

Works that analyse models in which there is an
a priori
bound on the local number of particles, so that in the limit the population density is always uniformly bounded (see for instance
[
24
,
60
,
25
]
).

(B)

Works that assume the spatial domain of the process is compact, e.g. the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=[0,1]^{d}
(see for instance
[
4
,
3
,
50
,
51
,
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
31
]
).

(C)

Works that apply the method of correlation functions to derive the limit
[
15
,
21
,
65
,
71
]
.

(D)

Works that use the relative entropy method
[
59
,
65
,
45
]
.

(E)

Works that analyse models with finitely many particles in the whole domain and bounded reproduction rates, so that there are bounds on the moments of the total mass
[
27
,
32
]
.

To the best of our knowledge, there are no existing works that address spatial birth–death processes with infinitely many particle types, unbounded reproduction rates, and an unbounded number of particles per site simultaneously. Consequently, the techniques developed in the works mentioned above cannot be applied to our model without significant adaptation.

Works in category (A) typically construct the birth–death interacting particle system using a countable collection of Poisson processes. Since, in these works, the number of particles per deme is bounded by some integer
N
, the approximate population density is uniformly bounded, see e.g.
[
24
,
60
,
25
]
. This uniform bound means that convergence can be studied in the space of continuous functions, which is strictly stronger than convergence in the space of Radon measures. Since no uniform density bound holds in our setting, these techniques cannot be applied directly.

To handle the absence of uniform bounds, Arnold and Theodosopulu
[
4
,
3
]
, Blount
[
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
]
, and Kotelenez
[
50
,
51
]
developed techniques in the 1980s and 1990s that apply to spatial birth–death processes on the one-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}=[0,1]
. These works (category (B) in the list above) exploit the construction of an explicit orthonormal basis of the Hilbert space
L_{2}([0,1])
to analyse weak convergence. Importantly, their techniques rely on properties of martingales taking values in Hilbert spaces, that are not shared by martingales taking values in general Banach spaces. Since our model involves infinitely many particle types, the solution of the limiting system of PDEs (
2.11
) is most naturally understood as an
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
-valued function on
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
. Consequently, it is not natural to study convergence in Hilbert spaces, and these techniques do not transfer directly to our setting.

In category (C), Boldrighini et al. were the first to rigorously analyse a spatial birth–death process with polynomial birth and death rates on the whole real line
[
15
,
21
]
. They consider a single-type particle system on the lattice
{\varepsilon}\mathbb{Z}
, where
{\varepsilon}>0
is a spatial scaling parameter, and study its behaviour as
{\varepsilon}\to 0
. Importantly, their setting does not involve scaling the initial number of particles per deme by
1/{\varepsilon}
to produce a mean-field limit. Instead, they use correlation functions, a form of duality function, to bound local moments and show that the limit satisfies a so-called BBGKY hierarchy consistent with the target PDE. More recently, Tendron also used the BBGKY hierarchy to study the scaling limit of branching processes with local competition
[
71
]
.

The BBGKY hierarchy becomes intractable for systems with infinitely many particle types (see
[
15
, Section 4]
), which means this method is not feasible for our purposes. Nonetheless, we adapt some ideas from
[
15
,
21
]
, in particular the use of correlation functions, to obtain the estimates in our companion article
[
56
, Theorem 2.3]
that are used in the proof of the hydrodynamic limit result in this article.

The relative entropy method, used by Mourragui
[
59
]
and others
[
65
,
45
]
(category (D)), requires the solution of the limiting PDE to be uniformly bounded away from zero. In our setting, the limiting population density
u(t,x)
takes values in
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
, so for any
t\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
we have
\lim_{k\to\infty}u_{k}(t,x)=0
. It is therefore not clear how to apply the relative entropy method to our model.

In a recent article closely related to our work, Etheridge et al.
[
27
]
study a spatial birth–death process on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with non-local interactions. The authors prove weak convergence of the process under different scaling regimes, either retaining the non-local interaction term in the limit or not. A notable advantage of the approach in
[
27
]
is that it does not require
a priori
bounds on the density of particles per unit region. By assuming the reproduction rate is uniformly bounded and that the initial configuration contains only finitely many particles, the authors derive precise moment estimates for the total mass of the process, which they use to establish tightness of the sequence of interacting particle systems and to characterise the limit. Moreover, to prove uniqueness of the limiting PDE in the space of measures, they apply
[
53
, Theorem 3.5]
, which requires the reproduction and death rates to be uniformly bounded and globally Lipschitz continuous.

In a similar vein, Flandoli and Huang
[
32
]
analyse the scaling limit of Brownian particles in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with a uniformly bounded reproduction rate, proving convergence to the Fisher–KPP equation without non-local interaction. Their technique also relies on the uniform bound on the reproduction rate and on having a finite initial number of particles. In contrast, in our setting, in general the reproduction rate is not uniformly bounded, and neither the reproduction nor death rates are globally Lipschitz continuous. We also allow for infinitely many particles at time
0
. Consequently, we cannot rely on these methods to prove convergence of the spatial Muller’s ratchet to the solution of the system of PDEs (
2.11
) without substantial adaptations.

We would also like to highlight some previous work on the hydrodynamic limit of coagulation-fragmentation processes
[
54
,
40
,
41
]
. Although in these works the authors study particle systems with infinitely many types, there is no creation of mass, since only coagulation, fragmentation and diffusion events are allowed. Hence, the total mass is always uniformly bounded, and
L_{\infty}
bounds can be obtained on the population densities. This is not possible in our setting, which makes our proof significantly different.

Finally, there is also previous work on the scaling limits of interacting particle systems with infinitely many types of particles but without spatial dependence
[
8
,
7
,
9
,
68
,
73
]
. These systems arise naturally from questions involving graph dynamics and epidemic models. In this class of systems, it is often possible to describe the deterministic scaling limit in terms of ordinary differential equations taking values in the space of sequences
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
. The fact that we analyse a population with spatial structure, however, makes our approach very different.

2.6
Overview of the proofs

Before introducing the main ideas behind the proofs, we highlight the principal challenges that must be overcome in the analysis of the spatial Muller’s ratchet model. First, the lack of
a priori
bounds on the local number of particles — together with unbounded birth and death rates — prevent us from establishing uniform estimates in space; in particular, we are not able to bound
\mathbb{E}\Big[\|u^{N}(t,\cdot)\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\Big]
. Second, letting the ‘type’ of a particle denote the number of mutations that it carries, having infinitely many types of particles requires us to embed the stochastic process in non-standard Polish spaces to study its scaling limit. Finally, the dynamics is non-local in type space, since particles of different types in the same deme interact with each other (in the sense that birth and death rates depend on the total number of particles in the deme).

Outline of proof of Theorem
2.1
.

The proof of Theorem
2.1
can be roughly divided into two steps: the proof of tightness of the sequence of processes
(u^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathscr{D}([0,\infty),(\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}},d))
, and the characterisation and proof of uniqueness of the limiting process.

An important ingredient comes from our companion article
[
56
, Theorem 2.3]
, where we
establish the following estimate:
for every
p\geq 1
and
T>0
, and
f=(f_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
satisfying Assumption
4
, defining
\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
as in (
2.1
) and letting
{\eta}^{N}(0)=\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\;\sup_{t\leq T}\;\sup_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{E}\left[\|u^{N}(t,x)\|^{p}_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\right]\lesssim_{p,T}\sup_{x\in L^{-1}_{N}\mathbb{Z}}\,\|f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}^{p}+1.

(2.27)

The estimate relies on the fact that by Assumption
2
,
s_{k}\leq 1

\forall k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and that by Assumption
3
, the birth and death polynomials are such that
0\leq\deg q_{+}<\deg q_{-}
. The proof in
[
56
]
uses the method of correlation functions introduced in
[
15
,
21
]
.
For ease of reference, we will state this result precisely in Theorem
3.3
below. Estimate (
2.27
) is a ‘local’ estimate in the sense that the supremum over space is outside the expectation; as mentioned at the start of this subsection, we are not able to bound
\mathbb{E}\Big[\|u^{N}(t,\cdot)\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\Big]
.

To establish tightness of
(u^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
, and to characterise any subsequential limit, we will need to establish more regularity properties of the sequence
(u^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
. This is carried out in Section
4
, where we will apply a Green’s function representation of the migration semigroup to write the population density in each deme in terms of a martingale. This technique is widely used for establishing scaling limits of spatial birth-death processes with an almost surely bounded number of particles per site
[
25
,
60
]
. More precisely, we will establish in Lemma
4.3
that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
T\geq 0
, there exists a càdlàg martingale
\Big(M^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(t)\Big)_{t\in[0,T]}
with
M^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(0)=0
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}u^{N}_{k}(T,x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{P}_{0}(X^{N}(T)=y-x)\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}u^{N}_{k}(0,y)+M^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(T)

(2.28)
