\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{P}_{0}(X^{N}(T-t)=y-x)\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}F_{k}(u^{N}(t-,y))\,dt,

where the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is given by (
2.12
), and
(X^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a simple symmetric random walk on
L^{-1}_{N}\mathbb{Z}
with total jump rate
m_{N}
, and
\mathbb{P}_{0}
is the probability measure under which
X^{N}(0)=0
. Combining (
2.28
), an expression for
\left\langle M^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}\right\rangle
and classical martingale estimates will allow us to derive weak equicontinuity properties for
(u^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
in Lemma
4.4
. Moreover, by combining (
2.28
) with the fact that by Assumption
2
,
s_{k}\rightarrow 0
as
k\rightarrow\infty
, we will be able to uniformly bound the expectation of the sum over large
k
of
u^{N}_{k}(t,x)
in
Lemma
4.7
. These properties, together with (
2.27
) and standard stochastic analysis arguments, will be used in our proof of tightness in Section
5
.

A major challenge in the proof of Theorem
2.1
is that, since we embed our process in the space of
\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
-valued càdlàg functions, tightness alone does not imply that any subsequential limit has sample paths lying in the subset of
\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
given by sequences of measures that are absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure, nor does it guarantee that the birth and death rate polynomials of the local population density converge in the space of measures. Moreover, even after establishing the existence of a sequence of densities for the limiting measure-valued process, we note that, because tightness is proved in the space of
\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
-valued càdlàg functions, proving uniqueness of the limit requires us to establish uniqueness of solutions to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) in
\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
. This is highly non-trivial due to the non-linear reaction term in (
2.12
) that is neither uniformly bounded nor globally Lipschitz continuous, and because, as explained at the start of this subsection, we do not have any guarantee that the limiting solution remains uniformly bounded in space over finite time intervals (see
[
18
,
53
,
20
]
and the references therein for a discussion of the challenges in establishing uniqueness of measure-valued solutions to non-linear PDEs).

To overcome these difficulties, we will also show tightness of
(u^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
in the functional space
L_{4\deg q_{-}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
, where for
r\geq 1
,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle L}_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Bigg\{v:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}:\;\|v\|_{{L}_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\|v(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}^{r}}{1+|x|^{2}}\,dx\,dt\Bigg)^{1/r}<\infty\Bigg\},

where
\hat{{\lambda}}
is a measure on
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
which is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure
{\lambda}
, and given by

\hat{{\lambda}}(dt\;dx)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{\mathds{1}_{\{t\in[0,T]\}}}{1+|x|^{2}}{\lambda}(dt\;dx).

We refer the reader to Section
3.2
for a brief overview of a rigorous definition of measurable
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
-valued functions on
\mathbb{R}
. The advantage of working on
{L}_{4\deg q_{-}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
instead of
\mathscr{D}\Big([0,\infty),\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}\Big)
is that, as in the usual
L_{r}
spaces, convergence in
{L}_{4\deg q_{-}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
implies convergence of the densities almost everywhere on the time-space box
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
. This idea generalises methods that rely on using Hilbert spaces to characterise the hydrodynamic limit of interacting particle systems as in
[
4
,
3
,
50
,
51
,
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
31
]
.

We are not aware of any previous results establishing tightness criteria for interacting particle systems taking values in general
L_{r}
spaces. By using Díaz and Mayoral’s characterisation of compact subsets of Bochner spaces
[
22
, Theorem 3.2]
(for which an elementary proof can be found in
[
72
]
), we will derive a tightness criterion for
{L}_{4\deg q_{-}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
in Lemma
6.4
. The criterion is simple, and requires the following conditions:

(i)

The
(8\deg q_{-})
-moments of
\big((u^{N}(t,x))_{t\in[0,T],\,x\in\mathbb{R}}\big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
are uniformly bounded, i.e.

\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\;\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\;\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\;\mathbb{E}\Big[\|u^{N}(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}^{2(4\deg q_{-})}\Big]<\infty.

(ii)

Bounds on the expectation of the tail of
u^{N}(t,x)
in type space:

\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\;\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\;\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\;\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\;\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}\,u^{N}_{j}(t,x)\bigg]=0.

(iii)

Integral weak equicontinuity property:
There exist
l_{1},l_{2},C_{T}>0
such that for every
{\gamma}\in(-1,1)
and
i\in\{1,2\}
,

\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\;\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\|{\theta}^{(i)}_{{\gamma}}u^{N}_{k}-u^{N}_{k}\|_{{L}_{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})}\Big]\leq C_{T}|{\gamma}|^{l_{i}},

where for any function
g:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}
,
{\theta}^{(1)}_{\gamma}
and
{\theta}^{(2)}_{\gamma}
are the following shift maps:

({\theta}^{(1)}_{{\gamma}}g)(t,x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=g(t+{\gamma},x)\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{t+{\gamma}\in[0,T]\}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad({\theta}^{(2)}_{{\gamma}}g)(t,x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=g(t,x+{\gamma})\quad\forall\,(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}.

Tightness of
(u^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
in
{L}_{4\deg q_{-}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
will then follow from this criterion by combining (
2.27
) with the regularity properties derived from the Green’s function representation in (
2.28
).

Let
v=(v_{k}(t,x))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,t\in[0,T],\,x\in\mathbb{R}}
be any subsequential limit of
(u^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
in
{L}_{4\deg q_{-}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
. By Skorokhod’s representation theorem, we can construct
(u^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
v
on the same probability space in such a way that the subsequence converges almost surely. Then, by taking the limit as
N\rightarrow\infty
on both sides of the Green’s function representation in (
2.28
), and using the fact that the random walk semigroup converges to the heat kernel under Brownian scaling, letting
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
denote the semigroup of Brownian motion run at speed
m
, we will be able to establish in Lemma
6.8
that

\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{1+|x|^{2}}\left|v_{k}(t,x)-(P_{t}f_{k})(x)-\int_{0}^{t}\Big(P_{t-{\tau}}F_{k}(v(t,\cdot))\Big)(x)\,d{\tau}\right|\,dx\,dt\Bigg]=0,

i.e. that
v
is a mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
). Since establishing regularity properties for mild solutions is much more straightforward than for measure-valued weak solutions, we will be able to establish uniqueness and smoothness properties in Section
7
. In fact, we establish uniqueness in
{L}_{4\deg q_{-}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
for
v
by standard PDE arguments. The only limitation of using the space
{L}_{4\deg q_{-}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
is that we lose information about any specific instant of time, since for any
t\in(0,T]
, the set
\{t\}\times\mathbb{R}
has null Lebesgue measure. This, however, is not an important limitation, since by the tightness in
\mathscr{D}([0,\infty),\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}})
, we will establish in Lemma
6.7
that any limiting process
(u(t))_{t\geq 0}=((u_{k}(t))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{t\geq 0}
in
\mathscr{D}([0,\infty),\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}})
satisfies the following identity in distribution, for every
H\in\mathbb{N}
,
(t_{h})_{h\in[\![H]\!]}\in[0,T)^{H}
,
({\varphi}_{h})_{h\in[\![H]\!]}\in\mathscr{C}_{c}(\mathbb{R})^{H}
and
(k_{h})_{h\in[\![H]\!]}\in(\mathbb{N}_{0})^{H}
:

(\langle u_{k_{h}}(t_{h}),\,{\varphi}_{h}\rangle)_{h\in[\![H]\!]}\overset{d}{=}\Bigg(\lim_{t^{\prime}\downarrow t_{h}}\frac{1}{t^{\prime}-t_{h}}\int_{t_{h}}^{t^{\prime}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}v_{k_{h}}({\tau},x){\varphi}_{h}(x)\,dx\,d{\tau}\Bigg)_{h\in[\![H]\!]}.

Together with the uniqueness of mild solutions, this relation implies uniqueness for the measure-valued process, and will complete the proof of our convergence result (Theorem
2.1
).

It is important to note that although we work in the general setting of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
-valued functions, the framework extends naturally to the analysis of scaling limits of interacting particle systems with finitely many particle types. In this case, condition (ii) in the tightness criterion is trivial. Moreover, our method does not require the particle density to be almost surely bounded, nor does it require the birth and death rates to be uniformly bounded, or the initial number of particles to be finite.

Asymptotic properties of solutions of the PDE.

The smoothness properties of the limiting solution of the system of PDEs (
2.11
) will allow us to write the evolution of each of the densities
u_{k}
in terms of a Feynman-Kac formula. Then, in Section
8
, using the definition of a monostable reaction term from Definition
2.3
and an induction argument, we will prove Theorem
2.5
. Armed with this result, we will then be able to adapt an argument from
[
64
]
(which in turn is based on ideas in
[
17
]
) to compute the spreading speed. In
[
64
]
, the spreading speed of solutions
U
to the non-local Fisher-KPP equation is determined; by separately considering the cases when the population density
U
is low or high, the Feynman-Kac formula and Brownian motion estimates imply a lower bound on the spreading speed. The argument in
[
64
]
relies on the fact that when the density is low, i.e. for small values of
U
, the per-capita growth rate is large, while when the density is high, i.e. for large values of
U
, then this means that
U
has already propagated to the region.

In our setting, we consider the cases when the total population density
\|u\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}
is large or small, and analyse the impact of this on the dynamics of
u_{0}
. We assume that the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is of Fisher-KPP type (in the sense of Definition
2.3
). Then, if the density
\|u\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}
is small, the per-capita growth rate of
u_{0}
is large. On the other hand, if
\|u\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}
is large, Theorem
2.5
implies that
u_{0}
is bounded away from
0
. In Section
8
, by combining these ideas and those of
[
64
]
, we will be able to prove Theorem
2.7
.

3
Preliminaries

In this section, first, in Section
3.1
we will state the main results of the companion article
[
56
]
that we will use in this article. Then, in Section
3.2
, we define the meaning of weak and mild
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
-valued solutions to partial differential equations.

3.1
Moment bound and martingale problem for the spatial Muller’s ratchet

In this subsection, we give an overview of the properties of the spatial Muller’s ratchet proved in our companion article
[
56
]
. We start by stating some topological properties of the state space
\mathcal{S}^{N}
, which can be found in
[
56
, Proposition A.5]
.

Proposition 3.1
.

For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the metric space
(\mathcal{S}^{N},d_{\mathcal{S}^{N}})
defined in (
2.3
) is complete and separable. Moreover, a subset
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{S}^{N}
is relatively compact in the topology induced by
d_{\mathcal{S}^{N}}
if and only if all the following conditions are satisfied:

(i)

\mathcal{K}
is
|||\cdot|||_{\mathcal{S}^{N}}
-bounded, i.e.
\sup_{\boldsymbol{{\xi}}\in\mathcal{K}}|||\boldsymbol{{\xi}}|||_{\mathcal{S}^{N}}<\infty
.

(ii)

For any
{\varepsilon}>0
, there exists
R_{{\varepsilon}}>0
such that

\sup_{\boldsymbol{{\xi}}=({\xi}(y))_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{K}}\;\sum_{\{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}:\,|x|\geq R_{{\varepsilon}}\}}\frac{\|{\xi}(x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}}{(1+|x|)^{2}}\leq{\varepsilon}.

(iii)

For each
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, there exists
J^{(x)}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
k\geq J^{(x)}
,
{\xi}_{k}(x)=0
for all
\boldsymbol{{\xi}}=({\xi}_{j}(y))_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{K}
.

We now prepare to formally introduce the process
({\eta}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
that we call the spatial Muller’s ratchet. For any fixed
N\in\mathbb{N}
, under Assumptions
2
and
3
, for an initial configuration
\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
satisfying (
2.2
), the process
({\eta}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
with
{\eta}^{N}(0)=\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
almost surely will be given by the weak limit of a sequence of approximating Feller processes
({\eta}^{N,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. More precisely, let
({\lambda}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(0,\infty)
and
(K_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
denote increasing sequences such that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,{\lambda}_{n}=\infty\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,K_{n}=\infty.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}_{N,n}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=L^{-1}_{N}\mathbb{Z}\,\cap\,[-{\lambda}_{n},{\lambda}_{n}]
; the process
({\eta}^{N,n}(t))_{t\geq 0}
will be an
\mathcal{S}^{N}
-valued càdlàg Markov process. Informally, the process
{\eta}^{N,n}
can be described as follows: particles outside the box
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}_{N,n}
are frozen. All particles living inside
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}_{N,n}
can
migrate and die (at the rates in the informal description of
{\eta}^{N}
at the start of Section
2
), but particles on the boundary of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}_{N,n}
can only migrate to a deme inside
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}_{N,n}
. Finally, only particles that carry
K_{n}
or fewer mutations are able to reproduce (again, at the rates in the informal description at the start of Section
2
). Since the number of demes in
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 3\relax}}}_{N,n}
is finite, and only particles carrying at most
K_{n}
mutations can reproduce, by ignoring frozen particles, the process
({\eta}^{N,n}(t))_{t\geq 0}
can be thought of as a Markov process with finitely many types. A formal definition of the process
({\eta}^{N,n}(t))_{t\geq 0}
in terms of its infinitesimal generator is given in the companion article
[
56
, Section 2]
. In
[
56
, Theorem 2.2]
, we show that for
\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
satisfying (
2.2
),
conditioning on
{\eta}^{N,n}(0)=\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the sequence of processes
(({\eta}^{N,n}(t))_{t\geq 0})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
converges weakly as
n\rightarrow\infty
. More precisely, define a set of initial configurations
\mathcal{S}^{N}_{0}\subset\mathcal{S}^{N}
as follows (see
[
56
, Assumption 3]
):

\mathcal{S}^{N}_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Bigg\{\boldsymbol{{\xi}}=({\xi}_{j}(x))_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}^{N}:\;\sup_{x\in L^{-1}_{N}\mathbb{Z}}\|{\xi}(x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}<\infty\;\textrm{and}\;\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\,\sup_{x\in L^{-1}_{N}\mathbb{Z}}\,\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}\,{\xi}_{j}(x)=0\Bigg\}.

Then, by (
2.2
), for
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
satisfying Assumption
4
, the initial configuration
\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
given by (
2.1
) is an element of
\mathcal{S}^{N}_{0}
, and so
[
56
, Theorem 2.2]
yields the following result. Recall the definition of
\mathscr{C}_{*}(\mathcal{S}^{N},\mathbb{R})
in (
2.5
), and note that by
[
56
, Lemma 6.3]
, for
{\phi}\in\mathscr{C}_{*}(\mathcal{S}^{N},\mathbb{R})
and
\mathcal{L}^{N}{\phi}
as defined in (
2.6
)- (
2
),
\mathcal{L}^{N}{\phi}
is well-defined and finite on
\mathcal{S}^{N}
.

Theorem 3.2
.

Suppose
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
L_{N},\,m_{N}>0
,
{\mu}\in[0,1]
, and
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
q_{+}
,
q_{-}
and
f
satisfy Assumptions
2
,
3
and
4
. Define
\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}\in\mathcal{S}^{N}_{0}
as in (
2.1
). Then conditioning on
{\eta}^{N,n}(0)=\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the sequence of processes
(({\eta}^{N,n}(t))_{t\geq 0})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges weakly with respect to the
J_{1}
-topology on
\mathscr{D}([0,\infty),\mathcal{S}^{N})
as
n\rightarrow\infty
to an
\mathcal{S}^{N}
-valued càdlàg Markov process
({\eta}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
with
{\eta}^{N}(0)=\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
almost surely. Moreover,
({\eta}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a strong Markov process with respect to its right-continuous natural filtration
\{\mathcal{F}^{{\eta}^{N}}_{t+}\}_{t\geq 0}
. Furthermore, for any
{\phi}\in\mathscr{C}_{*}(\mathcal{S}^{N},\mathbb{R})
, there exists a càdlàg
\{\mathcal{F}^{{\eta}^{N}}_{t+}\}_{t\geq 0}
-square integrable martingale
(M^{{\phi}}(t))_{t\geq 0}
such that for all
T\geq 0
,

{\phi}({\eta}^{N}(T))={\phi}(\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N})+M^{{\phi}}(T)+\int_{0}^{T}(\mathcal{L}^{N}{\phi})({\eta}^{N}(t-))\,dt,

where
\mathcal{L}^{N}{\phi}
is defined in (
2.6
)- (
2
).

We will refer to the process
({\eta}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
as the spatial Muller’s ratchet. For
\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}\in\mathcal{S}^{N}_{0}
, we let
\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}
denote the probability measure under which
({\eta}^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is the spatial Muller’s ratchet with
{\eta}^{N}(0)=\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
almost surely, and
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}
the corresponding expectation. We will also use the following moment bound from our companion article
[
56
]
, which we state here for ease of reference. Note that for
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
satisfying Assumption
4
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
as defined in (
2.1
), we have

\sup_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\|{\eta}^{N}(x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\leq N\esssup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\|f(x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}<\infty.

Hence, the following result is a direct consequence of
[
56
, Theorem 2.3]
.

Theorem 3.3
.

Suppose
q_{+}
and
q_{-}
satisfy Assumption
3
, and
f
satisfies Assumption
4
. Then for any
r\geq 1
, there is a non-decreasing function
C_{r}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[1,\infty)
such that the following holds. For any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
L_{N},m_{N}>0
,
{\mu}\in[0,1]
and
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
satisfying Assumption
2
, for
\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
as defined in (
2.1
) and for all
t\geq 0
,

\sup_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\|u^{N}(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}^{r}\right]\leq C_{r}(t),

where
u^{N}(t,x)
is defined in (
2.8
).

3.2
Partial differential equations in
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}

In this subsection, we define
L_{r}
spaces of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
-valued functions, and weak and mild solutions to the system of PDEs (
2.11
). Let
(S,{\mathord{\hbox{\char 6\relax}}},{\nu})
be a measure space. We say a function
g=(g_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:S\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
is (Bochner) measurable with respect to
(S,{\mathord{\hbox{\char 6\relax}}},{\nu})
if
g_{k}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is measurable for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Note that in this case,
\|g\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}:S\rightarrow[0,\infty)
is the pointwise limit of a sequence of measurable functions, i.e.
\|g(x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}|g_{k}(x)|\;\forall\,x\in S
, and so
\|g\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}
is also measurable. Following
[
43
, Chapters 1 and 2]
, for
r\in[1,\infty]
, we let

L_{r}(S;{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{g:S\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}:\;g\textrm{ is measurable and }\|g\|_{L_{r}(S;{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Big\|\,\|g\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big\|_{L_{r}({\nu})}<\infty\right\}.

Note that
L_{r}(S;{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
is a Banach space (see e.g.
[
43
, Proposition 1.2.29]
). For
d\in\mathbb{N}
, for the Lebesgue measure space
(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathscr{R}^{(d)},{\lambda})
, for
S\in\mathscr{R}^{(d)}
, we say that a measurable function
g:S\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
is an element of
L_{1,\textrm{loc}}(S;{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
if for all compact sets
\mathcal{K}\subseteq S
, we have

\int_{\mathcal{K}}\|g(x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\;dx<\infty.

We are now ready to properly define the meaning of a weak solution to the PDE (
2.11
). We will be thinking of weak solutions in the sense of distributions, rather than in the sense of elements of Sobolev spaces (see
[
43
, Chapter 2]
for the difference between these concepts).

Definition 3.4

(Weak solution)
.

We say that a measurable function
u\in L_{1,\textrm{loc}}([0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
is a weak solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) if the following conditions are satisfied:

(i)

u(0,x)=f(x)
for
{\lambda}
-almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

(ii)

u(T,\cdot)\in L_{1,\textrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
for all
T\geq 0
.

(iii)

F(u):[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
is an element of
L_{1,\textrm{loc}}([0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
, where
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is the reaction term defined in (
2.12
).

(iv)

For any
{\varphi}\in\mathscr{C}^{1,2}_{c}([0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
, i.e. for any continuous function
{\varphi}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with compact support, continuously differentiable in time and twice continuously differentiable in space, and any
T>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}u(T,x){\varphi}(T,x)\,dx=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}u(0,x){\varphi}(0,x)\,dx+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}u(t,x){\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}_{t}{\varphi}(t,x)\,dx\,dt

(3.1)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{m}{2}u(t,x)\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}{\varphi}(t,x)\,dx\,dt+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}F(u(t,x)){\varphi}(t,x)\,dx\,dt.

Note that (
3.1
) holds if and only if it holds in a coordinate-wise manner, i.e. if and only if for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}u_{k}(T,x){\varphi}(T,x)\,dx=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}u_{k}(0,x){\varphi}(0,x)\,dx+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}u_{k}(t,x){\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}_{t}{\varphi}(t,x)\,dx\,dt

(3.2)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{m}{2}u_{k}(t,x)\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}{\varphi}(t,x)\,dx\,dt+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}F_{k}(u(t,x)){\varphi}(t,x)\,dx\,dt.

We refer the reader to
[
43
, Chapter 1]
for a proof of this equivalence.

We will also need the definition of mild solutions to the system of PDEs (
2.11
). Let
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
denote the semigroup of Brownian motion run at speed
m
. The following notation will be useful: for
t>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we let

p(t,x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2{\pi}mt}}e^{-x^{2}/(2mt)}.

(3.3)

Analogously to the definition of the action of
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
on real-valued functions, with a slight abuse of notation we can define its action on
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
-valued functions as follows. Let
v:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
be a measurable function. For all
(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
such that
(P_{t}\|v\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})(x)<\infty
,
we can define

\left(P_{t}v\right)(x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}p(t,x-y)v(y)\;dy&\textrm{ if }t>0,\\
v(x)&\textrm{ if }t=0.\end{array}\right.

(3.4)

Definition 3.5

(Mild solution)
.

We say that a measurable function
u:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
is a (global) mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) if and only if the following conditions are satisfied:

(i)

For
{\lambda}
-almost every
(T,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
,
\left(P_{T-t}\|F(u(t,\cdot))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\right)(x)<\infty
for
{\lambda}
-almost every
t\in[0,T]
.

(ii)

For
{\lambda}
-almost every
(T,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
,

u(T,x)=\left(P_{T}f\right)(x)+\int_{0}^{T}\Big(P_{T-t}F(u(t,\cdot))\Big)(x)\,dt.

(3.5)

Remark 3.6
.

We collect here some observations regarding Definition
3.5
.

(a)

Although our definition of a mild solution is compatible with the integral formulations used in the
L_{r}
-regularity literature (see e.g.
[
67
, Chapter 5]
), classical semigroup approaches to deterministic PDEs typically require mild solutions to be continuous in time and to satisfy the variation-of-constants formula (
3.5
) pointwise in time (see e.g.
[
63
, Section 4.1]
). In contrast, we work with a weaker formulation in which (
3.5
) holds almost everywhere in time and in space. This choice reflects the fact that our construction of solutions takes place in
L_{r}
-based function spaces, where time and space continuity is not available
a priori
.

(b)

Let
T^{\prime}>0
be fixed. If a measurable function
u:[0,T^{\prime}]\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
satisfies Definition
3.5
(i) and (ii) for
{\lambda}
-almost every
(T,x)\in[0,T^{\prime}]\times\mathbb{R}
, we say
u
is a (local) mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
).

Our next result shows that, under mild additional assumptions, a mild solution
is also a weak solution.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
u:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
denote a measurable function. Suppose that
u
is a mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) in the sense of Definition
3.5
, and that it satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

u(0,x)=f(x)
for
{\lambda}
-almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

(ii)

For all
T>0
,
u\big|_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}}\in L_{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
,
where
u\big|_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}}
denotes the restriction of
u
to
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
.

(iii)

For all
T>0
,
u
satisfies identity (
3.5
) for
{\lambda}
-almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

Then
u
is a weak solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) in the sense of Definition
3.4
.

Although the equivalence between mild and weak solutions is classical under higher regularity assumptions, we did not find a result in the literature at the level of generality required in this article. For this reason, and for the sake of completeness, we include the proof of Lemma
3.7
in Section
0.A.6
of the appendix.

4
Green’s function representation

Recall the definition of the approximate density process
u^{N}
in (
2.8
). In this section, we will apply a technique used in
[
60
,
25
]
based on a Green’s function representation to obtain uniform estimates on
u^{N}_{k}(t,x)
for large
k
, and estimates on both space and time increments of
(u^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
. We begin by briefly highlighting the main differences between our setting and that of
[
60
,
25
]
.

Mueller and Tribe
[
60
]
, and later Durrett and Fan
[
25
]
, studied the density of particles in a scaled version of the voter model. Since in their model, under a suitable rescaling, the local density of particles is uniformly bounded by
1
, a Green’s function representation combined with random walk estimates allows them to prove Hölder estimates on the expectation of the particle density. In our case, we have two main challenges. First, the particle density is not uniformly bounded, so although we will be able to derive estimates for the space and time increments, we will not be able to establish Hölder estimates for these increments. Second, since we must keep track of infinitely many types of particles, we will prove estimates that depend on the subset of types of particles considered (here, the ‘type’ of a particle refers to the number of mutations that it carries). This will provide us with some control over
u^{N}_{k}(t,x)
for large
k
. We will repeatedly use the random walk estimates stated in Lemma
0.A.3
in Section
0.A.2
of the appendix.

For any set of indices
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
T\geq 0
, and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, let

U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T,x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}u^{N}_{k}(T,x).

(4.1)

In particular, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we have
U^{N}_{\{k\}}\equiv u^{N}_{k}
. Our strategy will be to write
U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T,x)
for each
T\geq 0
and
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
in terms of an appropriate martingale problem.

For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
(X^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
denote a simple symmetric random walk on
L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
with total jump rate
m_{N}
. We denote its infinitesimal generator by
\frac{m_{N}}{2}\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{N}_{m}
, where for any bounded function
g:L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and for all
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
,

\frac{m_{N}}{2}\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{N}_{m}g(x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{m_{N}}{2}\left(g(x+L_{N}^{-1})+g(x-L_{N}^{-1})-2g(x)\right).

(4.2)

Let
\{P^{N}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
be the semigroup associated with
\frac{m_{N}}{2}\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{N}_{m}
. By Assumption
1
(i), we have
\frac{m_{N}}{L_{N}^{2}}\rightarrow m
as
N\rightarrow\infty
, and so
\{P^{N}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
converges as
N\rightarrow\infty
to the semigroup
\left\{P_{t}\right\}_{t\geq 0}
associated with Brownian motion on
\mathbb{R}
run at total rate
m
(recall that we defined this before (
3.4
)). Following Durrett and Fan
[
25
]
, we define the normalised transition density
p^{N}
by letting

p^{N}(t,x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=L_{N}\mathbb{P}_{0}\left(X^{N}(t)=x\right)=L_{N}\mathbb{P}\left(X^{N}(t)=x\Big|X^{N}(0)=0\right)\quad\forall\,N\in\mathbb{N},\,t\geq 0,\,x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}.

(4.3)

Then, for any fixed
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
T\geq 0
and
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, let
{\phi}^{N,T,x}:[0,\infty)\times L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\left[0,L_{N}\right]
be given by

{\phi}^{N,T,x}(t,y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}p^{N}\left(T-t,y-x\right)&\textrm{if }t\in[0,T],\\
0&\textrm{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

(4.4)

Observe that, by standard large deviation results on Poisson processes and continuous-time random walks, see e.g.
[
56
, Corollary A.10]
, for
y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
such that
|y-x|\geq e^{2}m_{N}TL_{N}^{-1}
and
t\in[0,T]
,

{\phi}^{N,T,x}(t,y)\leq L_{N}e^{-L_{N}|y-x|}.

(4.5)

For
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
g,h:L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we write

\langle g,h\rangle_{N}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}g(x)h(x),

(4.6)

whenever the sum on the right-hand side is well defined. Using the notation defined above, we now work towards the Green’s function representation. We start by proving a simple consequence of the moment bound in Theorem
3.3
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Suppose
q_{+}
and
q_{-}
satisfy Assumption
3
, and
f
satisfies Assumption
4
. Then, for any
l,r\geq 1
and
T\geq 0
, there exists
C_{l,r,T}>0
such that for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
L_{N},m_{N}>0
,
{\mu}\in[0,1]
and
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
satisfying Assumption
2
, for
\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
as defined in (
2.1
),

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}\end{subarray}}\;\sup_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\;\sup_{t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,T]}\;\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\Big\langle U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t_{2},\cdot)^{l},{\phi}^{N,T,x}(t_{1},\cdot)\Big\rangle_{N}^{r}\right]\leq C_{l,r,T}.

Proof.

By the definitions in (
4.1
), (
4.4
), (
4.3
) and (
4.6
), observe that for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,T]
and
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle\Big\langle U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t_{2},\cdot)^{l},{\phi}^{N,T,x}(t_{1},\cdot)\Big\rangle_{N}\leq\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\|u^{N}(t_{2},y)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}^{l}\cdot\mathbb{P}_{0}\Big(X^{N}(T-t_{1})=y-x\Big).

(4.7)

By Jensen’s inequality, it follows that

\displaystyle\Big\langle U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t_{2},\cdot)^{l},{\phi}^{N,T,x}(t_{1},\cdot)\Big\rangle_{N}^{r}\leq\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\|u^{N}(t_{2},y)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}^{lr}\mathbb{P}_{0}\Big(X^{N}(T-t_{1})=y-x\Big).

(4.8)

By taking expectations on both sides of (
4.8
) and applying Theorem
3.3
, the result follows.
∎

It will be convenient to introduce, for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
T\geq 0
,
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, the function
g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{S}^{N}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
given by, for all
t\in[0,T]
and
\boldsymbol{{\xi}}=({\xi}_{k}(y))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}^{N}
,

g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,\boldsymbol{{\xi}})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Bigg\langle\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\frac{{\xi}_{k}(\cdot)}{N},{\phi}^{N,T,x}(t,\cdot)\Bigg\rangle_{N}.

(4.9)

By the definition of the metric space
(\mathcal{S}^{N},d_{\mathcal{S}^{N}})
in (
2.3
) and (
2.4
), and by (
4.5
),
g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}
is well defined as a real-valued continuous map on
[0,T]\times\mathcal{S}^{N}
. We now characterise the action of the infinitesimal generator
\mathcal{L}^{N}
(defined in (
2.6
) and (
2
)) on
g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}
. Before stating this result, we recall that
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is the reaction term defined in (
2.12
), and we define the modified reaction term
F^{+}=(F^{+}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
as follows. For all
u=(u_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we let

F^{+}_{k}(u)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=q_{+}(\|u\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})\left(s_{k}(1-{\mu})u_{k}+\mathds{1}_{\{k\geq 1\}}s_{k-1}{\mu}u_{k-1}\right)+q_{-}(\|u\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})u_{k}.

(4.10)

Recalling (
4.3
), for
t\geq 0
and
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, we let

\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(t,x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=L_{N}\left(p^{N}(t,x+L_{N}^{-1})-p^{N}(t,x)\right).

(4.11)

We also recall the definition of
\mathscr{C}_{*}(\mathcal{S}^{N};\mathbb{R})
in (
2.5
).

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
L_{N},m_{N}>0
,
{\mu}\in[0,1]
,
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
satisfies Assumption
2
,
q_{+}
and
q_{-}
satisfy Assumption
3
,
f
satisfies Assumption
4
, and
\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
is defined as in (
2.1
). Then, for any
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
,
T\geq 0
and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the map
g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{S}^{N}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
defined in (
4.9
) satisfies the following properties:

(i)

The map
[0,T]\times\mathcal{S}^{N}\ni(t,\boldsymbol{{\xi}})\mapsto g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,\boldsymbol{{\xi}})
is in
\mathscr{C}([0,T]\times\mathcal{S}^{N};\mathbb{R})
. Moreover, the map
(0,T)\times\mathcal{S}^{N}\ni(t,\boldsymbol{{\xi}})\mapsto\left(\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}t}g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{{\xi}})\right)(t)
is in
\mathscr{C}((0,T)\times\mathcal{S}^{N};\mathbb{R})
, and is given by, for
t\in(0,T)
and
\boldsymbol{{\xi}}\in\mathcal{S}^{N}
,

\left(\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}t}g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{{\xi}})\right)(t)=\frac{m_{N}}{2}\Big(2g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}-g^{N,T,x-L_{N}^{-1}}_{\mathcal{I}}-g^{N,T,x+L_{N}^{-1}}_{\mathcal{I}}\Big)(t,\boldsymbol{{\xi}}).

(4.12)

(ii)

For any
t\in[0,T]
, the map
\mathcal{S}^{N}\ni\boldsymbol{{\xi}}\mapsto g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,\boldsymbol{{\xi}})
is in
\mathscr{C}_{*}(\mathcal{S}^{N};\mathbb{R})
. Moreover, the map
[0,T]\times\mathcal{S}^{N}\ni(t,\boldsymbol{{\xi}})\mapsto\mathcal{L}^{N}\Big(g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,\cdot)\Big)(\boldsymbol{{\xi}})
is in
\mathscr{C}([0,T]\times\mathcal{S}^{N};\mathbb{R})
, and is given by, for
t\in[0,T]
and
\boldsymbol{{\xi}}=({\xi}_{k}(y))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}^{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{N}\Big(g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,\cdot)\Big)(\boldsymbol{{\xi}})

(4.13)

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{N}}{2}\Big(g^{N,T,x-L_{N}^{-1}}_{\mathcal{I}}+g^{N,T,x+L_{N}^{-1}}_{\mathcal{I}}-2g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}\Big)(t,\boldsymbol{{\xi}})+\Bigg\langle\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\,F_{k}\left(\frac{{\xi}(\cdot)}{N}\right),\,{\phi}^{N,T,x}(t,\cdot)\Bigg\rangle_{N}.

(iii)

The map
[0,T]\times\mathcal{S}^{N}\ni(t,\boldsymbol{{\xi}})\mapsto\mathcal{L}^{N}\Big((g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}})^{2}(t,\cdot)\Big)(\boldsymbol{{\xi}})
is in
\mathscr{C}([0,T]\times\mathcal{S}^{N};\mathbb{R})
, and is given by, for
t\in[0,T]
and
\boldsymbol{{\xi}}=({\xi}_{k}(y))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}^{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{N}\Big((g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}})^{2}(t,\cdot)\Big)(\boldsymbol{{\xi}})

(4.14)

\displaystyle=2g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,\boldsymbol{{\xi}})\mathcal{L}^{N}\Big(g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,\cdot)\Big)(\boldsymbol{{\xi}})+\frac{1}{NL_{N}^{2}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}p^{N}(T-t,y-x)^{2}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}F^{+}_{k}\left(\frac{{\xi}(y)}{N}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{m_{N}}{2NL^{4}_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\left(\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T-t,y-L_{N}^{-1}-x)^{2}+\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T-t,y-x)^{2}\right)\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\frac{{\xi}_{k}(y)}{N}.

(iv)

The following estimate holds:

\displaystyle\sup_{t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,T]}

\displaystyle\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Bigg[\Big(g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}\Big)^{2}(t_{1},{\eta}^{N}(t_{2}))+\left(\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}t}g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(\cdot,{\eta}^{N}(t_{2}))\right)^{2}(t_{1})\Bigg]

(4.15)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Bigg[\Big(g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}\Big)^{2}(t_{1},{\eta}^{N}(t_{2}))\left(\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}t}g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(\cdot,{\eta}^{N}(t_{2}))\right)^{2}(t_{1})\Bigg]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Bigg[\Big(\mathcal{L}^{N}(g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(t_{1},\cdot))\Big)^{2}({\eta}^{N}(t_{2}))+\Big(\mathcal{L}^{N}(g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}})^{2}(t_{1},\cdot)\Big)^{2}({\eta}^{N}(t_{2}))\Bigg]\Bigg)<\infty.

(v)

For any fixed
t_{1}\in[0,T]
, the process
(M(t))_{t\geq 0}
given by, for
t\geq 0
,

M(t)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Big(g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(t_{1},{\eta}^{N}(t))\Big)^{2}-\Big(g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(t_{1},{\eta}^{N}(0))\Big)^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}^{N}\Big((g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}})^{2}(t_{1},\cdot)\Big)({\eta}^{N}({\tau}-))\,d{\tau},

is a càdlàg martingale with respect to the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}^{{\eta}^{N}}_{t+}\}_{t\geq 0}
;

(vi)

For any
T_{1},T_{2}\in[0,T]
and
x_{1},x_{2}\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, the map
[0,T_{1}\wedge T_{2}]\times\mathcal{S}^{N}\ni(t,\boldsymbol{{\xi}})\,\mapsto\mathcal{L}^{N}\Big((g^{N,T_{1},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}-g^{N,T_{2},x_{2}}_{\mathcal{I}})^{2}(t,\cdot)\Big)(\boldsymbol{{\xi}})
is in
\mathscr{C}([0,T_{1}\wedge T_{2}]\times\mathcal{S}^{N};\mathbb{R})
and is given by, for
t\in[0,T_{1}\wedge T_{2}]
and
\boldsymbol{{\xi}}=({\xi}_{k}(y))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathcal{S}^{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{N}\Big((g^{N,T_{1},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}-g^{N,T_{2},x_{2}}_{\mathcal{I}})^{2}(t,\cdot)\Big)(\boldsymbol{{\xi}})

(4.16)

\displaystyle=2\Big(g^{N,T_{1},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}-g^{N,T_{2},x_{2}}_{\mathcal{I}}\Big)(t,\boldsymbol{{\xi}})\mathcal{L}^{N}\Big((g^{N,T_{1},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}-g^{N,T_{2},x_{2}}_{\mathcal{I}})(t,\cdot)\Big)(\boldsymbol{{\xi}})

\displaystyle+\frac{m_{N}}{2NL_{N}^{4}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\Big({\delta}^{N,T_{1},T_{2},x_{1},x_{2}}_{-}(t,y)+{\delta}^{N,T_{1},T_{2},x_{1},x_{2}}_{+}(t,y)\Big)\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\frac{{\xi}_{k}(y)}{N}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{NL_{N}^{2}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\;\Big(p^{N}(T_{1}-t,y-x_{1})-p^{N}(T_{2}-t,y-x_{2})\Big)^{2}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}F^{+}_{k}\left(\frac{{\xi}(y)}{N}\right),

where

\displaystyle{\delta}^{N,T_{1},T_{2},x_{1},x_{2}}_{-}(t,y)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Big(\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T_{1}-t,y-L_{N}^{-1}-x_{1})-\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T_{2}-t,y-L_{N}^{-1}-x_{2})\Big)^{2},

(4.17)

\displaystyle{\delta}^{N,T_{1},T_{2},x_{1},x_{2}}_{+}(t,y)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Big(\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T_{1}-t,y-x_{1})-\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T_{2}-t,y-x_{2})\Big)^{2}.

(4.18)

Furthermore,

\sup_{t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,T_{1}\wedge T_{2}]}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\left(\mathcal{L}^{N}\Big((g^{N,T_{1},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}-g^{N,T_{2},x_{2}}_{\mathcal{I}})^{2}(t_{1},\cdot)\Big)({\eta}^{N}(t_{2}))\right)^{2}\right]<\infty.

(4.19)

Since the proof of Lemma
4.2
follows from standard arguments, we postpone it until Section
0.A.6
in the appendix. Importantly, Lemma
4.2
will now allow us to prove a Green’s function representation of the spatial Muller’s ratchet. Recall from after (
4.2
) that for
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
\{P^{N}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
be the semigroup associated to the simple symmetric random walk on
L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
with total jump rate
m_{N}
. For any set of indices
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
, define
\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by letting, for
T\geq 0
and
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
,

\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T,x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T,x)-\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}P^{N}_{T}u^{N}_{k}(0,\cdot)(x),

(4.20)

and linearly interpolating between the demes
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
to extend the definition of
\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T,x)
to all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. We are now ready to characterise
\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}
in terms of a semimartingale, giving us our Green’s function representation.

Lemma 4.3

(Green’s function representation)
.

Suppose
N\in\mathbb{N}
and the conditions of Lemma
4.2
are satisfied. Then for any
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
,
T\geq 0
and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
, under
\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}
, there exist a càdlàg square integrable martingale
(M^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(t))_{t\in[0,T]}
with respect to the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}^{{\eta}^{N}}_{t+}\}_{t\geq 0}
with
M^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(0)=0
, and a finite variation process
(A^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(t))_{t\in[0,T]}
such that

\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T,x)=M^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(T)+A^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(T).

(4.21)

Moreover, for
t\in[0,T]
,

A^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(t)=\frac{1}{L_{N}}\,\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\;F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau}-,y))p^{N}(T-{\tau},y-x)\,d{\tau},

(4.22)

and the predictable bracket process of
(M^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(t))_{t\in[0,T]}
is given by, for
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\left\langle M^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}\right\rangle(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{NL_{N}^{2}}\,\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\int_{0}^{t}p^{N}(T-{\tau},y-x)^{2}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\,F_{k}^{+}(u^{N}({\tau}-,y))\,d{\tau}

(4.23)

\displaystyle+\frac{m_{N}}{2NL_{N}^{4}}\,\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\int_{0}^{t}\left(\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T-{\tau},y-L_{N}^{-1}-x)^{2}+\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T-{\tau},y-x)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}u^{N}_{k}({\tau}-,y)\,d{\tau}.

Proof.

The result will follow from an application of Lemma
4.2
and a standard stochastic chain rule formula (see Lemmas
0.A.1
and
0.A.2
in the appendix). Indeed, let
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
,
T\geq 0
and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
be fixed. By the definition of
g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}
in (
4.9
) and by (
4.4
), (
4.1
) and (
2.8
), we almost surely have

U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T,x)=g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(T,{\eta}^{N}(T)).

(4.24)

We now claim that the map
g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{S}^{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
satisfies the conditions of Lemmas
0.A.1
and
0.A.2
with
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}^{N}
,
{\eta}={\eta}^{N}
and
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}^{N}
. Indeed, checking the conditions for Lemma
0.A.1
, Lemma
4.2
tells us that
g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}\in\mathscr{C}([0,T]\times\mathcal{S}^{N},\mathbb{R})
, and
g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}
satisfies condition (i) by estimate (
4.15
) and Jensen’s inequality, condition (ii) by Lemma
4.2
(i), condition (iii) by Lemma
4.2
(ii), and condition (iv) by estimate (
4.15
). Then checking the conditions of Lemma
0.A.2
,
g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}
satisfies condition (i) by Lemma
4.2
(iii) and (v), and condition (ii) by estimate (
4.15
). Therefore, by Lemma
0.A.1
, there exists a càdlàg martingale
(M^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(t))_{t\in[0,T]}
with respect to the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}^{{\eta}^{N}}_{t+}\}_{t\geq 0}
with
M^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(0)=0
such that

\displaystyle g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(T,{\eta}^{N}(T))

\displaystyle=g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(0,{\eta}^{N}(0))+M^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(T)

(4.25)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\left(\left(\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}t}g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(\cdot,{\eta}^{N}(t-))\right)(t)+\mathcal{L}^{N}\Big(g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,\cdot)\Big)({\eta}^{N}(t-))\right)\,dt

\displaystyle=g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(0,{\eta}^{N}(0))+M^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(T)+\int_{0}^{T}\Bigg\langle\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\,F_{k}(u^{N}(t-,\cdot)),{\phi}^{N,T,x}(t,\cdot)\Bigg\rangle_{N}\,dt

\displaystyle=g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(0,{\eta}^{N}(0))+M^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(T)+A^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(T),

where in the second equality we used identities (
4.12
) and (
4.13
), and in the third equality we used (
4.22
) and (
4.4
). Moreover, by (
4.9
), (
4.4
) and the definition of
\{P^{N}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
after (
4.2
), we have

g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(0,{\eta}^{N}(0))=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\,P^{N}_{T}u^{N}_{k}(0,\cdot)(x).

(4.26)

Applying (
4.20
), (
4.24
) and (
4.26
) to (
4.25
), and then rearranging terms, we get

\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T,x)=M^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(T)+A^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(T).

It remains to compute the predictable bracket process of
(M^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}(t))_{t\in[0,T]}
. By Lemma
0.A.2
in the appendix, we have, for
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\left\langle M^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}\right\rangle(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left(\mathcal{L}^{N}\Big((g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}})^{2}({\tau},\cdot)\Big)({\eta}^{N}({\tau}-))-2g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}({\tau},{\eta}^{N}({\tau}-))\mathcal{L}^{N}\Big(g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}({\tau},\cdot)\Big)({\eta}^{N}({\tau}-))\right)d{\tau}.

Hence, by (
4.14
), we get (
4.23
), as claimed.

∎

We now apply Lemma
4.3
to obtain first moment estimates on the increments of
\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}
over time and space.

Lemma 4.4
.

Suppose
(m_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(L_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
q_{+}
,
q_{-}
and
f
satisfy Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
. For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, define
\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
as in (
2.1
). Then for any
T\geq 0
, there exists
C_{T}>0
such that for all
0\leq T_{1}\leq T_{2}\leq T
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
x_{1},x_{2}\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
,

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}\end{subarray}}\;\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\left|\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1},x_{1})-\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2},x_{2})\right|\right]\leq C_{T}\left(|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1/2}+|x_{1}-x_{2}|+\left(T_{2}-T_{1}\right)^{1/4}\right).

(4.27)

Proof.

By the triangle inequality, we have that almost surely,

\left|\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1},x_{1})-\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2},x_{2})\right|\leq\left|\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2},x_{1})-\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2},x_{2})\right|+\left|\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2},x_{1})-\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1},x_{1})\right|.

(4.28)

We will bound the two terms on the right-hand side of (
4.28
) separately.

Step
(1)
: Space increments

We claim that the space increments satisfy the following bound: there exists
C_{T}^{(1)}>0
such that for all
0\leq T_{2}\leq T
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{1},x_{2}\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\left|\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2},x_{1})-\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2},x_{2})\right|\right]\leq C^{(1)}_{T}\left(|x_{1}-x_{2}|+{|x_{1}-x_{2}|}^{1/2}\right).

(4.29)

We now prove (
4.29
). Observe first that by Lemma
4.3
and the triangle inequality, for
0\leq T_{2}\leq T
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{1},x_{2}\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\left|\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2},x_{1})-\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2},x_{2})\right|\leq\left|M^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2})-M^{N,T_{2},x_{2}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2})\right|+\left|A^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2})-A^{N,T_{2},x_{2}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2})\right|.

(4.30)

We will again bound the terms on the right-hand side of (
4.30
) separately. For the second term on the right-hand side, by (
4.22
) from Lemma
4.3
and the triangle inequality, we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\left|A^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2})-A^{N,T_{2},x_{2}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2})\right|\right]

(4.31)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\int_{0}^{T_{2}}\left|p^{N}\left(T_{2}-t,y-x_{1}\right)-p^{N}\left(T_{2}-t,y-x_{2}\right)\right|\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\left|F_{k}(u^{N}(t-,y))\right|\right]\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\int_{0}^{T_{2}}\left|p^{N}\left(T_{2}-t,y-x_{1}\right)-p^{N}\left(T_{2}-t,y-x_{2}\right)\right|\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[F_{k}^{+}(u^{N}(t-,y))\right]\,dt,

where in the second inequality we used the definitions of
(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
in (
2.12
) and
(F^{+}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
in (
4.10
) together with the triangle inequality. Now, notice that there exists
C^{(1)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f}(T)>0
such that for any
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
t\in[0,T]
and
y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\,\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[F_{k}^{+}(u^{N}(t-,y))\right]

(4.32)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[q_{+}\left(\|u^{N}(t-,y)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\right)\left(s_{k}(1-{\mu})u^{N}_{k}(t-,y)+\mathds{1}_{\{k\geq 1\}}s_{k-1}{\mu}u^{N}_{k-1}(t-,y)\right)

\displaystyle+q_{-}\left(\|u^{N}(t-,y)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\right)u^{N}_{k}(t-,y)\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[\|u^{N}(t-,y)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\left(q_{+}\left(\|u^{N}(t-,y)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\right)+q_{-}\left(\|u^{N}(t-,y)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\right)\right)\Big]

\displaystyle\leq{C}^{(1)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f}(T),

where the first inequality follows since
s_{k}\leq 1
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
by Assumption
2
, and the second inequality follows from Theorem
3.3
. Hence, applying (
4.32
) and then estimate (
0.A.7
) from Lemma
0.A.3
in the appendix to (
4.31
), we get that there exists
C^{(2)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f}(T)>0
such that for any
0\leq T_{2}\leq T
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{1},x_{2}\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\left|A^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2})-A^{N,T_{2},x_{2}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2})\right|\right]

(4.33)

\displaystyle\leq C^{(1)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f}(T)\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{T_{2}}\left|p^{N}\left(T_{2}-t,y-x_{1}\right)-p^{N}\left(T_{2}-t,y-x_{2}\right)\right|dt

\displaystyle\leq C^{(2)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f}(T)|x_{1}-x_{2}|.

Moving now to the first term on the right-hand side of (
4.30
), notice that since by Lemma
4.3
, both processes
(M^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(t))_{t\in[0,T_{2}]}
and
(M^{N,T_{2},x_{2}}_{\mathcal{I}}(t))_{t\in[0,T_{2}]}
are martingales with respect to the same filtration
\{\mathcal{F}^{{\eta}^{N}}_{t+}\}_{t\in[0,T_{2}]}
, their difference is also a martingale with respect to this filtration. We now bound the predictable bracket process of the difference.
By Lemma
4.2
(vi) and the same argument as in the proof of (
4.23
) in Lemma
4.3
, with
g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}
replaced by
g^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}-g^{N,T_{2},x_{2}}_{\mathcal{I}}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\left\langle M^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}-M^{N,T_{2},x_{2}}_{\mathcal{I}}\right\rangle(T_{2})\right]

(4.34)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{NL_{N}^{2}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{T_{2}}\left(p^{N}\left(T_{2}-t,y-x_{1}\right)-p^{N}\left(T_{2}-t,y-x_{2}\right)\right)^{2}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[F_{k}^{+}(u^{N}(t-,y))\right]\,dt

\displaystyle+\frac{m_{N}}{2NL_{N}^{4}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{T_{2}}\Big({\delta}^{N,T_{2},T_{2},x_{1},x_{2}}_{-}(t,y)+{\delta}^{N,T_{2},T_{2},x_{1},x_{2}}_{+}(t,y)\Big)\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[u^{N}_{k}(t-,y)\right]\,dt,

where the terms
{\delta}^{N,T_{2},T_{2},x_{1},x_{2}}_{-}(t,y)
and
{\delta}^{N,T_{2},T_{2},x_{1},x_{2}}_{+}(t,y)
are defined in (
4.17
) and (
4.18
) respectively. Using the elementary inequality (
0.A.34
) with
p=2
, we have that for
t\in[0,T_{2}]
and
y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\delta}^{N,T_{2},T_{2},x_{1},x_{2}}_{-}(t,y)\leq 2\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T_{2}-t,y-L_{N}^{-1}-x_{1})^{2}+2\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T_{2}-t,y-L_{N}^{-1}-x_{2})^{2},

(4.35)

\displaystyle\textrm{and }

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\delta}^{N,T_{2},T_{2},x_{1},x_{2}}_{+}(t,y)\leq 2\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T_{2}-t,y-x_{1})^{2}+2\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T_{2}-t,y-x_{2})^{2}.

Therefore, substituting into (
4.34
), using (
4.32
) and the random walk estimate (
0.A.9
) from Lemma
0.A.3
in the appendix to bound the first term on the right-hand side, and using Theorem
3.3
, (
4.35
), (
4.11
) and (
0.A.9
) again to bound the second term on the right-hand side, there exists
C^{(1)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f,m}(T)>0
such that for any
0\leq T_{2}\leq T
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{1},x_{2}\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\left\langle M^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}-M^{N,T_{2},x_{2}}_{\mathcal{I}}\right\rangle(T_{2})\right]\leq C^{(1)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f,m}(T)\left(\frac{1}{NL_{N}}|x_{1}-x_{2}|+\frac{m_{N}}{NL_{N}}\cdot L_{N}^{-1}\right).

(4.36)

Observe that there exists a constant
K>0
such that for any càdlàg martingale
(M(t))_{t\geq 0}
with
M(0)=0
and any
t\geq 0
, by Jensen’s inequality and then by the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy (BDG) inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{{\tau}\leq t}|M({\tau})|\Big]^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{{\tau}\leq t}|M({\tau})|^{2}\Big]\leq K\mathbb{E}\Big[[M](t)\Big]=K\mathbb{E}\Big[\langle M\rangle(t)\Big].

(4.37)

Therefore, by (
4.36
) and since
m_{N}/L_{N}^{2}\rightarrow m\in(0,\infty)
and
L_{N}={\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}(N)
as
N\rightarrow\infty
by Assumption
1
, there exists
C^{(2)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f,m}(T)>0
such that for any
0\leq T_{2}\leq T
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{1}\neq x_{2}\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}^{N}}}\left[\left|\left(M^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}-M^{N,T_{2},x_{2}}_{\mathcal{I}}\right)(T_{2})\right|\right]\leq C^{(2)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f,m}(T)|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1/2}.

(4.38)

Hence, combining (
4.30
), (
4.33
) and (
4.38
), we obtain (
4.29
), as claimed.

Step
(2)
: Time increments

We will now bound the expectation of the second term on the right-hand side of (
4.28
); we claim that there exists
C^{(2)}_{T}>0
such that for all
0\leq T_{1}\leq T_{2}\leq T
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}^{N}}}\left[\left|\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2},x_{1})-\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1},x_{1})\right|\right]\leq C^{(2)}_{T}(T_{2}-T_{1})^{1/4}.

(4.39)

To prove the claim, we begin by noting that by Lemma
4.3
and the triangle inequality, almost surely

\displaystyle\left|\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2},x_{1})-\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1},x_{1})\right|

(4.40)

\displaystyle\leq\left|A^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2})-A^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1})\right|+\left|A^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1})-A^{N,T_{1},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1})\right|

\displaystyle+\left|M^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2})-M^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1})\right|+\left|M^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1})-M^{N,T_{1},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1})\right|.

We will tackle each of the terms on the right-hand side of (
4.40
) separately. For the first term, note that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and all
u\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
,
|F_{k}(u)|\leq F^{+}_{k}(u)
by (
2.12
) and (
4.10
). Therefore, by (
4.22
) in Lemma
4.3
, and by Fubini’s theorem, and then by (
4.32
), for
0\leq T_{1}\leq T_{2}\leq T
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{1}\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}^{N}}}\left[\left|A^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2})-A^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1})\right|\right]

(4.41)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{T_{1}}^{T_{2}}p^{N}\left(T_{2}-t,y-x_{1}\right)\cdot\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[F^{+}_{k}(u^{N}(t-,y))\Big]\,dt

\displaystyle\leq C^{(1)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f}(T)\cdot\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{T_{1}}^{T_{2}}p^{N}\left(T_{2}-t,y-x_{1}\right)\,dt

\displaystyle=C^{(1)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f}(T)(T_{2}-T_{1}),

where for the last identity we applied the fact that, by the definition of
p^{N}
in (
4.3
),

\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}p^{N}\left({\tau},y-x_{1}\right)=1\quad\forall\,{\tau}\geq 0.

(4.42)

For the second term on the right-hand side of (
4.40
), we again apply (
4.22
) from Lemma
4.3
and use that
|F_{k}(u)|\leq F^{+}_{k}(u)

\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
u\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
, and then use estimate (
4.32
) and the random walk estimate (
0.A.6
) from Lemma
0.A.3
in the appendix, obtaining that there exists
C^{(3)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f,m}(T)>0
such that for any
0\leq T_{1}\leq T_{2}\leq T
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{1}\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\left|A^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1})-A^{N,T_{1},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1})\right|\right]

(4.43)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{T_{1}}\left|p^{N}\left(T_{2}-t,y-x_{1}\right)-p^{N}\left(T_{1}-t,y-x_{1}\right)\right|\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[F^{+}_{k}(u^{N}(t-,y))\Big]\,dt

\displaystyle\leq C^{(3)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f,m}(T)(T_{2}-T_{1})^{1/2}.

Now tackling the third term on the right-hand side of (
4.40
), by (
4.23
) from Lemma
4.3
combined with (
4.32
) and Theorem
3.3
, there exists
C^{(3)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f}(T)>0
such that for any
0\leq T_{1}\leq T_{2}\leq T
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{1}\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\left\langle M^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}\right\rangle(T_{2})-\left\langle M^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}\right\rangle(T_{1})\right]

(4.44)

\displaystyle\leq C^{(3)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f}(T)\Bigg(\frac{1}{NL_{N}^{2}}\,\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\int_{T_{1}}^{T_{2}}p^{N}(T_{2}-{\tau},y-x_{1})^{2}\,d{\tau}

\displaystyle+\frac{m_{N}}{2NL_{N}^{4}}\,\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\int_{T_{1}}^{T_{2}}\left(\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T_{2}-{\tau},y-L_{N}^{-1}-x_{1})^{2}+\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T_{2}-{\tau},y-x_{1})^{2}\right)d{\tau}\Bigg).

We will bound each term on the right-hand side of (
4.44
) separately. For the first term, by (
4.42
) and the fact that
p^{N}(t,z)\leq L_{N}

\forall\,t\geq 0,\,z\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, we can write

\frac{1}{NL_{N}^{2}}\,\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\int_{T_{1}}^{T_{2}}p^{N}(T_{2}-{\tau},y-x_{1})^{2}\,d{\tau}\leq\frac{T_{2}-T_{1}}{N}.

(4.45)

For the second term on the right-hand side of (
4.44
), observe that by Fubini’s theorem, and then by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle\frac{m_{N}}{2NL_{N}^{4}}\,\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\int_{T_{1}}^{T_{2}}\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T_{2}-{\tau},y-x_{1})^{2}\,d{\tau}

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{N}}{2NL_{N}^{4}}\int_{0}^{T_{2}}\,\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\mathds{1}_{\{T_{1}\leq{\tau}\leq T_{2}\}}\left|\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T_{2}-{\tau},y-x_{1})\right|^{3/2}\cdot\left|\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T_{2}-{\tau},y-x_{1})\right|^{1/2}\,d{\tau}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{m_{N}}{2NL_{N}^{4}}\int_{0}^{T_{2}}\,\Bigg(\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\mathds{1}_{\{T_{1}\leq{\tau}\leq T_{2}\}}\left|\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T_{2}-{\tau},y-x_{1})\right|^{3}\Bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\cdot\Bigg(\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\left|\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T_{2}-{\tau},y-x_{1})\right|\Bigg)^{1/2}\,d{\tau}.

Using the definition of
\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}
in (
4.11
), the fact that
p^{N}(t,z)\leq L_{N}

\forall\,t\geq 0,\,z\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, and the elementary inequality
(|a|+|b|)^{3}\leq 4(|a|^{3}+|b|^{3})\;\forall\,a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, and then by (
4.42
), it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{m_{N}}{2NL_{N}^{4}}\,\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\int_{T_{1}}^{T_{2}}\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T_{2}-{\tau},y-x_{1})^{2}\,d{\tau}

(4.46)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{m_{N}}{2NL_{N}^{4}}\int_{0}^{T_{2}}\,\Bigg(4L_{N}^{5}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\mathds{1}_{\{T_{1}\leq{\tau}\leq T_{2}\}}\Big(p^{N}(T_{2}-{\tau},y-x_{1})+p^{N}(T_{2}-{\tau},y+L_{N}^{-1}-x_{1})\Big)\Bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\cdot\Bigg(\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\left|\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T_{2}-{\tau},y-x_{1})\right|\Bigg)^{1/2}\,d{\tau}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}m_{N}}{NL_{N}^{3/2}}\int_{0}^{T_{2}}\mathds{1}_{\{T_{1}\leq{\tau}\leq T_{2}\}}\Bigg(\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\left|\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T_{2}-{\tau},y-x_{1})\right|\Bigg)^{1/2}\,d{\tau}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}m_{N}}{NL_{N}^{3/2}}\Bigg(\int_{0}^{T_{2}}\mathds{1}_{\{T_{1}\leq{\tau}\leq T_{2}\}}\,d{\tau}\Bigg)^{1/2}\cdot\Bigg(\int_{0}^{T_{2}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\left|\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T_{2}-{\tau},y-x_{1})\right|\,d{\tau}\Bigg)^{1/2},

where the last inequality follows by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Therefore, by (
0.A.7
) from Lemma
0.A.3
in the appendix, we can write

\frac{m_{N}}{2NL_{N}^{4}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{T_{1}}^{T_{2}}\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(T_{2}-{\tau},y-x_{1})^{2}\,d{\tau}\lesssim_{m,T}\frac{m_{N}}{NL_{N}^{3/2}}(T_{2}-T_{1})^{1/2}L_{N}^{1/2}.

Since the same bound holds when
y
is replaced by
y-L_{N}^{-1}
on the left-hand side, by (
4.44
) and (
4.45
) it follows that

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\left\langle M^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}\right\rangle(T_{2})-\left\langle M^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}\right\rangle(T_{1})\right]\lesssim_{m,T}C^{(3)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f}(T)\left(\frac{T_{2}-T_{1}}{N}+\frac{m_{N}}{NL_{N}}(T_{2}-T_{1})^{1/2}\right).

(4.47)

By Assumption
1
, we have
L_{N}={\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}(N)
and
m_{N}/L_{N}^{2}\rightarrow m\in(0,\infty)
as
N\rightarrow\infty
. Therefore, by (
4.47
), and since
\left(M^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(t)-M^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1})\right)_{t\in[T_{1},T_{2}]}
is a martingale, by applying Jensen’s and the BDG inequalities as in (
4.37
), we conclude that there exists
C^{(4)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f,m}(T)>0
such that for any
0\leq T_{1}\leq T_{2}\leq T
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{1}\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\left|M^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{2})-M^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(T_{1})\right|\right]\leq C^{(4)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f,m}(T)(T_{2}-T_{1})^{1/4}.

(4.48)

It remains to bound the expectation of the fourth term on the right-hand side of (
4.40
). Since the processes
(M^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(t))_{t\in[0,T_{2}]}
and
(M^{N,T_{1},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(t))_{t\in[0,T_{1}]}
are martingales with respect to the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}^{{{\eta}}^{N}}_{t+}\}_{t\geq 0}
, the càdlàg process
(M^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(t)-M^{N,T_{1},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}(t))_{t\in[0,T_{1}]}
is also a martingale with respect to the same filtration. By the same argument as for (
4.34
), i.e. by Lemma
4.2
(vi) and the same argument as in the proof of (
4.23
) in Lemma
4.3
, with
g^{N,T,x}_{\mathcal{I}}
replaced by
g^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}-g^{N,T_{1},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\left\langle M^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}-M^{N,T_{1},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}\right\rangle(T_{1})\right]

(4.49)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{NL_{N}^{2}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{T_{1}}\left(p^{N}\left(T_{2}-t,y-x_{1}\right)-p^{N}\left(T_{1}-t,y-x_{1}\right)\right)^{2}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[F_{k}^{+}(u^{N}(t-,y))\right]\,dt

\displaystyle+\frac{m_{N}}{2NL_{N}^{4}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{T_{1}}\Big({\delta}^{N,T_{2},T_{1},x_{1},x_{1}}_{-}(t,y)+{\delta}^{N,T_{2},T_{1},x_{1},x_{1}}_{+}(t,y)\Big)\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[u^{N}_{k}(t-,y)\right]\,dt,

where the terms
{\delta}^{N,T_{2},T_{1},x_{1},x_{1}}_{-}(t,y)
and
{\delta}^{N,T_{2},T_{1},x_{1},x_{1}}_{+}(t,y)
are defined in (
4.17
) and (
4.18
) respectively. Using (
4.11
) and that
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}\quad\forall\,a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, for
t\in[0,T_{1}]
and
y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\displaystyle{\delta}^{N,T_{2},T_{1},x_{1},x_{1}}_{-}(t,y)

\displaystyle\leq 2L_{N}^{2}\Big(p^{N}(T_{1}-t,y-x_{1})-p^{N}(T_{2}-t,y-x_{1})\Big)^{2}

(4.50)

\displaystyle+2L_{N}^{2}\Big(p^{N}(T_{1}-t,y-L_{N}^{-1}-x_{1})-p^{N}(T_{2}-t,y-L_{N}^{-1}-x_{1})\Big)^{2},

\displaystyle\textrm{and }{\delta}^{N,T_{2},T_{1},x_{1},x_{1}}_{+}(t,y)

\displaystyle\leq 2L_{N}^{2}\Big(p^{N}(T_{1}-t,y-x_{1})-p^{N}(T_{2}-t,y-x_{1})\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle+2L_{N}^{2}\Big(p^{N}(T_{1}-t,y+L_{N}^{-1}-x_{1})-p^{N}(T_{2}-t,y+L_{N}^{-1}-x_{1})\Big)^{2}.

Hence, substituting into (
4.49
), using (
4.32
) and the random walk estimate (
0.A.9
) from Lemma
0.A.3
in the appendix to bound the first term on the right-hand side, and using Theorem
3.3
, (
4.50
) and (
0.A.9
) again to bound the second term, there exists
C^{(5)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f,m}(T)>0
such that for any
0\leq T_{1}\leq T_{2}\leq T
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{1}\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\left\langle M^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}-M^{N,T_{1},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}\right\rangle(T_{1})\right]

\displaystyle\leq C^{(5)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f,m}(T)\left(\frac{({T_{2}-T_{1}})^{1/2}}{NL_{N}}+\frac{m_{N}}{NL_{N}}({T_{2}-T_{1}})^{1/2}\right).

Hence, by applying Jensen’s and the BDG inequalities as in (
4.37
), and since
m_{N}/L_{N}^{2}\rightarrow m\in(0,\infty)
and
L_{N}={\mathord{\hbox{\char 2\relax}}}(N)
as
N\rightarrow\infty
by Assumption
1
, there exists
C^{(6)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f,m}(T)>0
such that for any
0\leq T_{1}\leq T_{2}\leq T
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{1}\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\left|(M^{N,T_{2},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}}-M^{N,T_{1},x_{1}}_{\mathcal{I}})(T_{1})\right|\right]\leq C^{(6)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f,m}(T)\left(T_{2}-T_{1}\right)^{1/4}.

(4.51)

Combining (
4.40
), (
4.41
), (
4.43
), (
4.48
) and (
4.51
), our claim (
4.39
) holds. Therefore, combining our bounds on space increments (
4.29
) and time increments (
4.39
) with (
4.28
), the proof is complete.
∎

By combining standard random walk estimates with Lemma
4.4
, we can now derive an integral form of equicontinuity for
\Big((U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,x))_{t\in[0,T],\,x\in\mathbb{R}}\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
that will be useful for the characterisation of the limiting process in Section
6.1
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Under the conditions of Lemma
4.4
, for any
T>0
, there exists
C_{T}>0
such that for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
{\gamma}_{1}\in(-1,1)
,
{\gamma}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\left|U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}\left(t,x\right)-U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}\left(t+{\gamma}_{1},x+{\gamma}_{2}\right)\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{t+{\gamma}_{1}\in[0,T]\}}\right|\,dt\Bigg]\leq C_{T}\left(\left|{\gamma}_{1}\right|^{1/4}+\left|{\gamma}_{2}\right|^{1/2}+|{\gamma}_{2}|\right).

Proof.

The proof adapts ideas of Durrett and Fan from
[
25
, Lemma 5]
. Recall from (
4.1
) and (
2.8
) that
U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,x)
is defined via linear interpolation in space. We first observe that for
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
{\gamma}_{1}\in(-1,1)
,
{\gamma}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we have almost surely

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\left|U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}\left(t,x\right)-U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}\left(t+{\gamma}_{1},x+{\gamma}_{2}\right)\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{t+{\gamma}_{1}\in[0,T]\}}\right|\,dt

(4.52)

\displaystyle=\int_{0\vee(-{\gamma}_{1})}^{T\wedge(T-{\gamma}_{1})}\left|U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}\left(t,x\right)-U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}\left(t+{\gamma}_{1},x+{\gamma}_{2}\right)\right|\,dt+\int_{0}^{0\vee(-{\gamma}_{1})}U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}\left(t,x\right)\,dt

\displaystyle+\int_{T\wedge(T-{\gamma}_{1})}^{T}U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}\left(t,x\right)\,dt.

We will bound the expectation of the terms on the right-hand side of (
4.52
) separately. For the second and third terms, by (
4.1
) and (
2.8
), and then by Theorem
3.3
, there exists
C^{(1)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f}(T)>0
such that for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
{\gamma}_{1}\in(-1,1)
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{0\vee(-{\gamma}_{1})}U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}\left(t,x\right)\,dt+\int_{T\wedge(T-{\gamma}_{1})}^{T}U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}\left(t,x\right)\,dt\Bigg]

(4.53)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{0\vee(-{\gamma}_{1})}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}\left(t,x\right)\right]\,dt+\int_{T\wedge(T-{\gamma}_{1})}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}\left(t,x\right)\right]\,dt

\displaystyle\leq C^{(1)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f}(T)|{\gamma}_{1}|.

It remains to bound the expectation of the first term on the right-hand side of (
4.52
). For
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
{\gamma}_{1}\in(-1,1)
,
t\in[0\vee(-{\gamma}_{1}),T\wedge(T-{\gamma}_{1})]
, and
y_{1},y_{2}\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, by (
4.20
) and the triangle inequality we have

\displaystyle\left|U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,y_{1})-U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t+{\gamma}_{1},y_{2})\right|

(4.54)

\displaystyle\leq\left|\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,y_{1})-\widehat{U}^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t+{\gamma}_{1},y_{2})\right|+\left|P^{N}_{t}U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(0,\cdot)(y_{1})-P^{N}_{t+{\gamma}_{1}}U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(0,\cdot)(y_{2})\right|.

For the second term on the right-hand side of (
4.54
), recall from after (
4.2
) that
\{P^{N}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is the semigroup associated to the simple symmetric random walk on
L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
with total jump rate
m_{N}
, and recall the definition of
p^{N}
in (
4.3
). Since
\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}
is given by (
2.1
), where
f
satisfies Assumption
4
, we have
\|{\eta}^{N}(y)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\leq N\|f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}

\forall\,y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, and so

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Bigg[\int_{0\vee(-{\gamma}_{1})}^{T\wedge(T-{\gamma}_{1})}\left|P^{N}_{t}U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(0,\cdot)(y_{1})-P^{N}_{t+{\gamma}_{1}}U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(0,\cdot)(y_{2})\right|\,dt\Bigg]

(4.55)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0\vee(-{\gamma}_{1})}^{T\wedge(T-{\gamma}_{1})}\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\|f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\left|p^{N}(t,y-y_{1})-p^{N}(t+{\gamma}_{1},y-y_{2})\right|\,dt

\displaystyle\lesssim_{T,m}\|f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\left(|y_{1}-y_{2}|+|{\gamma}_{1}|^{1/2}\right),

where the second inequality follows from (
0.A.6
) and (
0.A.7
) in Lemma
0.A.3
in the appendix. By (
4.52
)-(
4.55
) and Lemma
4.4
, it follows that for
T>0
, there exists
C^{(1)}_{T}>0
such that for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
{\gamma}_{1}\in(-1,1)
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\left|U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}\left(t,y_{1}\right)-U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}\left(t+{\gamma}_{1},y_{2}\right)\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{t+{\gamma}_{1}\in[0,T]\}}\right|\,dt\Bigg]

(4.56)

\displaystyle\leq C^{(1)}_{T}\left(\left|{\gamma}_{1}\right|^{1/4}+\left|y_{1}-y_{2}\right|^{1/2}+|y_{1}-y_{2}|\right).

From now on in the proof, for
y\in\mathbb{R}
we write
\lfloor y\rfloor_{N}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor L_{N}y\rfloor\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
. Take
{\gamma}_{1}\in(-1,1)
and
{\gamma}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
; for
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}

,

\displaystyle\left|U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,x)-U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t+{\gamma}_{1},x+{\gamma}_{2})\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{t+{\gamma}_{1}\in[0,T]\}}\right|

(4.57)

\displaystyle\leq\left|U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,x)-U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,x+{\gamma}_{2})\right|+\left|U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,x+{\gamma}_{2})-U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t+{\gamma}_{1},x+{\gamma}_{2})\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{t+{\gamma}_{1}\in[0,T]\}}\right|.

We will control the terms on the right-hand side of (
4.57
) separately. For the first term, we consider the cases
|{\gamma}_{2}|<L_{N}^{-1}
and
|{\gamma}_{2}|\geq L_{N}^{-1}
separately.

First, suppose
|{\gamma}_{2}|<L_{N}^{-1}
. Then we must have
x+{\gamma}_{2}\in\left[\lfloor x\rfloor_{N}-L_{N}^{-1},\lfloor x\rfloor_{N}+2L_{N}^{-1}\right]
, and so by the mean value theorem,

\displaystyle\left|U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,x)-U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,x+{\gamma}_{2})\right|

\displaystyle\leq L_{N}|{\gamma}_{2}|\max_{y\in\{\lfloor x\rfloor_{N}-L_{N}^{-1},\lfloor x\rfloor_{N},\lfloor x\rfloor_{N}+L_{N}^{-1}\}}\;\left|U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,y+L_{N}^{-1})-U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq L_{N}|{\gamma}_{2}|\sum_{y\in\{\lfloor x\rfloor_{N}-L_{N}^{-1},\lfloor x\rfloor_{N},\lfloor x\rfloor_{N}+L_{N}^{-1}\}}\left|U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,y+L_{N}^{-1})-U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,y)\right|.

By (
4.56
), it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\left|U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}\left(t,x\right)-U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}\left(t,x+{\gamma}_{2}\right)\right|\,dt\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq 3C^{(1)}_{T}L_{N}|{\gamma}_{2}|(L_{N}^{-1/2}+L_{N}^{-1})

(4.58)

\displaystyle\leq 3C^{(1)}_{T}|{\gamma}_{2}|^{1/2}(1+L_{N}^{-1/2}),

where the second inequality follows since
|{\gamma}_{2}|<L_{N}^{-1}
.

Now we consider the case
|{\gamma}_{2}|\geq L_{N}^{-1}
. By the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\left|U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,x)-U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,x+{\gamma}_{2})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,x)-U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,\lfloor x\rfloor_{N})\right|+\left|U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,x+{\gamma}_{2})-U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,\lfloor x+{\gamma}_{2}\rfloor_{N})\right|

(4.59)

\displaystyle+\left|U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,\lfloor x\rfloor_{N})-U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,\lfloor x+{\gamma}_{2}\rfloor_{N})\right|.

By applying (
4.59
) to the integrand and using (
4.58
) to bound the first two terms and (
4.56
) to bound the last term with the observation that
|\lfloor x\rfloor_{N}-\lfloor x+{\gamma}_{2}\rfloor_{N}|\leq|{\gamma}_{2}|+L_{N}^{-1}\;\forall x\in\mathbb{R}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\left|U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}\left(t,x\right)-U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}\left(t,x+{\gamma}_{2}\right)\right|\,dt\Bigg]

(4.60)

\displaystyle\leq 6C^{(1)}_{T}L_{N}^{-1/2}(1+L_{N}^{-1/2})+C^{(1)}_{T}\left((|{\gamma}_{2}|+L_{N}^{-1})^{1/2}+|{\gamma}_{2}|+L_{N}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\leq(8+\sqrt{2})C^{(1)}_{T}(1+L_{N}^{-1/2})(|{\gamma}_{2}|^{1/2}+|{\gamma}_{2}|),

where the second inequality follows since
|{\gamma}_{2}|\geq L_{N}^{-1}
.

It remains to control the second term on the right-hand side of (
4.57
). Since
U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,\cdot)
and
U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t+{\gamma}_{1},\cdot)
are defined by linear interpolation in space, we can write

\displaystyle\left|U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,x+{\gamma}_{2})-U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t+{\gamma}_{1},x+{\gamma}_{2})\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{t+{\gamma}_{1}\in[0,T]\}}\right|

\displaystyle=\Big|L_{N}(x+{\gamma}_{2}-\lfloor x+{\gamma}_{2}\rfloor_{N})\Big(U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,\lfloor x+{\gamma}_{2}\rfloor_{N}+L_{N}^{-1})

\displaystyle-U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t+{\gamma}_{1},\lfloor x+{\gamma}_{2}\rfloor_{N}+L_{N}^{-1})\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{t+{\gamma}_{1}\in[0,T]\}}\Big)

\displaystyle+L_{N}(\lfloor x+{\gamma}_{2}\rfloor_{N}+L_{N}^{-1}-(x+{\gamma}_{2}))\Big(U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,\lfloor x+{\gamma}_{2}\rfloor_{N})

\displaystyle-U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t+{\gamma}_{1},\lfloor x+{\gamma}_{2}\rfloor_{N})\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{t+{\gamma}_{1}\in[0,T]\}}\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{y\in\{\lfloor x+{\gamma}_{2}\rfloor_{N},\lfloor x+{\gamma}_{2}\rfloor_{N}+L_{N}^{-1}\}}\left|U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t,y)-U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(t+{\gamma}_{1},y)\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{t+{\gamma}_{1}\in[0,T]\}}\right|.

Therefore, by (
4.56
),

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\left|U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}\left(t,x+{\gamma}_{2}\right)-U^{N}_{\mathcal{I}}\left(t+{\gamma}_{1},x+{\gamma}_{2}\right)\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{t+{\gamma}_{1}\in[0,T]\}}\right|\,dt\Bigg]\leq 2C^{(1)}_{T}\left|{\gamma}_{1}\right|^{1/4}.

(4.61)

Recall from Assumption
1
that
L_{N}\rightarrow\infty
as
N\rightarrow\infty
. Therefore, by combining (
4.57
) with (
4.58
) and (
4.61
) in the case
|{\gamma}_{2}|<L_{N}^{-1}
and with (
4.60
) and (
4.61
) in the case
|{\gamma}_{2}|\geq L_{N}^{-1}
, the result follows.
∎

Since the action of the reaction part of the generator of our process involves polynomials in the local number of particles (recall (
2
)), we will need a weak equicontinuity property for moments of the local density. This will be our next result.

Lemma 4.6
.

Under the conditions of Lemma
4.4
, for
T\geq 0
and
r\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
C_{r,T}>0
such that for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\left|u_{k}^{{{N}}}(t-,x_{1})\|u^{{{N}}}(t-,x_{1})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}^{r}-u_{k}^{{{N}}}(t-,x_{2})\|u^{{{N}}}(t-,x_{2})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}^{r}\right|\right]\,dt

\displaystyle\leq C_{r,T}\Big(|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1/4}+|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1/2}\Big).

Proof.

Observe that for
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\left|u_{k}^{{{N}}}(t-,x_{1})\|u^{{{N}}}(t-,x_{1})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}^{r}-u_{k}^{{{N}}}(t-,x_{2})\|u^{{{N}}}(t-,x_{2})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}^{r}\right|

(4.62)

\displaystyle=\Big|\|u^{{{N}}}(t-,x_{2})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}^{r}\left(u^{N}_{k}(t-,x_{1})-u^{N}_{k}(t-,x_{2})\right)

\displaystyle+u^{N}_{k}(t-,x_{1})\left(\|u^{{{N}}}(t-,x_{1})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}^{r}-\|u^{{{N}}}(t-,x_{2})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}^{r}\right)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\|u^{{{N}}}(t-,x_{2})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}^{r}\left|u^{N}_{k}(t-,x_{1})-u^{N}_{k}(t-,x_{2})\right|
