N\in\mathbb{N}
, we can bound the expectation on the right-hand side of (
6.21
) by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,\frac{1}{1+|x|^{2}}\left|u^{N}_{k}(t,x)-(P_{t}f_{k})(x)-\int_{0}^{t}\Big(P_{t-{\tau}}F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau},\cdot))\Big)(x)\,d{\tau}\right|\,dx\,dt\Bigg]

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{L_{N}(1+|x|^{2})}\int_{0}^{1}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[|u^{N}_{k}(t,x)-u^{N}_{k}(t,x+L_{N}^{-1})|\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Bigg[\Bigg|u^{N}_{k}(t,x)-(P_{t}f_{k})(x+hL_{N}^{-1})

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{t}\left(P_{t-{\tau}}F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau},\cdot))\right)(x+hL_{N}^{-1})\,d{\tau}\Bigg|\Bigg]\Bigg)\,dh\,dt.

Therefore, by Lemma
4.3
, (
4.20
) and the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,\frac{1}{1+|x|^{2}}\left|u^{N}_{k}(t,x)-(P_{t}f_{k})(x)-\int_{0}^{t}\Big(P_{t-{\tau}}F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau},\cdot))\Big)(x)\,d{\tau}\right|\,dx\,dt\Bigg]

\displaystyle\;\lesssim\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{L_{N}(1+|x|^{2})}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[\Big|M^{N,t,x}_{\{k\}}(t)\Big|\Big]\,dt

\displaystyle\quad\;+\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{L_{N}(1+|x|^{2})}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[\Big|u^{N}_{k}(t,x)-u^{N}_{k}(t,x+L_{N}^{-1})\Big|\Big]\,dt

\displaystyle\quad\;+\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{L_{N}(1+|x|^{2})}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\left|A^{N,t,x}_{\{k\}}(t)-\int_{0}^{t}\Big(P_{t-{\tau}}F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau},\cdot))\Big)(x+hL_{N}^{-1})\,d{\tau}\right|\right]dh\,dt

\displaystyle\quad\;+\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{L_{N}(1+|x|^{2})}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\Big|(P_{t}f_{k})(x+hL_{N}^{-1})-(P^{N}_{t}u^{N}_{k}(0,\cdot))(x)\Big|\right]\,dh\,dt,

(6.23)

where for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
,
\Big(M^{N,t,x}_{\{k\}}({\tau})\Big)_{{\tau}\in[0,t]}
is a càdlàg square integrable martingale with
M^{N,t,x}_{\{k\}}(0)=0
whose predictable bracket process is given in (
4.23
), and
\Big(A^{N,t,x}_{\{k\}}({\tau})\Big)_{{\tau}\in[0,t]}
is a finite variation process defined in (
4.22
).
We now bound each of the terms on the right-hand side of (
6.2
) separately.

Step (1): Bound on the norm of the martingale term

For the first term on the right-hand side of (
6.2
), by using Jensen’s inequality and the BDG inequality as in (
4.37
) and then applying (
4.23
), we have that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[\Big|M^{N,t,x}_{\{k\}}(t)\Big|\Big]^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{NL_{N}^{2}}\,\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\int_{0}^{t}p^{N}(t-{\tau},y-x)^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[F_{k}^{+}(u^{N}({\tau}-,y))\Big]\,d{\tau}

\displaystyle+\frac{m_{N}}{2NL_{N}^{4}}\,\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(t-{\tau},y-L_{N}^{-1}-x)^{2}

\displaystyle+\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}(t-{\tau},y-x)^{2}\Big)\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[u^{N}_{k}({\tau}-,y)\Big]\,d{\tau}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{T}\frac{1}{NL_{N}^{2}}\,\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\int_{0}^{T}p^{N}({\tau},y-x)^{2}\,d{\tau}+\frac{m_{N}}{NL_{N}^{4}}\,\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\int_{0}^{T}\nabla_{L_{N}}p^{N}({\tau},y-x)^{2}d{\tau}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{T}N^{-1},

where for the second inequality we used estimate (
4.32
) and Theorem
3.3
, and for the last inequality, for the first term we used the fact that
p^{N}({\tau},y-x)\leq L_{N}
, (by (
4.3
)) and identity (
4.42
), and for the second term we used (
4.11
), the standard random walk estimate (
0.A.9
) from Lemma
0.A.3
in the appendix and the fact that, by Assumption
1
,
m_{N}/L_{N}^{2}\rightarrow m\in(0,\infty)
as
N\rightarrow\infty
. We then conclude that

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{L_{N}(1+|x|^{2})}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[\Big|M^{N,t,x}_{\{k\}}(t)\Big|\Big]\,dt=0.

(6.24)

Step (2): Bound on the error term arising from the linear interpolation

For the second term on the right-hand side of (
6.2
), applying Fubini’s theorem and then (
4.1
) and Lemma
4.5
yields

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{L_{N}(1+|x|^{2})}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[\Big|u^{N}_{k}(t,x)-u^{N}_{k}(t,x+L_{N}^{-1})\Big|\Big]\,dt\lesssim_{T}\sum_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{L_{N}^{3/2}(1+|x|^{2})}\lesssim L_{N}^{-1/2},

and so, since
L_{N}\rightarrow\infty
as
N\rightarrow\infty
by Assumption
1
,

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{L_{N}(1+|x|^{2})}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[\Big|u^{N}_{k}(t,x)-u^{N}_{k}(t,x+L_{N}^{-1})\Big|\Big]\,dt=0.

(6.25)

Step (3): Bound on the error arising from approximating
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
by
\{P^{N}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}

For the third term on the right-hand side of (
6.2
), we note that by the definition of the Brownian semigroup
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
before (
3.4
) and the definition of
p(t,x)
in (
3.3
), for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\in[0,T]
,
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and
h\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\Big(P_{t-{\tau}}F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau},\cdot))\Big)(x+hL_{N}^{-1})\,d{\tau}

(6.26)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\,\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{L_{N}}\int_{0}^{1}p\Big(t-{\tau},y-x+(h^{\prime}-h)L_{N}^{-1}\Big)F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau},y+h^{\prime}L_{N}^{-1}))\,dh^{\prime}\,d{\tau}.

Combining (
4.22
) and (
6.26
), and then applying the triangle inequality and Fubini’s theorem, we conclude that for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\in[0,T]
,
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and
h\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\left|A^{N,t,x}_{\{k\}}(t)-\int_{0}^{t}\Big(P_{t-{\tau}}F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau},\cdot))\Big)(x+hL_{N}^{-1})\,d{\tau}\right|\right]

(6.27)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{t}\left|p^{N}(t-{\tau},y-x)-p(t-{\tau},y-x)\right|\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[\Big|F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau}-,y))\Big|\Big]\,d{\tau}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{t}p(t-{\tau},y-x)\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[\Big|F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau}-,y))-F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau},y+h^{\prime}L_{N}^{-1}))\Big|\Big]\,dh^{\prime}d{\tau}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[\Big|F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau},y+h^{\prime}L_{N}^{-1}))\Big|\Big]

\displaystyle\cdot\left|p(t-{\tau},y-x)-p(t-{\tau},y-x+(h^{\prime}-h)L_{N}^{-1})\right|\,dh^{\prime}d{\tau}.

We now bound each of the terms on the right-hand side of (
6.27
) separately. For the first term, recall from (
2.12
) and (
4.10
) that
|F_{k}(u)|\leq F^{+}_{k}(u)\;\forall\,u\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
. Hence, by (
4.32
), there exists
C^{(1)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f}(T)>0
such that for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{t}\left|p^{N}(t-{\tau},y-x)-p(t-{\tau},y-x)\right|\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[\Big|F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau}-,y))\Big|\Big]\,d{\tau}

(6.28)

\displaystyle\leq C^{(1)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f}(T)\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{t}\left|p^{N}(t-{\tau},y-x)-p(t-{\tau},y-x)\right|\,d{\tau}

\displaystyle\leq C^{(1)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f}(T)\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{T}\left|p^{N}({\tau},y)-p({\tau},y)\right|\,d{\tau}.

Fix
{\varepsilon}\in(0,T)
, and let
(W(t))_{t\geq 0}
denote a Brownian motion run at rate
m
. Also, recall from before (
4.2
) that for
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
(X^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
denote a simple symmetric random walk on
L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
with total jump rate
m_{N}
. Suppose
N
is sufficiently large that
{\varepsilon}^{-1}>L_{N}^{-1}
. By symmetry, and then by the triangle inequality, identity (
4.42
) and since
p({\tau},\cdot)
is decreasing on
[0,\infty)
for
{\tau}>0
with
\int_{0}^{\infty}p({\tau},z)\,dz=1/2
, we can write

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{T}\left|p^{N}({\tau},y)-p({\tau},y)\right|\,d{\tau}

(6.29)

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\left|p^{N}({\tau},L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor yL_{N}\rfloor)-p({\tau},L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor yL_{N}\rfloor)\right|\,d{\tau}\,dy

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{0}^{{\varepsilon}^{-1}}\int_{\varepsilon}^{T}\left|p^{N}({\tau},L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor yL_{N}\rfloor)-p({\tau},L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor yL_{N}\rfloor)\right|\,d{\tau}\,dy+2\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{3}{2}+L_{N}^{-1}p({\tau},0)\right)\,d{\tau}

\displaystyle+2\int_{0}^{T}\Big(\mathbb{P}_{0}(X^{N}({\tau})\geq L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor{\varepsilon}^{-1}L_{N}\rfloor)+\mathbb{P}_{0}(W({\tau})\geq L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor{\varepsilon}^{-1}L_{N}\rfloor-L_{N}^{-1})\Big)\,d{\tau}.

By Lemma
0.A.4
in the appendix, which is a local central limit theorem for continuous-time random walks, and using dominated convergence and that
m_{N}/L_{N}^{2}\rightarrow m\in(0,\infty)
as
N\rightarrow\infty
by Assumption
1
, the first term on the right-hand side of (
6.29
) converges to
0
as
N\rightarrow\infty
.

For the third term on the right-hand side of (
6.29
), note that for
t\geq 0
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
, letting
(\mathcal{N}^{+}({\tau}))_{{\tau}\geq 0}
and
(\mathcal{N}^{-}({\tau}))_{{\tau}\geq 0}
denote independent Poisson processes with rate 1, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0}\left[X^{N}(t)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[L_{N}^{-2}(\mathcal{N}^{+}(m_{N}t/2)-\mathcal{N}^{-}(m_{N}t/2))^{2}\right]

(6.30)

\displaystyle=L_{N}^{-2}\left(2\left(\tfrac{1}{4}m_{N}^{2}t^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}m_{N}t\right)-2\cdot\tfrac{1}{4}m_{N}^{2}t^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=m_{N}L_{N}^{-2}t.

Therefore, using Markov’s inequality and that
m_{N}/L_{N}^{2}\rightarrow m\in(0,\infty)
and
L_{N}\to\infty
as
N\rightarrow\infty
by Assumption
1
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\Big(\mathbb{P}_{0}(X^{N}({\tau})\geq L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor{\varepsilon}^{-1}L_{N}\rfloor)+\mathbb{P}_{0}(W({\tau})\geq L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor{\varepsilon}^{-1}L_{N}\rfloor-L_{N}^{-1})\Big)\,d{\tau}

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\left(m_{N}L_{N}^{-2}{\tau}\cdot({\varepsilon}^{-1}/2)^{-2}+m{\tau}({\varepsilon}^{-1}/2)^{-2}\right)\,d{\tau}

\displaystyle=4{\varepsilon}^{2}mT^{2}.

For the second term on the right-hand side of (
6.29
), we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{3}{2}+L_{N}^{-1}p({\tau},0)\right)\,d{\tau}

\displaystyle=\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\left(\frac{3{\varepsilon}}{2}+L_{N}^{-1}\cdot\frac{2}{\sqrt{2{\pi}m}}{\varepsilon}^{1/2}\right)

\displaystyle=3{\varepsilon}/2.

Since
{\varepsilon}\in(0,T)
can be taken arbitrarily small, it now follows from (
6.29
) that

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{T}\left|p^{N}({\tau},y)-p({\tau},y)\right|\,d{\tau}=0.

(6.31)

For the second term on the right-hand side of (
6.27
), fix
r\in(1,2)
; then by using Hölder’s inequality with exponents
r
and
r/(r-1)
, and Jensen’s inequality, and then using (
4.32
) and the fact that
|F_{k}(u)|\leq F^{+}_{k}(u)\;\forall\,u\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
by (
2.12
) and (
4.10
), there exists
C^{(2)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f,r}(T)>0
such that for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{t}p(t-{\tau},y-x)\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[\Big|F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau}-,y))-F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau},y+h^{\prime}L_{N}^{-1}))\Big|\Big]\,dh^{\prime}\,d{\tau}

(6.32)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}e^{-\frac{(y-x)^{2}}{2mT}}\Bigg(\int_{0}^{T}(2{\pi}m{\tau})^{-r/2}\,d{\tau}\Bigg)^{1/r}

\displaystyle\cdot\Bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[\Big|F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau}-,y))-F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau},y+h^{\prime}L_{N}^{-1}))\Big|\Big]^{r/(r-1)}\,dh^{\prime}\,d{\tau}\Bigg)^{(r-1)/r}

\displaystyle\leq C^{(2)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f,r}(T)\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}e^{-\frac{(y-x)^{2}}{2mT}}

\displaystyle\cdot\Bigg(\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[\Big|F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau}-,y))-F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau},y+h^{\prime}L_{N}^{-1}))\Big|\Big]\,d{\tau}\,dh^{\prime}\Bigg)^{(r-1)/r}.

By the definition of
F_{k}
in (
2.12
) and by Lemma
4.6
, there exists
C^{(3)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f}(T)>0
such that for
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and
h^{\prime}\in[0,1]
,

\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[\Big|F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau}-,y))-F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau},y+h^{\prime}L_{N}^{-1}))\Big|\Big]\,d{\tau}\leq C^{(3)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f}(T)L_{N}^{-1/4}.

Therefore, substituting into (
6.32
), there exists
C^{(4)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f,r}(T)>0
such that for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{t}p(t-{\tau},y-x)\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[\Big|F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau}-,y))-F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau},y+h^{\prime}L_{N}^{-1}))\Big|\Big]\,dh^{\prime}\,d{\tau}

(6.33)

\displaystyle\leq C^{(4)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f,r}(T)L_{N}^{-(r-1)/(4r)},

and since we chose
r>1
, the right-hand side converges to
0
as
N\rightarrow\infty
by Assumption
1
.

For the third term on the right-hand side of (
6.27
), by (
4.32
) and the fact that
|F_{k}(u)|\leq F^{+}_{k}(u)\;\forall\,u\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
by (
2.12
) and (
4.10
), there exists
C^{(5)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f}(T)>0
such that for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\in[0,T]
,
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and
h\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[\Big|F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau},y+h^{\prime}L_{N}^{-1}))\Big|\Big]

(6.34)

\displaystyle\cdot\left|p(t-{\tau},y-x)-p(t-{\tau},y-x+(h^{\prime}-h)L_{N}^{-1})\right|\,dh^{\prime}d{\tau}

\displaystyle\leq C^{(5)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f}(T)\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{1}\left|p(t-{\tau},y-x)-p(t-{\tau},y-x+(h^{\prime}-h)L_{N}^{-1})\right|\,dh^{\prime}d{\tau}

\displaystyle\leq C^{(5)}_{q_{+},q_{-},f}(T)\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{1}\left|p({\tau},L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor L_{N}y\rfloor)-p({\tau},L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor L_{N}(y+(h^{\prime}-h)L_{N}^{-1})\rfloor)\right|\,dh^{\prime}\,dy\,d{\tau}.

Now fix
{\varepsilon}\in(0,T)
, then since
p({\tau},z)
is decreasing in
|z|
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}}p({\tau},z)\,dz=1
for
{\tau}>0
, we can write

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{1}\left|p({\tau},L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor L_{N}y\rfloor)-p({\tau},L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor L_{N}(y+(h^{\prime}-h)L_{N}^{-1})\rfloor)\right|\,dh^{\prime}\,dy\,d{\tau}

(6.35)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{{\varepsilon}}\left(2+2L_{N}^{-1}p({\tau},0)\right)\,d{\tau}

\displaystyle+\int_{\varepsilon}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{1}\left|p({\tau},L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor L_{N}y\rfloor)-p({\tau},L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor L_{N}(y+(h^{\prime}-h)L_{N}^{-1})\rfloor)\right|\,dh^{\prime}\,dy\,d{\tau}.

By dominated convergence, the second term on the right-hand side of (
6.35
) converges to
0
uniformly in
h\in[0,1]
as
N\rightarrow\infty
. Therefore, since
{\varepsilon}\in(0,T)
can be chosen arbitrarily small, we have

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\,\sup_{h\in[0,1]}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{1}\left|p({\tau},L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor L_{N}y\rfloor)-p({\tau},L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor L_{N}(y+(h^{\prime}-h)L_{N}^{-1})\rfloor)\right|\,dh^{\prime}\,dy\,d{\tau}=0.

(6.36)

Applying (
6.28
), (
6.31
), (
6.33
), (
6.34
) and (
6.36
) to (
6.27
), we conclude that

\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{L_{N}(1+|x|^{2})}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\left|A^{N,t,x}_{\{k\}}(t)-\int_{0}^{t}\Big(P_{t-{\tau}}F_{k}(u^{N}({\tau},\cdot))\Big)(x+hL_{N}^{-1})\,d{\tau}\right|\right]\,dh\,dt\rightarrow 0

(6.37)

as
N\rightarrow\infty
.

Step (4): Bound on the error term arising from approximating the initial condition

Finally, for the fourth term on the right-hand side of (
6.2
), note that for
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
,
h\in[0,1]
and
t\in(0,T]
, we can write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\Big|(P_{t}f_{k})(x+hL_{N}^{-1})-(P^{N}_{t}u^{N}_{k}(0,\cdot))(x)\Big|\right]

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(p(t,y)f_{k}(x+hL_{N}^{-1}-y)-p^{N}(t,L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor L_{N}y\rfloor)N^{-1}{\eta}^{N}_{k}(x-L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor L_{N}y\rfloor)\right)\,dy\right|.

Now fix
{\varepsilon}\in(0,T)
; then for
N\in\mathbb{N}
, since
\sup_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}{\eta}^{N}_{k}(y)\leq N\|f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}
by (
2.1
), by the triangle inequality we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{L_{N}(1+|x|^{2})}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\left[\Big|(P_{t}f_{k})(x+hL_{N}^{-1})-(P^{N}_{t}u^{N}_{k}(0,\cdot))(x)\Big|\right]\,dh\,dt

(6.38)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\sum_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{L_{N}(1+|x|^{2})}\|f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\,dt

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{L_{N}(1+|x|^{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus[-{\varepsilon}^{-1},{\varepsilon}^{-1}]}\|f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\left(p(t,y)+p^{N}(t,L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor L_{N}y\rfloor)\right)\,dy\,dt

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{\{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}:\,|x|\geq{\varepsilon}^{-1}\}}\frac{1}{L_{N}(1+|x|^{2})}\|f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\,dt

\displaystyle+\int_{{\varepsilon}}^{T}\sum_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}\cap[-{\varepsilon}^{-1},{\varepsilon}^{-1}]}\,\frac{1}{L_{N}(1+|x|^{2})}\int_{0}^{1}\Bigg|\int_{-{\varepsilon}^{-1}}^{{\varepsilon}^{-1}}\Big(p(t,y)f_{k}(x+hL_{N}^{-1}-y)

\displaystyle-p^{N}(t,L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor L_{N}y\rfloor)N^{-1}{\eta}^{N}_{k}(x-L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor L_{N}y\rfloor)\Big)\,dy\Bigg|\,dh\,dt.

By the definition of
{\eta}^{N}_{k}
in (
2.1
), and by Lemma
0.A.4
in the appendix, and since
\|f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}<\infty
and
f
is continuous almost everywhere by Assumption
4
, and
m_{N}/L_{N}^{2}\rightarrow m\in(0,\infty)
as
N\rightarrow\infty
by Assumption
1
, we can apply dominated convergence to see that the last term on the right-hand side of (
6.38
) converges to
0
as
N\rightarrow\infty
. For the second term on the right-hand side of (
6.38
), letting
(W(t))_{t\geq 0}
denote a Brownian motion run at speed
m
and
(X^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0}
a simple symmetric random walk on
L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
with total jump rate
m_{N}
, we can write

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus[-{\varepsilon}^{-1},{\varepsilon}^{-1}]}\left(p(t,y)+p^{N}(t,L_{N}^{-1}\lfloor L_{N}y\rfloor)\right)\,dy\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\;2\int_{0}^{T}\left(\mathbb{P}_{0}(W(t)\geq{\varepsilon}^{-1})+\mathbb{P}_{0}(X^{N}(t)\geq{\varepsilon}^{-1}/2)\right)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\;2\int_{0}^{T}\left({\varepsilon}^{2}mt+4{\varepsilon}^{2}m_{N}L_{N}^{-2}t\right)\,dt

\displaystyle=5{\varepsilon}^{2}mT^{2},

where in the second inequality we used (
6.30
) and Markov’s inequality. Therefore, taking
{\varepsilon}\in(0,T)
arbitrarily small, we conclude that

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\,\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{L_{N}(1+|x|^{2})}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{{\eta}}^{N}}\Big[\Big|(P_{t}f_{k})(x+hL_{N}^{-1})-(P^{N}_{t}u^{N}_{k}(0,\cdot))(x)\Big|\Big]\,dh\,dt=0.

(6.39)

Therefore, by applying (
6.2
) to (
6.21
), and then using (
6.24
), (
6.25
), (
6.37
) and (
6.39
), the proof is complete.
∎

We are now ready to prove Proposition
6.2
.

Proof of Proposition
6.2
.

The desired tightness follows from Lemma
6.6
, while condition (i) follows from Lemma
6.7
. To establish that
v
is a mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
), we first observe that since
v\in L_{4\deg q_{-}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
, since the heat kernel decays exponentially fast in space and by the definition of the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
in (
2.12
), for any
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
,

\left(P_{t-{\tau}}\|F(v({\tau},\cdot))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\right)(x)<\infty,

for
{\lambda}
-almost every
{\tau}\in[0,t]
. Moreover, by Lemma
6.8
, for
{\lambda}
-almost every
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
,

v(t,x)=(P_{t}f)(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\Big(P_{t-{\tau}}F(v({\tau},\cdot))\Big)(x)\,d{\tau}.

Then, it follows from Definition
3.5
that
v
is a mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
), which completes the proof.
∎

7
Uniqueness and regularity of mild solutions

Existence of
{\lambda}
-almost everywhere non-negative mild solutions to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) follows from Proposition
6.2
. In this section, we will establish uniqueness and regularity properties of mild solutions to the system of PDEs (
2.11
). For
T>0
and
r\in[1,\infty)
, recall the functional space
{L}_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
defined in (
6.2
). For
v^{(1)},v^{(2)}\in L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
, we call
v^{(2)}
a
version
of
v^{(1)}
in
L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
if

v^{(1)}(t,x)={v}^{(2)}(t,x)\textrm{ for }{\lambda}\textrm{-almost every }(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R},

(7.1)

where
{\lambda}
denotes the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Recall the definition of mild solutions to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) in Definition
3.5
. Our first main result of this section concerns uniqueness of mild solutions to the system of PDEs (
2.11
).

Proposition 7.1
.

Suppose
m>0
,
{\mu}\in[0,1]
,
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
satisfies Assumption
2
,
q_{+},q_{-}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
satisfy Assumption
3
, and
f\in L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
satisfies Assumption
4
. For
T>0
, let
v^{(1)},v^{(2)}\in L_{4\deg q_{-}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
be
{\lambda}
-almost everywhere non-negative mild solutions to the system of PDEs (
2.11
). Then
v^{(2)}
is a version of
v^{(1)}
in
L_{4\deg q_{-}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
, i.e.
v^{(1)}
and
v^{(2)}
satisfy (
7.1
).

We will also establish the following regularity property of mild solutions.

Proposition 7.2
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
7.1
, there exists a unique function
v=(v_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
such that
v\in\mathscr{C}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+})
and satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

v=(v_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is a global non-negative mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) in the sense of Definition
3.5
, and it satisfies (
3.5
) for all
(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
, i.e. for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
,

v_{k}(t,x)=(P_{t}f_{k})(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\Big(P_{t-{\tau}}F_{k}(v({\tau},\cdot))\Big)(x)\,d{\tau}.

(7.2)

(ii)

For all
T>0
,

v|_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}}\in L_{4\deg q_{-}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})\cap L_{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}).

(iii)

For all
T>0
,
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\;\|{v}(t,\cdot)\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}<\infty
.

Moreover, the unique function
v=(v_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
satisfies the following conditions:

(iv)

v_{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{1,2}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

(iiv)

The map
(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\ni(t,x)\mapsto\|v(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}
is in
\mathscr{C}^{1,2}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
.

We can now complete the proof of Theorem
2.1
, the functional law of large numbers for the spatial Muller’s ratchet.

Proof of Theorem
2.1
.

By Proposition
5.1
, the sequence
((u^{N}(t))_{t\geq 0})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight in the
J_{1}
-topology on
\mathscr{D}\left([0,\infty),(\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}},d)\right)
. Moreover, since Proposition
6.2
(i) implies that the finite-dimensional distributions of any subsequential limiting process
(u(t))_{t\geq 0}
satisfy (
6.3
), it follows from Proposition
6.2
(ii) and Proposition
7.1
that
(u(t))_{t\geq 0}
is unique.

We now characterise the limiting process
(u(t))_{t\geq 0}=((u_{k}(t))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{t\geq 0}
. We start by observing that by (
2.8
) and (
2.1
), and since by Assumption
4
(i)
f=(f_{k}):\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
is continuous
{\lambda}
-almost everywhere, the following limit holds almost surely for
{\lambda}
-almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}
:

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}u^{N}_{k}(0,x)=f_{k}(x).

Therefore, by (
2.1
), (
2.8
), Assumption
4
(ii) and dominated convergence, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
{\varphi}\in\mathscr{C}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
,

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\langle u^{N}_{k}(0),{\varphi}\rangle=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}u^{N}_{k}(0,x){\varphi}(x)\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{k}(x){\varphi}(x)\,dx.

(7.3)

Since
(u(t))_{t\geq 0}
is an element of
\mathscr{D}\left([0,\infty);(\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}},d)\right)
,
u
is continuous at
t=0
, and therefore, by (
7.3
) and standard properties of the Skorokhod topology (see e.g.
[
29
, Proposition 3.5.2]
), for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the measure
u_{k}(0)\in\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure and has density given by
f_{k}
. We now prove that for all
t>0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
u_{k}(t)
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, and characterise the density. Let
v=(v_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
be the unique map satisfying the conditions (i)-(v) from Proposition
7.2
. By Proposition
6.2
and Proposition
7.1
, we have that for all
t>0
,
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
{\varphi}\in\mathscr{C}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
,

\langle u_{k}(t),{\varphi}\rangle=\lim_{t^{\prime}\downarrow t}\frac{1}{t^{\prime}-t}\int_{t}^{t^{\prime}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}v_{k}({\tau},x){\varphi}(x)\,dx\,d{\tau}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}v_{k}(t,x){\varphi}(x)\,dx,

where the second equality holds since by Proposition
7.2
(iv),
v_{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{1,2}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
. Hence, for all
t>0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
u_{k}(t)
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, with density given by
v_{k}(t,\cdot)
. In particular, the limiting process
(u(t))_{t\geq 0}=((u_{k}(t))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{t\geq 0}
satisfies condition (i) from Theorem
2.1
. Moreover, by conditions (i) and (iv) from Proposition
7.2
,
(u(t))_{t\geq 0}=((u_{k}(t))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{t\geq 0}
also satisfies conditions (ii) and (iii) from Theorem
2.1
. The fact that
(u(t))_{t\geq 0}=((u_{k}(t))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{t\geq 0}
is the unique element in
\mathscr{D}([0,\infty),\mathscr{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\mathbb{N}_{0}})
satisfying Theorem
2.1
(i)-(iii) follows from Proposition
7.1
. Let
(u_{k}(t,x))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,t\in[0,\infty),\,x\in\mathbb{R}}
denote the density process associated to
(u(t))_{t\geq 0}=((u_{k}(t))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}})_{t\geq 0}
. Since for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
u_{k}(0)
is a Radon measure with density
f_{k}
with respect to the Lebesgue measure, the associated density process
(u_{k}(t,x))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,t\in[0,\infty),\,x\in\mathbb{R}}
satisfies Lemma
3.7
(i). Moreover, since
u_{k}(t)
has density given by
v_{k}(t,\cdot)
, we conclude by Proposition
7.2
(i) and (ii) that
(u_{k}(t,x))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,t\in[0,\infty),\,x\in\mathbb{R}}
satisfies both conditions (ii) and (iii) from Lemma
3.7
. Therefore, it follows from Lemma
3.7
that
(u_{k}(t,x))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,t\in[0,\infty),\,x\in\mathbb{R}}
is a weak solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) in the sense of Definition
3.4
, which completes the proof.
∎

In the remainder of this section, we will prove Propositions
7.1
and
7.2
.

7.1
Proof of Proposition
7.1

Our first step towards the proof of Proposition
7.1
will be to prove that any non-negative mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) must be essentially bounded. Although we do not follow any particular reference, similar strategies to establish regularity properties of weak or mild solutions to partial differential equations are common in the PDE literature (e.g.
[
18
, Proof of Theorem 1]
and
[
63
, Section 4.2]
).

Lemma 7.3
.

Suppose the assumptions of Proposition
7.1
hold. For
T>0
, let
v\in L_{4\deg q_{-}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
be a
{\lambda}
-almost everywhere non-negative mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
). Then
v\in L_{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
. Moreover, there exists a version
\tilde{v}\in L_{4\deg q_{-}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
of
v
satisfying the following conditions:

(i)

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and any
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
,

\tilde{v}_{k}(t,x)=(P_{t}f_{k})(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\Big(P_{t-{\tau}}F_{k}(\tilde{v}({\tau},\cdot))\Big)(x)\,d{\tau}.

(7.4)

(ii)

\tilde{v}\in\mathscr{C}((0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+})
.

(iii)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\;\|\tilde{v}(t,\cdot)\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}<\infty.

Proof.

By the definition of the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
in (
2.12
), the fact that
s_{k}\leq 1
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
by Assumption
2
(i) and (iii), and using that
q_{+}
and
q_{-}
are non-negative by Assumption
3
, we conclude that for all
z\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
,

\|F(z)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\leq\|z\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}(q_{+}(\|z\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})+q_{-}(\|z\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})),

(7.5)

and that for all
J\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\sum_{k=0}^{J}F_{k}(z)\leq\sum_{k=0}^{J}z_{k}(q_{+}(\|z\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})-q_{-}(\|z\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}))\leq\sup_{U\geq 0}\Big(U(q_{+}(U)-q_{-}(U))\Big).

(7.6)

Since
v
is a mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
), by Definition
3.5
(ii) we have that for
{\lambda}
-almost every
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
,

v_{k}(t,x)=(P_{t}f_{k})(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\Big(P_{t-{\tau}}F_{k}(v({\tau},\cdot))\Big)(x)\,d{\tau}\quad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

where
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is the semigroup of Brownian motion run at speed
m
defined before (
3.3
). Hence, by using Assumption
4
(ii) and (
7.6
), we conclude that for
{\lambda}
-almost every
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
, the following estimate holds for every
J\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{J}v_{k}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\left(P_{t}\sum_{k=0}^{J}f_{k}\right)(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\left(P_{t-{\tau}}\sum_{k=0}^{J}F_{k}(v({\tau},\cdot))\right)(x)\,d{\tau}

(7.7)

\displaystyle\leq\|f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}+T\sup_{U\geq 0}\Big(U(q_{+}(U)-q_{-}(U))\Big).

Moreover, since
v
is
{\lambda}
-almost everywhere non-negative, we conclude that for
{\lambda}
-almost every
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
,
\lim_{J\rightarrow\infty}\;\sum_{k=0}^{J}v_{k}(t,x)=\|v(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}
,
and therefore, by taking the limit as
J\rightarrow\infty
on the left-hand side of (
7.7
), we conclude that for
{\lambda}
-almost every
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\|{v}(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\leq\|f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}+T\sup_{U\geq 0}\Big(U(q_{+}(U)-q_{-}(U))\Big).

(7.8)

Since, by Assumption
3
,
q_{+}
and
q_{-}
are non-negative polynomials with
0\leq\deg q_{+}<\deg q_{-}
, the term on the right-hand side of (
7.8
) is finite, and therefore
v\in L_{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
.

Now, let
\tilde{v}=(\tilde{v}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
be given by, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and any
(t,x)\in[0,T]
,

\tilde{v}_{k}(t,x)=(P_{t}f_{k})(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\Big(P_{t-{\tau}}F_{k}({v}({\tau},\cdot))\Big)(x)\,d{\tau}.

(7.9)

Then, by Definition
3.5
(ii),
\tilde{v}
is a version of
v
, and so
\tilde{v}=(\tilde{v}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is equal to
v=(v_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}

{\lambda}
-almost everywhere in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
. Hence, identity (
7.9
) implies that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\tilde{v}_{k}(t,x)

\displaystyle=(P_{t}f_{k})(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}p(t-{\tau},x-y)F_{k}(v({\tau},y))\,dy\,d{\tau}

\displaystyle=(P_{t}f_{k})(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}p(t-{\tau},x-y)F_{k}(\tilde{v}({\tau},y))\,dy\,d{\tau},

where
p
is the Gaussian kernel function defined in (
3.3
). Hence, we conclude that
\tilde{v}=(\tilde{v}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
satisfies (
7.4
) for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, since
v\in L_{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
and
\tilde{v}
is a version of
v
, we conclude that
\tilde{v}\in L_{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
. Hence, by (
7.5
), the map

[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\ni(t,x)\mapsto F(\tilde{v}(t,x))\textrm{ is in }L_{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}).

(7.10)

Combining (
7.10
) and (
7.4
), and then applying the smoothing properties of the Gaussian kernel (see Lemma
0.A.7
in the appendix), we conclude that
\tilde{v}=(\tilde{v}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in\mathscr{C}((0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
. By continuity and the fact that
\tilde{v}=(\tilde{v}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is almost everywhere non-negative, we conclude that
\tilde{v}_{k}(t,x)\geq 0\;\forall\,(k,t,x)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\times(0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
, i.e. that condition (ii) of the lemma holds.

Moreover, condition (iii) of the lemma follows from the fact that
\tilde{v}\in L_{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
and that
\tilde{v}\in\mathscr{C}((0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
, together with the observation that by (
7.4
),
v(0,\cdot)=f(\cdot)
, and that
f\in L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
by Assumption
4
(ii). This completes the proof.
∎

Next, we establish a local Lipschitz condition for the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, which, together with Lemma
7.3
, will enable us to apply Grönwall’s inequality in the proof of Proposition
7.1
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Suppose the assumptions of Proposition
7.1
hold. Then the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
defined in (
2.12
) is locally Lipschitz continuous on
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
, i.e.
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is Lipschitz continuous on bounded subsets of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
.

Proof.

Take
A>0
, and then take
z,\tilde{z}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
such that
\|z\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\vee\|\tilde{z}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\leq A
. Recalling the definition of
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
in (
2.12
), and then using the triangle inequality and the fact that by Assumption
2
,
s_{k}\leq 1\;\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\|F(z)-F(\tilde{z})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}

(7.11)

\displaystyle\leq\|z-\tilde{z}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big(q_{+}(\|z\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})+q_{-}(\|z\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})\Big)

\displaystyle+\|\tilde{z}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big|q_{+}(\|z\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})-q_{+}(\|\tilde{z}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})\Big|+\|\tilde{z}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big|q_{-}(\|z\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})-q_{-}(\|\tilde{z}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{A}\|z-\tilde{z}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}+\Big|q_{+}(\|z\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})-q_{+}(\|\tilde{z}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})\Big|+\Big|q_{-}(\|z\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})-q_{-}(\|\tilde{z}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})\Big|,

where for the last inequality we used the fact that we chose
z,\tilde{z}
such that
\|z\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\vee\|\tilde{z}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\leq A
. Observe that by the triangle inequality and the fact that
q_{+}
and
q_{-}
are polynomials, there exist polynomials
Q_{+},Q_{-}:[0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
with non-negative coefficients such that for any
z,\tilde{z}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
,

\displaystyle\Big|q_{+}(\|z\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})-q_{+}(\|\tilde{z}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})\Big|\leq\|z-\tilde{z}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}Q_{+}\left(\|z\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}},\|\tilde{z}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\right),

(7.12)

\displaystyle\textrm{ and }

\displaystyle\Big|q_{-}(\|z\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})-q_{-}(\|\tilde{z}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})\Big|\leq\|z-\tilde{z}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}Q_{-}\left(\|z\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}},\|\tilde{z}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\right).

Hence, applying (
7.12
) to (
7.11
), and then recalling that
\|z\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\vee\|\tilde{z}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\leq A
, we conclude that for any
A>0
and any
z,\tilde{z}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
such that
\|z\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\vee\|\tilde{z}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\leq A
, we have

\|F(z)-F(\tilde{z})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\lesssim_{A}\|z-\tilde{z}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}},

which completes the proof.
∎

We are now ready to prove Proposition
7.1
.

Proof of Proposition
7.1
.

By Lemma
7.3
, we can assume without loss of generality that
v^{(1)}
and
v^{(2)}
satisfy conditions (i)-(iii) of Lemma
7.3
. Also by Lemma
7.3
, we can take
A>0
sufficiently large that

\|v^{(1)}\|_{L_{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\vee\|v^{(2)}\|_{L_{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\leq A.

(7.13)

By Lemma
7.3
(i) and the triangle inequality, we have that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle|v^{(1)}_{k}(t,x)-v^{(2)}_{k}(t,x)|\leq\int_{0}^{t}\Big(P_{t-{\tau}}|F_{k}({v}^{(1)}({\tau},\cdot))-F_{k}({v}^{(2)}({\tau},\cdot))|\Big)(x)\,d{\tau}.

(7.14)

By summing both sides of (
7.14
) over
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and then using (
7.13
) and the fact that by Lemma
7.4
, the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is locally Lipschitz, we conclude that for every
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
,

\|v^{(1)}(t,x)-v^{(2)}(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\lesssim_{A}\int_{0}^{t}\sup_{{\theta}\leq{\tau}}\|v^{(1)}({\theta},\cdot)-v^{(2)}({\theta},\cdot)\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\,d{\tau}.

Hence, for every
t\in[0,T]
,

\sup_{{\theta}\leq t}\|v^{(1)}({\theta},\cdot)-v^{(2)}({\theta},\cdot)\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\lesssim_{A}\int_{0}^{t}\sup_{{\theta}\leq{\tau}}\|v^{(1)}({\theta},\cdot)-v^{(2)}({\theta},\cdot)\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\,d{\tau}.

(7.15)

Applying Lemma
7.3
(iii) and Grönwall’s lemma to (
7.15
), we conclude that

\sup_{{\theta}\leq T}\|v^{(1)}({\theta},\cdot)-v^{(2)}({\theta},\cdot)\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}=0.

Therefore,
v^{(2)}
is a version of
v^{(1)}
, and the proof is complete.
∎

7.2
Proof of Proposition
7.2

Our first step towards the proof of Proposition
7.2
will be to derive a weak formulation of Hölder continuity for a mild solution
v\in L_{4\deg q_{-}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
to the system of PDEs (
2.11
).

Lemma 7.5
.

Suppose the assumptions of Proposition
7.1
hold. Let
T>0
and
v\in L_{4\deg q_{-}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
be a version of the non-negative mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) satisfying Lemma
7.3
(i)-(iii). For any
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
, let

V_{\mathcal{I}}(t,x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}v_{k}(t,x)\quad\forall\,(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}.

Then, for all
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
{\delta}\in(0,T)
, there exists
C_{{\delta},p,T}>0
such that for any
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
, any
t_{1},t_{2}\in[{\delta},T]
and any
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\Big|V_{\mathcal{I}}(t_{1},x_{1})\|v(t_{1},x_{1})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}^{p}-V_{\mathcal{I}}(t_{2},x_{2})\|v(t_{2},x_{2})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}^{p}\Big|\leq C_{{\delta},p,T}\Big(|x_{1}-x_{2}|+|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{1/2}\Big).

Proof.

First observe that from Lemma
7.3
(ii) and (iii), we conclude that

\sup_{(t,x)\in[{\delta},T]\times\mathbb{R}}\|v(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}<\infty.

(7.16)

Hence, applying the triangle inequality, and then the elementary inequality (
4.63
) and (
7.16
), we conclude that for any
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
t_{1},t_{2}\in[{\delta},T]
and
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\Big|V_{\mathcal{I}}(t_{1},x_{1})\|v(t_{1},x_{1})\|^{p}_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}-V_{\mathcal{I}}(t_{2},x_{2})\|v(t_{2},x_{2})\|^{p}_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big|

(7.17)

\displaystyle\leq\Big|V_{\mathcal{I}}(t_{1},x_{1})-V_{\mathcal{I}}(t_{2},x_{2})\Big|\|v(t_{1},x_{1})\|^{p}_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}+V_{\mathcal{I}}(t_{2},x_{2})\Big|\|v(t_{1},x_{1})\|^{p}_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}-\|v(t_{2},x_{2})\|^{p}_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{p,T}\Big|V_{\mathcal{I}}(t_{1},x_{1})-V_{\mathcal{I}}(t_{2},x_{2})\Big|+\Big|\|v(t_{1},x_{1})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}-\|v(t_{2},x_{2})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq 2\|v(t_{1},x_{1})-v(t_{2},x_{2})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}},

where the last inequality follows from the triangle inequality. By Lemma
7.3
,
v\in L_{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
, and therefore (
7.5
) implies that the map
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\ni(t,x)\mapsto F(v(t,x))

is in
L_{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
, i.e. that the source term in (
7.4
) is essentially bounded. Hence, by Assumption
4
(ii) and Lemma
7.3
(i), we can apply estimate (
0.A.18
) from Lemma
0.A.7
in the appendix to bound the term on the right-hand side of (
7.17
), which completes the proof.
∎

We are now ready to prove Proposition
7.2
.

Proof of Proposition
7.2
.

We will first establish the existence of a unique function
v=(v_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in\mathscr{C}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
which is a global mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
). For
T_{2}>T_{1}>0
and functions
v^{(1)}\in L_{4\deg q_{-}}([0,T_{1}]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
and
v^{(2)}\in L_{4\deg q_{-}}([0,T_{2}]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
which are both local mild solutions to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) in the sense of Definition
3.5
, Proposition
7.1
implies that
v^{(1)}(t,x)=v^{(2)}(t,x)
for
{\lambda}
-almost every
(t,x)\in[0,T_{1}]\times\mathbb{R}
. Since for any
T>0
, Proposition
6.2
implies the existence of a local mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) up to time
T
, Proposition
7.1
and Lemma
7.3
imply the existence of a unique function
v=(v_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
satisfying conditions (i)-(iii) of Proposition
7.2
.

To complete the proof, it will suffice then to establish that
v=(v_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
satisfies conditions (iv) and (v). Take
{\delta}>0
. By the semigroup property of the heat kernel
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
defined in (
3.4
) and by condition (i) of this proposition, observe that
for all
t>{\delta}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

v(t,x)=(P_{t-{\delta}}v({\delta},\cdot))(x)+\int_{{\delta}}^{t}\Big(P_{t-{\tau}}F(v({\tau},\cdot))\Big)(x)\,d{\tau}.

(7.18)

By the definition of the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
in (
2.12
) and the fact that
q_{+},q_{-}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
are polynomials, combined with Lemma
7.5
, we conclude that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the map

[{\delta},\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\ni(t,x)\mapsto F_{k}(v(t,x))

is locally Hölder continuous. Standard theory for linear parabolic PDEs (see e.g.
[
52
, Theorem 8.10.1]
) then implies that
v_{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{1,2}(({\delta},\infty)\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
, for all
{\delta}>0
. It follows that
v_{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{1,2}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, i.e. that
v=(v_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
satisfies condition (iv) of this proposition. Finally, by (
7.10
) stated in the proof of Lemma
7.3
, we can combine identity (
7.18
) and Fubini’s theorem to conclude that for
{\delta}>0
, for all
t>{\delta}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\|v(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}=(P_{t-{\delta}}\|v({\delta},\cdot)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})(x)+\int_{{\delta}}^{t}\Big(P_{t-{\tau}}g({\tau},\cdot))\Big)(x)\,d{\tau},

(7.19)

where the map
g:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is given by

g(t,x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}F_{k}(v(t,x))\quad\forall\,(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}.

(7.20)

Then, by applying the triangle inequality to (
7.20
), and then using Lemma
7.3
(iii) and the fact that by Lemma
7.4
, the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
is locally Lipschitz continuous, we conclude that for all
T>0
,
{\delta}\in(0,T)
,
t_{1},t_{2}\in[{\delta},T]
and
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle|g(t_{1},x_{1})-g(t_{2},x_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq\|F(v(t_{1},x_{1}))-F(v(t_{2},x_{2}))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{T}\|v(t_{1},x_{1})-v(t_{2},x_{2})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{{\delta},T}|x_{1}-x_{2}|+|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{1/2},

where for the last estimate we used estimate (
0.A.18
) from Lemma
0.A.7
in the appendix (by the same argument as at the end of the proof of Lemma
7.5
). Hence, the map
g:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined in (
7.20
) is locally Hölder continuous on
(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
. In particular, (
7.19
) shows that the scalar function
V_{\mathbb{N}_{0}}(t,x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\|v(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}
is a mild solution to a linear parabolic problem with locally Hölder continuous forcing term
g
on
({\delta},\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\;\forall\,{\delta}>0
. Therefore, again applying standard regularity properties of linear PDEs (see
[
52
, Theorem 8.10.1]
) to (
7.19
), we conclude that condition (v) of this proposition holds, which completes the proof.
∎

Next, we establish a bound on the local mass that holds uniformly over time and space, for the continuous non-negative mild solution
v
given in Proposition
7.2
, with the extra assumption that the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is monostable (see Definition
2.3
), and that the initial condition
f\in L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
satisfies Assumption
5
in addition to Assumption
4
. Although this result will be used only in Section
8
to address the asymptotic behaviour of the system of PDEs (
2.11
), we will state and prove it here since its proof follows from results in this section.

Lemma 7.6
.

Suppose
m>0
,
{\mu}\in[0,1]
,
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
satisfies Assumption
2
,
q_{+},q_{-}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
satisfy Assumption
3
, and
f\in L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
satisfies Assumption
4
and
5
.
Suppose also that the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is monostable in the sense of Definition
2.3
. Let
v=(v_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
be the version of the mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) given in Proposition
7.2
. Then,

\sup_{T\geq 0}\;\|v(T,\cdot)\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\leq 1.

Proof.

Observe that since
s_{k}\leq 1\;\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
by Assumption
2
, and by the definition of the reaction term in (
2.12
), for any
t\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}F_{k}(v(t,x))\leq\|v(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big(q_{+}(\|v(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})-q_{-}(\|v(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})\Big).

(7.21)

Let
w:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the unique non-negative function which is a classical solution to the reaction-diffusion PDE

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}_{t}w=\frac{m}{2}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}w+w\Big(q_{+}(w)-q_{-}(w)\Big),&t>0,\\
w(0,\cdot)=\|f(\cdot)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}.&\end{array}\right.

(7.22)

By conditions (iii) and (iv) from Definition
2.3
, and by Assumption
5
(i), we have (see e.g.
[
5
, Proposition 2.1]
)

\sup_{T\geq 0}\|w(T,\cdot)\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})}\leq 1.

(7.23)

Moreover, by Proposition
7.2
(v), identities (
7.19
) and (
7.20
), and by (
7.21
) and (
7.22
), we have that for all
(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}_{t}\|v(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}-\frac{m}{2}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}\|v(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}-\|v(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big(q_{+}(\|v(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})-q_{-}(\|v(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})\Big)

(7.24)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}_{t}w(t,x)-\frac{m}{2}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}w(t,x)-w(t,x)\Big(q_{+}(w(t,x))-q_{-}(w(t,x))\Big).

By Proposition
7.2
(iii) and by (
7.24
), we can apply classical comparison theorems for parabolic PDEs (see e.g.
[
5
, Proposition 2.1]
), and conclude from (
7.23
) that

\sup_{(T,x)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}}\|v(T,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\leq\sup_{(T,x)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}}w(T,x)\leq 1,

which (together with Assumption
5
(i)) yields the desired uniform bound and completes the proof.
∎

8
Asymptotic behaviour of the system of PDEs

In this section, we will investigate the asymptotic behaviour of solutions of the system of PDEs (
2.11
), and prove Theorem
2.5
, as well as Theorems
2.7
,
2.8
and
2.9
. In what follows, we denote the version of the mild solution of the system of PDEs (
2.11
) given in Proposition
7.2
by

u=(u_{k}(t,x))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,t\geq 0,\,x\in\mathbb{R}}.

We will refer to this solution as the continuous mild solution of (
2.11
).

We will assume that the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
defined in (
2.12
) is monostable, i.e. that
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
satisfies conditions (i)-(v) of Definition
2.3
, and that the initial condition
f=(f_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
of the system of PDEs (
2.11
) satisfies both Assumptions
4
and
5
. Our strategy to determine the long-term behaviour of
u
will be to represent each of the functions
u_{k}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
in terms of a Feynman-Kac formula. We divide the reminder of this section into four subsections. In Section
8.1
, we state and prove the Feynman-Kac representation. We prove Theorem
2.5
and Theorem
2.8
in Section
8.2
. The control over the evolution of the prevalence of mutations gained in Section
8.2
, together with ideas adapted from
[
64
]
, will be used to prove Theorem
2.7
in Section
8.3
. Finally, we will prove Theorem
2.9
in Section
8.4
.

8.1
Feynman-Kac representation formula

Fix
m\in(0,\infty)
, let
(W(t))_{t\geq 0}
denote a Brownian motion run at speed
m
, and for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
, let
\mathbb{P}_{x}
denote the probability measure associated to
(W(t))_{t\geq 0}
started from
x
, and let
\mathbb{E}_{x}
denote the corresponding expectation. In this section, we will extensively use a Feynman-Kac formula. For ease of reference, we quote a version stated and proved in
[
10
, Proposition 3.1]
, which does not require continuity of the solution at time
0
.

Proposition 8.1

(Feynman-Kac formula)
.

Suppose
G,H:(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
are continuous, and uniformly bounded on compact time intervals. Suppose that
v\in\mathscr{C}^{1,2}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R})
is bounded on compact time intervals, and satisfies

\displaystyle{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}_{t}v

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{2}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}v+Gv+H\quad\forall(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R},\quad v(0,\cdot)=f(\cdot),

and
v(t,x)\rightarrow f(x)
as
t\rightarrow 0
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
such that
f
is continuous at
x
, where
f\in L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
is continuous at
{\lambda}
-almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, for any
T\geq 0
,
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle v(T,x)=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\Bigg[v(T-t,W(t))\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}G(T-{\tau},W({\tau}))d{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}H(T-{\tau},W({\tau}))\exp\left(\int_{0}^{{\tau}}G(T-{{\theta}},W({\theta}))\,d{\theta}\right)d{\tau}\Bigg].

Our next result provides a Feynman–Kac representation of
u_{k}
, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Lemma 8.2

(Feynman-Kac representation for solutions of (
2.11
))
.

Suppose
m>0
,
{\mu}\in[0,1]
,
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
satisfies Assumption
2
,
q_{+},q_{-}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
satisfy Assumption
3
, and
f\in L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
satisfies Assumption
4
. Let
u=(u_{k}(t,x))_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,t\geq 0,\,x\in\mathbb{R}}
denote the continuous mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) given in Proposition
7.2
. Then, recalling that
u_{k}(0,x)=f_{k}(x)\;\forall k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,x\in\mathbb{R}
, for any
T\geq 0
,
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle u_{0}(T,x)=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[u_{0}(T-t,W(t))\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\Big((1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-}\Big)\Big(\|u(T-{\tau},W({\tau}))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big)\,d{\tau}\right)\right].

(8.1)

Moreover, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
T\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle u_{k}(T,x)=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[f_{k}(W(T))\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}\Big(s_{k}(1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-}\Big)\Big(\|u(T-t,W(t))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big)dt\right)\right]

(8.2)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{x}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\Big(s_{k-1}{\mu}u_{k-1}q_{+}(\|u\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})\Big)(T-t,W(t))

\displaystyle\cdot\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\Big(s_{k}(1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-}\Big)\Big(\|u(T-{\tau},W({\tau}))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big)d{\tau}\right)\;dt\Bigg].

Remark 8.3
.

We collect some observations concerning the Feynman-Kac representation below.

(a)

We observe that the Feynman-Kac formula for
u_{0}
differs from the formula for
u_{k}
with
k\in\mathbb{N}
since, by the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
defined in (
2.12
), only the subpopulation without mutations can be a source for
u_{0}
, while for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the subpopulations with
k-1
or
k
mutations can both be sources for
u_{k}
.

(b)

In the special case
t=T
of (
8.1
), we have

u_{0}(T,x)=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[f_{0}(W(T))\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}\Big((1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-}\Big)\Big(\|u(T-{\tau},W({\tau}))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big)\,d{\tau}\right)\right].

(8.3)

Proof of Lemma
8.2
.

By the properties of
u
given in Proposition
7.2
, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
T>0
,
u_{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{1,2}((0,T]\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
is uniformly bounded on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
, and
u_{k}
satisfies (
7.2
). Moreover, the functions

\displaystyle(t,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto G^{(k)}(t,x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Big(s_{k}(1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-}\Big)\Big(\|u(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big),

\displaystyle\textrm{and }(t,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto H^{(k)}(t,x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Big(\mathds{1}_{\{k\geq 1\}}s_{k-1}{\mu}u_{k-1}q_{+}(\|u\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})\Big)(t,x)

are bounded and continuous on
(0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
. Since, by Assumption
4
,
f_{k}\in L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
is continuous at
{\lambda}
-almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}
, identities (
8.1
) and (
8.2
) follow directly from Proposition
8.1
.
∎

8.2
Evolution of the prevalence of mutations

In this subsection, we will prove Theorem
2.5
(and then prove Theorem
2.8
as a consequence of Theorem
2.1
). Throughout this subsection, we assume that
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is monostable in the sense of Definition
2.3
, and so in particular
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
satisfies Assumption
2
and is strictly decreasing, and
{\mu}\in(0,1)
. Recall that the sequence
({\alpha}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
defined in (
2.16
) is given by
{\alpha}_{0}=1
and

{\alpha}_{k}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{{\mu}s_{i-1}}{(1-{\mu})(1-s_{i})}\quad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N}.

(8.4)

Observe that since, by Assumption
2
(iv),
s_{k}\rightarrow 0
as
k\rightarrow\infty
, we have
({\alpha}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
. Moreover, recall from (
2.17
) that

\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}=\min_{U\in[0,1]}q_{+}(U)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}=\max_{U\in[0,1]}q_{+}(U).

Our first step towards the proof of Theorem
2.5
will be to define a sequence of functions
(\underline{{\pi}}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
that we will use to obtain a lower bound on the ratio of
u_{k}
and
u_{0}
, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Lemma 8.4
.

Suppose
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is monostable in the sense of Definition
2.3
. Define a sequence
(\underline{{\pi}}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
of non-negative real-valued functions on
[0,\infty)
with the following recursive formula: for all
T\geq 0
, let

\underline{{\pi}}_{k}(T)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}1&\textrm{if }k=0,\\
{\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}\int_{0}^{T}\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}(1-s_{1})(1-{\mu})\Big)\,dt&\textrm{if }k=1,\\
{\mu}s_{k-1}\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}\int_{0}^{T}\underline{{\pi}}_{k-1}(T-t)\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}(1-s_{k})(1-{\mu})\Big)\,dt&\textrm{for }k\geq 2.\end{array}\right.

(8.5)

Then, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\underline{{\pi}}_{k}
is strictly increasing on
[0,\infty)
and strictly positive on
(0,\infty)
. Moreover, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\underline{{\pi}}_{k}(T)={\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}\right)^{k}>0.

(8.6)

By our observation after (
8.4
) that
({\alpha}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
, and the fact that by (
2.17
) and by Definition
2.3
(ii),
0<\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}\leq\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}
, we have that
\left({\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}\right)^{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
.

Proof.

We will use an induction argument on
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Observe that by the definition of
\underline{{\pi}}_{1}
in (
8.5
), we have for all
T>0
,

\frac{d}{dT}\underline{{\pi}}_{1}(T)={\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}\exp\Big(-T\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}(1-s_{1})(1-{\mu})\Big)>0,

(8.7)

since by Definition
2.3
(ii), the fact that
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is monostable implies that
\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}>0
. We conclude from (
8.7
) that
\underline{{\pi}}_{1}
is strictly increasing on
[0,\infty)
. By taking the limit in (
8.5
) as
T\rightarrow\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\underline{{\pi}}_{1}(T)

\displaystyle={\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}(1-s_{1})(1-{\mu})\Big)dt

(8.8)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}{(1-{\mu})(1-s_{1})\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}

\displaystyle={\alpha}_{1}\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}},

where for the second equality we used our assumption that
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is monostable and so
(1-s_{1})(1-{\mu})>0
by Definition
2.3
, and for the last line we used the definition of
{\alpha}_{1}
in (
2.16
).

Suppose now that for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\underline{{\pi}}_{k}
satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

\underline{{\pi}}_{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{1}((0,\infty))
with bounded derivative, and for all
T>0
,

\frac{d}{dT}\underline{{\pi}}_{k}(T)>0.

(8.9)

(ii)

The limit in (
8.6
) holds for
\underline{{\pi}}_{k}
.

Under conditions (i) and (ii), by differentiating
\underline{{\pi}}_{k+1}(T)
with respect to
T
, using (
8.5
), dominated convergence and the fact that (
8.5
) implies that
\underline{{\pi}}_{k}(0)=0
, we conclude that for
T>0
,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dT}\underline{{\pi}}_{k+1}(T)={\mu}s_{k}\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{d}{dT}\underline{{\pi}}_{k}(T-t)\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}(1-s_{k+1})(1-{\mu})\Big)\,dt>0,

(8.10)

where the inequality follows from (
8.9
). Observe that (
8.10
) implies, in particular, that
\underline{{\pi}}_{k+1}
is strictly increasing on
[0,\infty)
. Hence, to complete the induction argument, it remains to show that the limit in (
8.6
) holds with
k
replaced by
k+1
. By taking the limit in (
8.5
) as
T\rightarrow\infty
, and then using monotone convergence and our assumption that (
8.6
) holds for
\underline{{\pi}}_{k}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\underline{{\pi}}_{k+1}(T)

\displaystyle={\mu}s_{k}\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathds{1}_{\{t\leq T\}}\underline{{\pi}}_{k}(T-t)\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}(1-s_{k+1})(1-{\mu})\Big)\,dt

\displaystyle={\mu}s_{k}\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}\right)^{k}\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}(1-s_{k+1})(1-{\mu})\Big)\,dt

\displaystyle={\alpha}_{k+1}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}\right)^{k+1},

where we used the definition of
{\alpha}_{k}
in (
2.16
) for the last identity. This completes the proof, by induction on
k
.
∎

We now define a sequence of auxiliary functions that we will use for upper bounds on the ratio of
u_{k}
and
u_{0}
. Suppose that
f=(f_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
satisfies Assumptions
4
and
5
, and recall that
\left(\hat{{\pi}}_{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
is defined in Assumption
5
(iii).

Lemma 8.5
.

Suppose
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is monostable in the sense of Definition
2.3
. Define
{\phi}=({\phi}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, where
{\phi}_{k}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, as follows. Let
{\phi}_{1}\equiv 0
and, for each
k\geq 2
and
T\geq 0
, let

{{\phi}_{k}}(T)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{({\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}T)^{k-i}}{(k-i)!}\Bigg(\prod_{j=i}^{k-1}s_{j}\Bigg)\hat{{\pi}}_{i}\exp\Big(-T\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{i})(1-{\mu})\Big).

(8.11)

Then
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}(T)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\|{{\phi}(T)}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}<\infty
for every
T\geq 0
, with
\sup_{T\geq 0}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}(T)<\infty
and

\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}(T)=0.

(8.12)

Proof.

We start by noticing that by (
8.11
) and Fubini’s theorem,
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}(T)=\|{\phi}(T)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}
is given by

\displaystyle{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}(T)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\;\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{({\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}T)^{k-i}}{(k-i)!}\Bigg(\prod_{j=i}^{k-1}s_{j}\Bigg)\hat{{\pi}}_{i}\exp\Big(-T\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{i})(1-{\mu})\Big)

(8.13)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\;\sum_{k=i+1}^{\infty}\frac{({\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}T)^{k-i}}{(k-i)!}\Bigg(\prod_{j=i}^{k-1}s_{j}\Bigg)\hat{{\pi}}_{i}\exp\Big(-T\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{i})(1-{\mu})\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\hat{{\pi}}_{i}\exp\Big(-T\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{i})(1-{\mu})\Big)\sum_{k=i+1}^{\infty}\frac{({\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}T)^{k-i}}{(k-i)!}\Bigg(\prod_{j=i}^{k-1}s_{j}\Bigg).

Since, by Definition
2.3
,
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is strictly decreasing,
{\mu}\in(0,1)
and
\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}>0
, and by Assumption
2
(iv),
s_{k}\rightarrow 0
as
k\rightarrow\infty
, there exists
J\in\mathbb{N}
such that

0<s_{k}<\frac{(1-s_{1})(1-{\mu})}{2{\mu}}\cdot\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}\quad\forall k\geq J.

(8.14)

For any
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=i+1}^{\infty}\frac{({\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}T)^{k-i}}{(k-i)!}\Bigg(\prod_{j=i}^{k-1}s_{j}\Bigg)

(8.15)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=i+1}^{\infty}\frac{({\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}T)^{k-i}}{(k-i)!}\Bigg(\prod_{j=i}^{k-1}s_{j}\Bigg)\mathds{1}_{\{k\leq J\}}+\sum_{k=i+1}^{\infty}\frac{({\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}T)^{k-i}}{(k-i)!}\Bigg(\prod_{j=i}^{k-1}s_{j}\Bigg)\mathds{1}_{\{k>J\}}.

We will bound each of the sums on the right-hand side of (
8.15
) separately. For the first sum, we note that since
s_{j}\leq 1\;\forall\,j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
by Assumption
2
, for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=i+1}^{\infty}\frac{({\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}T)^{k-i}}{(k-i)!}\Bigg(\prod_{j=i}^{k-1}s_{j}\Bigg)\mathds{1}_{\{k\leq J\}}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{J-1}\frac{({\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}T)^{k}}{k!}.

(8.16)

For the second sum on the right-hand side of (
8.15
), we note that by letting
k^{\prime}=k-(J\vee i)
and using that
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is monotonically decreasing by Assumption
2
(iii), we have for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=i+1}^{\infty}\frac{({\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}T)^{k-i}}{(k-i)!}\Bigg(\prod_{j=i}^{k-1}s_{j}\Bigg)\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{k>J\}}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(1\vee({\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}T)^{J}\Big)\cdot\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{\infty}\frac{({\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}s_{J}T)^{k^{\prime}}}{(k^{\prime})!}

(8.17)

\displaystyle=\Big(1\vee({\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}T)^{J}\Big)\exp\Big({\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}s_{J}T\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\Big(1\vee({\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}T)^{J}\Big)\exp\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{1})(1-{\mu})T}{2}\right),

where for the last inequality we used (
8.14
). Finally, applying estimates (
8.15
), (
8.16
) and (
8.17
) to (
8.13
), and then using the fact that
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is monotonically decreasing again, we conclude that for all
T\geq 0
,

\displaystyle{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}(T)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{J-1}\frac{({\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}T)^{k}}{k!}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\hat{{\pi}}_{i}\exp\Big(-T\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{i})(1-{\mu})\Big)

(8.18)

\displaystyle+\Big(1\vee({\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}T)^{J}\Big)\exp\left(\frac{T\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{1})(1-{\mu})}{2}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\hat{{\pi}}_{i}\exp\Big(-T\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{i})(1-{\mu})\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{T\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{1})(1-{\mu})}{2}\right)\|\hat{{\pi}}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{J-1}\frac{({\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}T)^{k}}{k!}+\Big(1\vee({\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}T)^{J}\Big)\Bigg).

Hence, for any
T\geq 0
, we have
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}(T)<\infty
. Moreover, by Definition
2.3
we have
s_{1},{\mu}<1
and
\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}>0
, and so
\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{1})(1-{\mu})>0
. Hence, recalling that
J\in\mathbb{N}
is a fixed parameter satisfying (
8.14
), we see that
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}(T)
is bounded, and by taking
T\rightarrow\infty
, the right-hand side of (
8.18
) vanishes, which completes the proof.
∎

Our next result combines the Feynman–Kac representation from Lemma
8.2
with Lemmas
8.4
and
8.5
to control the evolution of the ratio of
u_{k}
and
u_{0}
over time. We recall the sequences of non-negative real numbers
({\alpha}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
and
(\hat{{\pi}}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
defined in (
2.16
) and in Assumption
5
(iii). Also, recall the sequences of non-negative functions
(\underline{{\pi}}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
and
({\phi}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
defined in (
8.5
) and (
8.11
).

Lemma 8.6
.

Suppose that the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is monostable in the sense of Definition
2.3
, and that
f
satisfies Assumptions
4
and
5
, and let
u=(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be the continuous mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
)given in Proposition
7.2
. Then, for any
T>0
,
x\in\mathbb{R}
and every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\underline{{\pi}}_{k}(T)u_{0}(T,x)\leq u_{k}(T,x)\leq\Bigg(\hat{{\pi}}_{k}\exp\Big(-T\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k})(1-{\mu})\Big)+{\phi}_{k}(T)+{\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}\right)^{k}\Bigg)u_{0}(T,x).

(8.19)

Proof.

We start by using an induction argument to establish the lower bound in (
8.19
), i.e. that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\underline{{\pi}}_{k}(T)u_{0}(T,x)\leq u_{k}(T,x)\;\forall\,T>0\textrm{ and }\forall\,x\in\mathbb{R}.

(8.20)

Observe that by Definition
2.3
,

s_{k}<s_{0}=1\quad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N}.

(8.21)

Moreover, by Lemma
7.6
and (
2.17
),

\inf_{t\geq 0}\;\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}}q_{+}(\|u(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})\geq\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\sup_{t\geq 0}\;\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}q_{+}(\|u(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})\leq\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}.

(8.22)

By using (
8.2
) from Lemma
8.2
with
k=1
, and using the fact that
f_{1}
is non-negative by Assumption
4
, together with identity (
8.21
), and then using estimate (
8.22
), we have that for any
T\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle u_{1}(T,x)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}_{x}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\Big({\mu}u_{0}q_{+}(\|u\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})\Big)(T-t,W(t))

\displaystyle\cdot\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\Big(s_{1}(1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-}\Big)\Big(\|u(T-{\tau},W({\tau}))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big)d{\tau}\right)\;dt\Bigg]

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{T}{\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}(1-s_{1})(1-{\mu})\Big)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathbb{E}_{x}\Bigg[u_{0}(T-t,W(t))\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\Big((1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-}\Big)\Big(\|u(T-{\tau},W({\tau}))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big)d{\tau}\right)\Bigg]dt

\displaystyle=\underline{{\pi}}_{1}(T)u_{0}(T,x),

where the final equality follows from the Feynman-Kac formula given in (
8.1
) and the definition of
\underline{{\pi}}_{1}
in (
8.5
).

Now take
k\in\mathbb{N}
and suppose that (
8.20
) holds. Then, by using the Feynman-Kac representation in (
8.2
) for
u_{k+1}
and the fact that
f_{k+1}\geq 0
by Assumption
4
, and then by our assumption (
8.20
), and by (
8.21
) and (
8.22
), and then Fubini’s theorem, we have that for any
T\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle u_{k+1}(T,x)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}_{x}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\Big(s_{k}{\mu}u_{k}q_{+}(\|u\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})\Big)(T-t,W(t))

\displaystyle\cdot\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\Big(s_{k+1}(1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-}\Big)\Big(\|u(T-{\tau},W({\tau}))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big)d{\tau}\right)\;dt\Bigg]

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{T}{\mu}s_{k}\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}\underline{{\pi}}_{k}(T-t)\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}(1-s_{k+1})(1-{\mu})\Big)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathbb{E}_{x}\Bigg[u_{0}(T-t,W(t))\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\Big((1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-}\Big)\Big(\|u(T-{\tau},W({\tau}))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big)d{\tau}\right)\Bigg]\;dt

\displaystyle=\underline{{\pi}}_{k+1}(T)u_{0}(T,x),

where we used (
8.5
) and (
8.1
) for the final equality. Hence, by induction, (
8.20
) holds for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

It remains to establish the upper bound in (
8.19
), i.e. that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
T>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

u_{k}(T,x)\leq\Bigg(\hat{{\pi}}_{k}\exp\Big(-T\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k})(1-{\mu})\Big)+{\phi}_{k}(T)+{\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}\right)^{k}\Bigg)u_{0}(T,x).

(8.23)

We will also use an induction argument to establish (
8.23
). For
k=1
, by using (
8.2
), Assumption
5
(iii), (
8.22
) and that
s_{0}=1
by Assumption
2
(i), we have that for
T>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle u_{1}(T,x)

\displaystyle\leq\hat{{\pi}}_{1}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[f_{0}(W(T))\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}\Big(s_{1}(1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-}\Big)\Big(\|u(T-t,W(t))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big)dt\right)\right]

(8.24)

\displaystyle+{\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}\mathbb{E}_{x}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{T}u_{0}(T-t,W(t))

\displaystyle\cdot\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\Big(s_{1}(1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-}\Big)\Big(\|u(T-{\tau},W({\tau}))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big)d{\tau}\right)\;dt\Bigg].

We now bound the terms on the right-hand side of (
8.24
) separately. For the first term, by using (
8.21
) and (
8.22
), we have

\displaystyle\hat{{\pi}}_{1}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[f_{0}(W(T))\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}\Big(s_{1}(1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-}\Big)\Big(\|u(T-t,W(t))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big)dt\right)\right]

(8.25)

\displaystyle\leq\hat{{\pi}}_{1}\exp\Big(-T\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{1})(1-{\mu})\Big)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[f_{0}(W(T))\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}\Big((1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-}\Big)\Big(\|u(T-t,W(t))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big)dt\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\hat{{\pi}}_{1}\exp\Big(-T\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{1})(1-{\mu})\Big)u_{0}(T,x),

where for the last equality we used (
8.3
). To bound the second term on the right-hand side of (
8.24
), we use (
8.21
), (
8.22
) and Fubini’s theorem, and then (
8.1
), obtaining

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}\mathbb{E}_{x}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{T}u_{0}(T-t,W(t))\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\Big(s_{1}(1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-}\Big)\Big(\|u(T-{\tau},W({\tau}))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big)d{\tau}\right)\;dt\Bigg]

(8.26)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}{\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{1})(1-{\mu})\Big)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathbb{E}_{x}\Bigg[u_{0}(T-t,W(t))\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\Big((1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-}\Big)\Big(\|u(T-{\tau},W({\tau}))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big)d{\tau}\right)\Bigg]\,dt

\displaystyle={\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}u_{0}(T,x)\int_{0}^{T}\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{1})(1-{\mu})\Big)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq{\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}u_{0}(T,x)\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{1})(1-{\mu})\Big)\,dt

\displaystyle={\alpha}_{1}\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}u_{0}(T,x),

where for the last equality we used (
2.16
) and the fact that
\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{1})(1-{\mu})>0
by Definition
2.3
. Applying (
8.25
) and (
8.26
) to (
8.24
), and recalling that we defined
{\phi}_{1}\equiv 0
in the statement of Lemma
8.5
, we conclude that (
8.23
) holds for all
T>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
for
k=1
.

Suppose now that for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, (
8.23
) holds for all
T>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
. By applying (
8.2
) for
u_{k+1}
, and using Assumption
5
(iii), the induction hypothesis (
8.23
) for
k
, (
8.22
), and (
8.1
), for
T>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\displaystyle u_{k+1}(T,x)

(8.27)

\displaystyle\leq\hat{{\pi}}_{k+1}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[f_{0}(W(T))\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}\Big(s_{k+1}(1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-}\Big)\Big(\|u(T-t,W(t))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big)dt\right)\right]

\displaystyle+{\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}s_{k}\hat{{\pi}}_{k}u_{0}(T,x)\int_{0}^{T}\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k+1})(1-{\mu})-(T-t)\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k})(1-{\mu})\Big)\,dt

\displaystyle+{\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}s_{k}u_{0}(T,x)\int_{0}^{T}\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k+1})(1-{\mu})\Big){\phi}_{k}(T-t)\,dt

\displaystyle+{\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}s_{k}u_{0}(T,x)\int_{0}^{T}\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k+1})(1-{\mu})\Big){\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}\right)^{k}\,dt.

We will bound each of the terms on the right-hand side of (
8.27
) separately. For the first term, by using (
8.21
), (
8.22
), and (
8.3
), we have

\displaystyle\hat{{\pi}}_{k+1}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[f_{0}(W(T))\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}\Big(s_{k+1}(1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-}\Big)\Big(\|u(T-t,W(t))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big)dt\right)\right]

(8.28)

\displaystyle\leq\hat{{\pi}}_{k+1}\exp\Big(-T\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k+1})(1-{\mu})\Big)u_{0}(T,x).

For the second term on the right-hand side of (
8.27
), we use that
1-s_{k+1}\geq 1-s_{k}
by Assumption
2
(iii), and therefore

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}s_{k}\hat{{\pi}}_{k}u_{0}(T,x)\int_{0}^{T}\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k+1})(1-{\mu})-(T-t)\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k})(1-{\mu})\Big)\,dt

(8.29)

\displaystyle\leq{\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}s_{k}\hat{{\pi}}_{k}u_{0}(T,x)\int_{0}^{T}\exp\Big(-T\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k})(1-{\mu})\Big)\,dt

\displaystyle={\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}Ts_{k}\hat{{\pi}}_{k}\exp\Big(-T\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k})(1-{\mu})\Big)u_{0}(T,x).

To bound the third term on the right-hand side of (
8.27
), we use the definition of
{\phi}_{k}
in (
8.11
), and then that
1-s_{i}\leq 1-s_{k+1}
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}\cap\{1,\ldots,k\}
by Assumption
2
(iii), to deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k+1})(1-{\mu})\Big){\phi}_{k}(T-t)\,dt

(8.30)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k+1})(1-{\mu})\Big)

\displaystyle\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{({\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}(T-t))^{k-i}}{(k-i)!}\Bigg(\prod_{j=i}^{k-1}s_{j}\Bigg)\hat{{\pi}}_{i}\exp\Big(-(T-t)\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{i})(1-{\mu})\Big)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\Bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\frac{({\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}(T-t))^{k-i}}{(k-i)!}\,dt\Bigg)\Bigg(\prod_{j=i}^{k-1}s_{j}\Bigg)\hat{{\pi}}_{i}\exp\Big(-T\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{i})(1-{\mu})\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{{\mu}^{k-i}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}^{k-i}T^{k+1-i}}{(k+1-i)!}\Bigg(\prod_{j=i}^{k-1}s_{j}\Bigg)\hat{{\pi}}_{i}\exp\Big(-T\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{i})(1-{\mu})\Big).

Applying (
8.30
) to the third term on the right-hand side of (
8.27
), we get

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}s_{k}u_{0}(T,x)\int_{0}^{T}\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k+1})(1-{\mu})\Big){\phi}_{k}(T-t)\,dt

(8.31)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{({\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}T)^{k+1-i}}{(k+1-i)!}\Bigg(\prod_{j=i}^{k}s_{j}\Bigg)\hat{{\pi}}_{i}\exp\Big(-T\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{i})(1-{\mu})\Big)u_{0}(T,x).

Combining (
8.29
), (
8.31
) and the definition of
{\phi}_{k+1}
in (
8.11
), we conclude that the second and the third terms on the right-hand side of (
8.27
) satisfy

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}s_{k}\hat{{\pi}}_{k}u_{0}(T,x)\int_{0}^{T}\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k+1})(1-{\mu})-(T-t)\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k})(1-{\mu})\Big)\,dt

(8.32)

\displaystyle+{\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}s_{k}u_{0}(T,x)\int_{0}^{T}\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k+1})(1-{\mu})\Big){\phi}_{k}(T-t)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq{\phi}_{k+1}(T)u_{0}(T,x).

Finally, for the fourth term on the right-hand side of (
8.27
), we observe that

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}s_{k}u_{0}(T,x)\int_{0}^{T}\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k+1})(1-{\mu})\Big){\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}\right)^{k}\,dt

(8.33)
