\displaystyle\leq{\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}s_{k}{\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}\right)^{k}u_{0}(T,x)\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k+1})(1-{\mu})\Big)\,dt

\displaystyle={\alpha}_{k+1}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}\right)^{k+1}u_{0}(T,x),

where we used (
2.16
) and the fact that
\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k+1})(1-{\mu})>0
by Definition
2.3
for the last equality. Applying (
8.28
), (
8.32
) and (
8.33
) to (
8.27
), we conclude that (
8.23
) holds with
k
replaced by
k+1
, which completes the induction argument. The proof is then complete.
∎

Before proving Theorem
2.5
, we state and prove a result that is a corollary of Lemmas
8.5
and
8.6
, and that will be used in bounding the spreading speed in Section
8.3
.

Corollary 8.7
.

Suppose that
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is monostable in the sense of Definition
2.3
, and that
f
satisfies Assumptions
4
and
5
, and let
u=(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be the continuous mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) given in Proposition
7.2
. Then there exists
A>0
such that for any
T>0
and any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

u_{0}(T,x)\leq\|u(T,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\leq(1+A)u_{0}(T,x).

Proof.

The first inequality follows from the definition of the norm
\|\cdot\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}
. For the second inequality, let

A\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sup_{t\geq 0}\;\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\hat{{\pi}}_{k}\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k})(1-{\mu})\Big)+{\phi}_{k}(t)+{\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}\right)^{k}\right),

(8.34)

where
({\alpha}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
and
(\hat{{\pi}}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
are defined in (
2.16
) and Assumption
5
(iii), and
({\phi}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is defined in (
8.11
).
By Assumption
5
(iii), the sequence
(\hat{{\pi}}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is summable. Moreover, by Lemma
8.5
, we have
\sup_{t\geq 0}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}(t)<\infty
, where
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}(t)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\phi}_{k}(t)\;\forall\,t\geq 0
. Also, as observed after Remark
2.6
, the sequence
\left({\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}\right)^{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is summable. Therefore, the constant
A
defined in (
8.34
) is finite, and the result follows from Lemma
8.6
.
∎

We are now ready to prove Theorem
2.5
.

Proof of Theorem
2.5
.

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we define
\underline{{\pi}}_{k}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
as in (
8.5
), and the map
\overline{{\pi}}_{k}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
by

\overline{{\pi}}_{k}(T)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\hat{{\pi}}_{k}\exp\Big(-T\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k})(1-{\mu})\Big)+{\phi}_{k}(T)+{\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}\right)^{k}\quad\forall\,T\geq 0.

Note that
(\hat{{\pi}}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
by Assumption
5
(iii), and for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k})(1-{\mu})>\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{1})(1-{\mu})>0

by Definition
2.3
.
Moreover, as observed after Remark
2.6
, the sequences

\left({\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}\right)^{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\left({\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}\right)^{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}

are elements of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
.
Therefore, Theorem
2.5
follows directly from Lemmas
8.4
,
8.5
and
8.6
, noting that since
\underline{{\pi}}_{k}
is increasing for each
k
by Lemma
8.4
, the limit in (
8.6
) also holds in
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
.
∎

We now combine the law of large numbers given by Theorem
2.1
and our control over the evolution of the proportions of mutations given by Theorem
2.5
to prove Theorem
2.8
.

Proof of Theorem
2.8
.

By Theorem
2.5
, for any
{\delta}>0
and
K\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
T_{{\delta},K}>0
such that for any
T\geq T_{{\delta},K}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
k\leq K
,

\Bigg({\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}\right)^{k}-\frac{{\delta}}{2}\Bigg)u_{0}(T,x)\leq u_{k}(T,x)\leq\Bigg({\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}\right)^{k}+\frac{{\delta}}{2}\Bigg)u_{0}(T,x),

where
u=(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
is the continuous mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) given in Proposition
7.2
. Fix
T\geq T_{{\delta},K}
, and let
\mathcal{I}
be a compact interval in
\mathbb{R}
which is not a singleton. Since the measure induced by
u_{k}
on
\mathbb{R}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, by Theorem
2.1
, Skorokhod’s representation theorem and standard results on the vague topology in the space of Radon measures (see, for instance,
[
47
, Lemma 4.1(iv)]
), it is possible to construct the sequence of stochastic processes
(u^{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
on the same probability space in such a way that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, almost surely

u^{N}_{k}(T)(\mathcal{I})\xrightarrow{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathcal{I}}u_{k}(T,x)\,dx.

Moreover, since by Assumption
5
(ii) the set

\{x\in\mathbb{R}:f_{0}(x)>0\}
has positive Lebesgue measure, the Feynman–Kac representation (
8.3
) (together with the strict positivity of the transition
density of a Brownian motion run at speed
m>0
for
T>0
) implies that
u_{0}(T,x)>0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. In particular,

\int_{\mathcal{I}}u_{0}(T,x)\,dx>0.

Since almost sure convergence implies convergence in probability, we then conclude that for every
{\varepsilon}>0
, we can choose
N_{{\delta},{\varepsilon},\mathcal{I},K,T}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
N\geq N_{{\delta},{\varepsilon},\mathcal{I},K,T}
and
k\in[\![K]\!]
,

\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\Bigg({\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}\right)^{k}-{\delta}\Bigg)u^{N}_{0}(T)(\mathcal{I})\leq u^{N}_{k}(T)(\mathcal{I})\leq\Bigg({\alpha}_{k}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}}\right)^{k}+{\delta}\Bigg)u^{N}_{0}(T)(\mathcal{I})\Bigg)\geq 1-{\varepsilon},

which completes the proof.
∎

8.3
Spreading speed under Fisher-KPP dynamics

In this subsection, we will prove Theorem
2.7
by computing the spreading speed of the solution of (
2.11
) when the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
satisfies a Fisher-KPP condition. Throughout this subsection, we let
u=(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
denote the continuous mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) given in Proposition
7.2
. We suppose that
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
q_{+}
,
q_{-}
and
f
satisfy Assumptions
2
,
3
,
4
and
5
, and that the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
defined in (
2.12
) is monostable (in the sense of Definition
2.3
) and is of Fisher-KPP type, i.e. we suppose that (
2.14
) holds.

Our strategy will be to adapt the arguments used in the proof of
[
64
, Theorem 1.1]
. We start by proving a Hölder estimate for
u_{0}
that holds uniformly in time, analogous to
[
64
, Lemma 2.2]
. To simplify notation, let

H_{\min}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\min_{U\in[0,1]}\Big((1-{\mu})q_{+}(U)-q_{-}(U)\Big)\quad\textrm{and}\quad H_{\max}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(1-{\mu})q_{+}(0)-q_{-}(0),

(8.35)

and observe that since we are assuming throughout this subsection that the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is of Fisher-KPP type in the sense of Definition
2.3
, we have

H_{\max}=\max_{U\in[0,1]}\Big((1-{\mu})q_{+}(U)-q_{-}(U)\Big)>0.

(8.36)

Lemma 8.8
.

Suppose that
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
q_{+}
and
q_{-}
satisfy Assumptions
2
and
3
, that
{\mu}\in(0,1)
, that
m>0
, that the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
defined in (
2.12
) is of Fisher-KPP type in the sense of Definition
2.3
, and that
f
satisfies Assumptions
4
and
5
. Then, there exists
{\varepsilon}_{0}>0
such that for any
{\varepsilon}\in(0,{\varepsilon}_{0})
, for any
t\geq 1
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
with
|x-y|\leq{\varepsilon}^{3}
,

|u_{0}(t,x)-u_{0}(t,y)|\leq{\varepsilon}.

Proof.

The proof closely follows the arguments used in
[
64
, Lemma 2.2]
. By Lemma
7.6
, we have
\|u(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\leq 1
for all
t>0
,
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Therefore, by the Feynman-Kac representation in (
8.1
) and by (
8.35
) and (
8.36
), we have that for every
z\in\mathbb{R}
, any
t\geq 1
and any
{\varepsilon}\in(0,1)
,

\exp({\varepsilon}^{2}H_{\min})\mathbb{E}_{z}[u_{0}(t-{\varepsilon}^{2},W({\varepsilon}^{2}))]\leq u_{0}(t,z)\leq\exp({\varepsilon}^{2}H_{\max})\mathbb{E}_{z}[u_{0}(t-{\varepsilon}^{2},W({\varepsilon}^{2}))].

(8.37)

Hence, for any
t\geq 1
and any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle u_{0}(t,x)-u_{0}(t,y)

\displaystyle\leq\exp({\varepsilon}^{2}H_{\max})(\mathbb{E}_{x}[u_{0}(t-{\varepsilon}^{2},W({\varepsilon}^{2}))]-\mathbb{E}_{y}[u_{0}(t-{\varepsilon}^{2},W({\varepsilon}^{2}))])

(8.38)

\displaystyle+\exp({\varepsilon}^{2}H_{\max})\Big(1-\exp(-{\varepsilon}^{2}(H_{\max}-H_{\min}))\Big)\mathbb{E}_{y}[u_{0}(t-{\varepsilon}^{2},W({\varepsilon}^{2}))].

We will bound each term on the right-hand side of (
8.38
) separately. Recall the definition of
p({\tau},z)
in (
3.3
). By Lemma
7.6
, we can write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}[u_{0}(t-{\varepsilon}^{2},W({\varepsilon}^{2}))]-\mathbb{E}_{y}[u_{0}(t-{\varepsilon}^{2},W({\varepsilon}^{2}))]

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}(p({\varepsilon}^{2},x-z)-p({\varepsilon}^{2},y-z))^{+}\,dz

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{0}\Big(|W({\varepsilon}^{2})|\leq|x-y|/2\Big).

Since
p({\tau},z)\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2{\pi}m{\tau}}}\;\forall\,z\in\mathbb{R},\,{\tau}>0
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}[u_{0}(t-{\varepsilon}^{2},W({\varepsilon}^{2}))]-\mathbb{E}_{y}[u_{0}(t-{\varepsilon}^{2},W({\varepsilon}^{2}))]\leq\frac{|x-y|}{{\varepsilon}\sqrt{2{\pi}m}}.

(8.39)

For the second term on the right-hand side of (
8.38
), we use Lemma
7.6
and the fact that
1-\exp(-r)\leq r
for every
r\geq 0
, obtaining

\displaystyle\Big(1-\exp(-{\varepsilon}^{2}(H_{\max}-H_{\min}))\Big)\mathbb{E}_{y}[u_{0}(t-{\varepsilon}^{2},W({\varepsilon}^{2}))]\leq{\varepsilon}^{2}(H_{\max}-H_{\min}).

(8.40)

By applying (
8.39
) and (
8.40
) to (
8.38
), for
t\geq 1
,
{\varepsilon}\in(0,1)
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
with
|x-y|\leq{\varepsilon}^{3}
, we get

u_{0}(t,x)-u_{0}(t,y)\leq\exp(H_{\max})\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2{\pi}m}}+H_{\max}-H_{\min}\right){\varepsilon}^{2}.

(8.41)

Therefore, by taking

{\varepsilon}_{0}\leq 1\wedge\left(\exp(H_{\max})\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2{\pi}m}}+H_{\max}-H_{\min}\right)\right)^{-1},

the result follows directly from (
8.41
).
∎

In the remainder of this subsection, take
A>0
as given in Corollary
8.7
, and let

U_{\textrm{eq}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\min\Big\{U\geq 0:\,(1-{\mu})q_{+}(U)-q_{-}(U)=0\Big\}.

(8.42)

Observe that, since the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is assumed to be of Fisher-KPP type in the sense of Definition
2.3
, we have
U_{\textrm{eq}}\in(0,1)
and
U_{\textrm{eq}}
is the smallest strictly positive equilibrium state of the ODE:

\frac{d}{dt}U=U((1-{\mu})q_{+}(U)-q_{-}(U)).

We now prove that for any
{\delta}>0
, if
u_{0}(T,x)
is sufficiently small, then
u_{0}
grows exponentially fast until at some time
T+t
, there is some
y\in\mathbb{R}
close to
x
such that
u_{0}(T+t,y)\geq\frac{U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}}{1+A}
. This result is analogous to
[
64
, Lemma 2.3]
.

Lemma 8.9
.

Suppose the assumptions of Lemma
8.8
hold. Let
u=(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
denote the continuous mild solution to (
2.11
) given in Proposition
7.2
, let
A>0
denote the constant given in Corollary
8.7
and let
{\delta}\in(0,U_{\textrm{eq}})
. Then, there exist positive real numbers
{\tau}={\tau}({\delta})
,
R=R({\delta})
and
z^{*}=z^{*}({\delta})
such that for
z\in(0,z^{*})
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, if
T\geq 1
and
u_{0}(T,x)>z
, then there exists
t\in[T,T+{\tau}\log(1/z)]
and
y\in[x-R,x+R]
such that
u_{0}(t,y)\geq\frac{U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}}{1+A}
.

Proof.

We will closely follow the proof of
[
64
, Lemma 2.3]
. Let
R>1
and
{\tau}>1
be large constants depending on
{\delta}
that we will later specify. Let
z^{*}\in(0,(2{\varepsilon}_{0})\wedge e^{-1})
to be specified later, where
{\varepsilon}_{0}
is defined in Lemma
8.8
, and suppose
z\in(0,z^{*})
,
T\geq 1
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
with
u_{0}(T,x)>z
. Following
[
64
]
, we divide the proof into two cases:

(1)

For each
t\in[T,T+{\tau}\log(1/z)]
and
y\in[x-R,x+R]
, we have

\|u(t,y)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}<U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}.

(8.43)

(2)

There exist
\tilde{t}\in[T,T+{\tau}\log(1/z)]
and
\tilde{y}\in[x-R,x+R]
such that

\|u(\tilde{t},\tilde{y})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\geq U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}.

(8.44)

Case
(1)
:
Since
u_{0}(T,x)>z
, and
z<z^{*}<2{\varepsilon}_{0}
, by Lemma
8.8
with
{\varepsilon}=z/2
, we have that

u_{0}(T,y)>\tfrac{1}{2}z\quad\forall\,y\in\left[x-\tfrac{1}{8}z^{3},x+\tfrac{1}{8}z^{3}\right].

(8.45)

Let

H_{\min,{\delta}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\min_{U\in[0,U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}]}\,\Big((1-{\mu})q_{+}(U)-q_{-}(U)\Big)>0,

(8.46)

where the inequality holds by (
8.42
) and our assumption that
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is of Fisher-KPP type in the sense of Definition
2.3
. Then, by (
8.43
) and (
8.46
), on the event that
|W(t)-x|\leq R
for all
t\in[0,{\tau}\log(1/z)]
, we have

\int_{0}^{{\tau}\log(1/z)}\Big((1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-}\Big)\Big(\|u(T+{\tau}\log(1/z)-t,W(t))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big)\,dt\geq{\tau}\log(1/z)H_{\min,{\delta}}.

(8.47)

Hence, by combining the Feynman-Kac representation in (
8.1
) with (
8.45
) and (
8.47
), we have

\displaystyle u_{0}(T+{\tau}\log(1/z),x)

(8.48)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{z}{2}\exp\left({\tau}\log(1/z)H_{\min,{\delta}}\right)\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(|W(t)-x|\leq R\;\forall t\leq{\tau}\log(1/z),\;|W({\tau}\log(1/z))-x|\leq\frac{z^{3}}{8}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{z}{2}\exp\left({\tau}\log(1/z)H_{\min,{\delta}}\right)\mathbb{P}_{0}\left(|W(t)|\leq 1\;\forall t\leq{\tau}R^{-2}\log(1/z),\;|W({\tau}R^{-2}\log(1/z))|\leq\frac{z^{3}}{8R}\right),

where the last line holds due to Brownian scaling. Let
C=C^{(1)}_{1}>0
denote the constant given in Lemma
0.A.6
(iii) in the appendix, and recall from the start of the proof that
{\tau}>1
,
\log(1/z)>1
and
z^{3}/(8R)<2/3
. Therefore, by applying the Brownian motion estimate (
0.A.15
) from Lemma
0.A.6
(iii) to (
8.48
), we conclude that

\displaystyle u_{0}(T+{\tau}\log(1/z),x)\geq\frac{z}{2}\cdot\frac{z^{3}}{8R}\cdot C\cdot\exp\left({\tau}\log(1/z)H_{\min,{\delta}}-\frac{{\pi}^{2}m{\tau}}{8R^{2}}\log(1/z)\right)=\frac{C}{16R}z^{a},

(8.49)

where

a\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{-8{\tau}H_{\min,{\delta}}+{\pi}^{2}m{\tau}R^{-2}+32}{8}.

(8.50)

Hence, by taking
R>1
,
{\tau}>1
large enough that the following estimates hold:

R^{2}>\frac{{\pi}^{2}m}{8H_{\min,{\delta}}}\textrm{ and }{\tau}>\frac{32}{8H_{\min,{\delta}}-{\pi}^{2}mR^{-2}},

(8.51)

we conclude from (
8.50
) that
a<0
. Therefore, by (
8.49
), we can choose
z^{*}=z^{*}({\delta})>0
to be sufficiently small that for
z\in(0,z^{*})
, we have

u_{0}(T+{\tau}\log(1/z),x)\geq\frac{U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}}{1+A},

which completes the analysis of case (1).

Case
(2)
:
By Corollary
8.7
and then by (
8.44
), we have

u_{0}(\tilde{t},\tilde{y})\geq\frac{\|u(\tilde{t},\tilde{y})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}}{1+A}\geq\frac{U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}}{1+A},

which completes the proof.
∎

As in the statement of Theorem
2.7
, let

c^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sqrt{2m\Big((1-{\mu})q_{+}(0)-q_{-}(0)\Big)}>0.

(8.52)

Our next result shows that for any
{\varepsilon}>0
,
u_{0}
spreads with speed at least
c^{*}-{\varepsilon}
. Its proof is an adaptation of
[
64
, Lemma 2.4]
, with the modification that in our setting the non-local interaction occurs in the type space, i.e. in terms of numbers of mutations, rather than in the spatial domain, and that the polynomials
q_{+}
and
q_{-}
must satisfy the Fisher-KPP condition (
2.14
) in Definition
2.3
, which generalises the classical Fisher-KPP condition.

Lemma 8.10
.

Suppose the assumptions of Lemma
8.8
hold. Let
u=(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
denote the continuous mild solution to (
2.11
) given in Proposition
7.2
, and let
A>0
denote the constant given in Corollary
8.7
. For
c\in(0,c^{*})
, there exist
{{\nu}}^{*}_{0}={{\nu}}^{*}_{0}(c)\in\left(0,\frac{U_{\textrm{eq}}}{2(1+A)}\right)
and
{t}^{*}={t}^{*}(c)<\infty
such that for
T\geq{t}^{*}
and
t\geq 1
, if
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
with
u_{0}(t,x)\geq{{\nu}}^{*}_{0}
and
|x-x^{\prime}|\leq cT
, then
u_{0}(t+T,x^{\prime})\geq{{\nu}}^{*}_{0}
.

Proof.

We start by defining certain constants; the reasons for the choice of constants will become clear later. Recall the definition of
U_{\textrm{eq}}
in (
8.42
). Fix
c\in(0,c^{*})
, and let
{\delta}={\delta}(c)\in(0,U_{\textrm{eq}})
be such that

H_{\min,{\delta}}>\frac{c^{2}}{2m},

(8.53)

where
H_{\min,{\delta}}
is defined in (
8.46
). Indeed, by (
8.52
) we have

(1-{\mu})q_{+}(0)-q_{-}(0)=\frac{(c^{*})^{2}}{2m}>\frac{c^{2}}{2m},

and so it is always possible to choose
{\delta}\in(0,U_{\textrm{eq}})
such that (
8.53
) holds. Let
R>2
be sufficiently large that

H_{\min,{\delta}}-\frac{c^{2}}{2m}-\frac{{\pi}^{2}m}{8(R-1)^{2}}>0.

(8.54)

By Lemma
8.8
, there exists
{{\varepsilon}}^{(1)}\in(0,1)
sufficiently small that for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
with
|x-y|\leq{{\varepsilon}}^{(1)}
and any
{\tau}\geq 1
, we have

|u_{0}({\tau},x)-u_{0}({\tau},y)|\leq\frac{U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}}{2(1+A)}.

(8.55)

We then take
{{\nu}}^{*}_{0}>0
small enough that

{{\nu}}^{*}_{0}\leq\frac{(U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}){{\varepsilon}}^{(1)}}{(1+A)\sqrt{6m{\pi}}}\exp\left(3H_{\min}-\frac{(3c+R+1)^{2}}{2m}\right),

(8.56)

where
H_{\min}
is defined in (
8.35
). Let
C^{(2)}_{R-1}=C^{(2)}_{R-1}(c^{*})>0
be the constant defined in Lemma
0.A.6
(iv) in the appendix for
estimate (
0.A.16
). We will also assume that
{{\nu}}^{*}_{0}
is sufficiently small that

{{\nu}}^{*}_{0}\leq e^{2H_{\min}}\frac{(U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta})C^{(2)}_{R-1}}{4(1+A)}\mathbb{P}_{c+R+1}\Big(|W(1)|\leq{{\varepsilon}}^{(1)}\Big)\mathbb{P}_{0}\left(|W(1)+c|\leq\frac{1}{2}\right).

(8.57)

Applying Lemma
8.8
again, let
{\varepsilon}^{(2)}\in(0,1)
be sufficiently small that for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
with
|x-y|\leq{{\varepsilon}}^{(2)}
and any
{\tau}\geq 1
, we have

|u_{0}({\tau},x)-u_{0}({\tau},y)|\leq{{{\nu}}^{*}_{0}}/{2}.

(8.58)

Finally, by (
8.54
), we can take
{t}^{*}>2
sufficiently large that

\frac{{\varepsilon}^{(2)}}{4}C^{(2)}_{R-1}\exp\left((t^{*}-1)\left(H_{\min,{\delta}}-\frac{c^{2}}{2m}-\frac{{\pi}^{2}m}{8(R-1)^{2}}\right)\right)>e^{-H_{\min}}\mathbb{P}_{0}\Big(|W(1)-c|\leq{\varepsilon}^{(2)}/2\Big)^{-1}.

(8.59)

Observe that since the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is monostable, Definition
2.3
and (
8.35
) imply that
H_{\min}<0
, and so
e^{-H_{\min}}>1
.

We are finally ready to start the proof. Take
T\geq t^{*}>2
,
t\geq 1
and
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
with
|x-x^{\prime}|\leq cT
, and let
a\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(x^{\prime}-x)/T
. Suppose that
u_{0}(t,x)\geq{\nu}^{*}_{0}
. Following the proof of
[
64
, Lemma 2.4]
, we will consider three different cases:

(1)

For each
{\tau}\in[0,T-1]
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
with
|y-(x+a{\tau})|\leq R
, we have
\|u(t+{\tau},y)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}<U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}
.

(2)

There exist
\tilde{{\tau}}\in[T-3,T-1]
and
\tilde{y}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
|\tilde{y}-(x+a\tilde{{\tau}})|\leq R
and
\|u(t+\tilde{{\tau}},\tilde{y})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\geq U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}
.

(3)

There exist
\tilde{{\tau}}\in[0,T-3]
and
\tilde{y}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
|\tilde{y}-(x+a\tilde{{\tau}})|\leq R
and
\|u(t+\tilde{{\tau}},\tilde{y})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\geq U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}
, and such that for any
({\tau},y)
with
{\tau}\in[\tilde{{\tau}}+1,T-1]
and
|y-(x+a{\tau})|\leq R
, we have
\|u(t+{\tau},y)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}<U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}
.

For simplicity, we assume that
x^{\prime}\geq x
, and so
a\in[0,c]
, since the proof for
x^{\prime}\leq x
is analogous. We will address each of the cases listed separately.

Case
(1)
:
Take
{\tau}\in[0,T-1]
and suppose
|W({\tau})-(x+a(T-1-{\tau}))|\leq R
; then by the assumptions of case
(1)
, we have
\|u(t+T-1-{\tau},W({\tau}))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}<U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}
. Hence, by (
8.46
) we have

((1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-})(\|u(t+T-1-{\tau},W({\tau}))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})\geq H_{\min,{\delta}}.

(8.60)

Moreover, by (
8.58
) and the fact that
t\geq 1
and
u_{0}(t,x)\geq{{\nu}}^{*}_{0}
, we also have that

u_{0}(t,y)\geq\tfrac{1}{2}{{\nu}}^{*}_{0}\quad\forall y\in[x-{\varepsilon}^{(2)},x+{\varepsilon}^{(2)}].

(8.61)

By combining the Feynman-Kac representation in (
8.1
) with (
8.60
) and (
8.61
), we conclude that for any
y\in[x-{\varepsilon}^{(2)}/2,x+{\varepsilon}^{(2)}/2]
, we have

\displaystyle u_{0}(t+T-1,y+a(T-1))

(8.62)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{{{\nu}}^{*}_{0}}{2}\exp\Big((T-1)H_{\min,{\delta}}\Big)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathbb{P}_{y+a(T-1)}\Big(|W({\tau})-(x+a(T-1-{\tau}))|\leq R\;\forall{\tau}\leq T-1,\,|W(T-1)-x|\leq{\varepsilon}^{(2)}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{{{\nu}}^{*}_{0}}{2}\exp\Big((T-1)H_{\min,{\delta}}\Big)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathbb{P}_{0}\left(|W({\tau})+a{\tau}|\leq R-1\;\forall{\tau}\leq T-1,\,|W(T-1)+a(T-1)|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}{\varepsilon}^{(2)}\right),

where for the second inequality we used the fact that
|x-y|\leq{\varepsilon}^{(2)}/2
and
{\varepsilon}^{(2)}<1
. Note that since
|x^{\prime}-x|\leq cT
, we have
|a|\leq c
. Thus, applying estimate (
0.A.16
) from Lemma
0.A.6
(iv) in the appendix to (
8.62
), we conclude that

\displaystyle u_{0}(t+T-1,y+a(T-1))

\displaystyle\geq\frac{{{\nu}}^{*}_{0}{\varepsilon}^{(2)}}{4}C^{(2)}_{R-1}\exp\left((T-1)\left(H_{\min,{\delta}}-\frac{a^{2}}{2m}-\frac{{\pi}^{2}m}{8(R-1)^{2}}\right)\right)

(8.63)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{{{\nu}}^{*}_{0}{\varepsilon}^{(2)}}{4}C^{(2)}_{R-1}\exp\left((t^{*}-1)\left(H_{\min,{\delta}}-\frac{c^{2}}{2m}-\frac{{\pi}^{2}m}{8(R-1)^{2}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle>{{\nu}}^{*}_{0}e^{-H_{\min}}\mathbb{P}_{0}\Big(|W(1)-c|\leq{\varepsilon}^{(2)}/2\Big)^{-1},

where in the second inequality we used the fact that
T\geq t^{*}
and (
8.54
), and in the third inequality we used (
8.59
). Since (
8.63
) holds for any
y\in[x-{\varepsilon}^{(2)}/2,x+{\varepsilon}^{(2)}/2]
and since
x^{\prime}=x+aT
, by using the Feynman-Kac representation in (
8.1
), Lemma
7.6
and the definition of
H_{\min}
in (
8.35
), and then using (
8.63
), we conclude that

\displaystyle u_{0}(t+T,x^{\prime})

(8.64)

\displaystyle\geq e^{H_{\min}}\mathbb{P}_{x+aT}\Big(|W(1)-x-a(T-1)|\leq{\varepsilon}^{(2)}/2\Big)\inf_{|y-x|\leq{\varepsilon}^{(2)}/2}u_{0}(t+T-1,y+a(T-1))

\displaystyle\geq e^{H_{\min}}\mathbb{P}_{0}\Big(|W(1)+a|\leq{\varepsilon}^{(2)}/2\Big){{\nu}}^{*}_{0}e^{-H_{\min}}\mathbb{P}_{0}\Big(|W(1)-c|\leq{\varepsilon}^{(2)}/2\Big)^{-1}

\displaystyle>{{\nu}}^{*}_{0},

where for the last inequality we used the fact that
0\leq a\leq c
. This completes the analysis of case
(1)
.

Case
(2)
:
By the assumptions of case
(2)
and by Corollary
8.7
, we have

u_{0}(t+\tilde{{\tau}},\tilde{y})\geq\frac{U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}}{1+A}.

Hence, by (
8.55
) and since
t\geq 1
, we have

u_{0}(t+\tilde{{\tau}},y)\geq\frac{U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}}{2(1+A)}\quad\forall y\in[\tilde{y}-{{\varepsilon}}^{(1)},\tilde{y}+{{\varepsilon}}^{(1)}].

(8.65)

Since, by the assumptions of case (2),
T-\tilde{{\tau}}\leq 3
and since
x^{\prime}=x+aT
, by using the Feynman-Kac representation in (
8.1
), and then by applying (
8.65
), Lemma
7.6
, the definition of
H_{\min}
in (
8.35
), and our observation after (
8.59
) that
H_{\min}<0
, we conclude that

\displaystyle u_{0}(t+T,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq e^{3H_{\min}}\frac{U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}}{2(1+A)}\mathbb{P}_{x+aT}\Big(|W(T-\tilde{{\tau}})-\tilde{y}|\leq{{\varepsilon}}^{(1)}\Big)

(8.66)

\displaystyle=e^{3H_{\min}}\frac{U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}}{2(1+A)}\mathbb{P}_{0}\Big(|W(T-\tilde{{\tau}})-(\tilde{y}-x-aT)|\leq{{\varepsilon}}^{(1)}\Big).

Then since, by the triangle inequality and the assumptions of case (2), we have

|\tilde{y}-x-aT|\leq|\tilde{y}-(x+a\tilde{{\tau}})|+|a(\tilde{{\tau}}-T)|\leq R+3a,

it follows from (
8.66
) that

\displaystyle u_{0}(t+T,x^{\prime})\geq e^{3H_{\min}}\frac{U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}}{2(1+A)}\mathbb{P}_{0}\Big(|W(T-\tilde{{\tau}})+3a+R|\leq{{\varepsilon}}^{(1)}\Big).

(8.67)

Hence, by applying the standard Brownian motion estimate (
0.A.14
) from Lemma
0.A.6
in the appendix, and the fact that
1\leq T-\tilde{{\tau}}\leq 3
,
0\leq a\leq c
and
{\varepsilon}^{(1)}<1
to (
8.67
), we conclude that

\displaystyle u_{0}(t+T,x^{\prime})\geq\frac{(U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}){{\varepsilon}}^{(1)}}{(1+A)\sqrt{6m{\pi}}}\exp\left(3H_{\min}-\frac{(3c+R+1)^{2}}{2m}\right)\geq{{\nu}}^{*}_{0},

where for the second inequality we used (
8.56
). This completes the proof for case
(2)
.

Case
(3)
:
Observe that by the same argument as in case (2), (
8.65
) holds. Now take
y\in[x-1,x+1]
. By the Feynman-Kac representation in (
8.1
), Lemma
7.6
, the definition of
H_{\min}
in (
8.35
), and (
8.65
), we have

\displaystyle u_{0}(t+\tilde{{\tau}}+1,y+a(\tilde{{\tau}}+1))\geq e^{H_{\min}}\frac{U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}}{2(1+A)}\mathbb{P}_{y+a(\tilde{{\tau}}+1)}\Big(|W(1)-\tilde{y}|\leq{{\varepsilon}}^{(1)}\Big).

(8.68)

Since, by the assumptions of case (3),
|\tilde{y}-(x+a\tilde{{\tau}})|\leq R
, and since
0\leq a\leq c
and
|x-y|\leq 1
, we have

|\tilde{y}-(y+a(\tilde{{\tau}}+1))|\leq c+R+1,

and we conclude from (
8.68
) that

u_{0}(t+\tilde{{\tau}}+1,y+a(\tilde{{\tau}}+1))\geq e^{H_{\min}}\frac{U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}}{2(1+A)}\mathbb{P}_{c+R+1}\Big(|W(1)|\leq{{\varepsilon}}^{(1)}\Big).

(8.69)

By the assumptions of case (3), for
{\tau}\in[0,T-\tilde{{\tau}}-2]
, if
|W({\tau})-(x+a(T-1-{\tau}))|\leq R
, we have
\|u(t+T-1-{\tau},W({\tau}))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}<U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta}
. Hence, by (
8.46
), if
|W({\tau})-(x+a(T-1-{\tau}))|\leq R
for some
{\tau}\in[0,T-\tilde{{\tau}}-2]
, we have

((1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-})(\|u(t+T-1-{\tau},W({\tau}))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})\geq H_{\min,{\delta}}.

(8.70)

Hence, for
y^{\prime}\in[x-1/2,x+1/2]
, by applying (
8.70
) to (
8.1
), we have

\displaystyle u_{0}(t+T-1,y^{\prime}+a(T-1))

(8.71)

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{y\in[x-1,x+1]}u_{0}(t+\tilde{{\tau}}+1,y+a(\tilde{{\tau}}+1))\exp\Big(H_{\min,{\delta}}(T-\tilde{{\tau}}-2)\Big)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathbb{P}_{y^{\prime}+a(T-1)}\Big(|W({\tau})-(y^{\prime}+a(T-1-{\tau}))|\leq R-1\;\forall{\tau}\leq T-\tilde{{\tau}}-2,

\displaystyle|W(T-\tilde{{\tau}}-2)-(y^{\prime}+a(\tilde{{\tau}}+1))|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\Big).

Since
T-\tilde{{\tau}}-2\geq 1
and
0\leq a\leq c
, by applying the Brownian motion estimate (
0.A.16
) from Lemma
0.A.6
in the appendix together with (
8.69
) to (
8.71
), we conclude that, for any
y^{\prime}\in[x-1/2,x+1/2]
,

\displaystyle u_{0}(t+T-1,y^{\prime}+a(T-1))

\displaystyle\geq e^{H_{\min}}\frac{(U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta})C^{(2)}_{R-1}}{4(1+A)}\mathbb{P}_{c+R+1}\Big(|W(1)|\leq{{\varepsilon}}^{(1)}\Big)

(8.72)

\displaystyle\cdot\exp\left((T-\tilde{{\tau}}-2)\left(H_{\min,{\delta}}-\frac{c^{2}}{2m}-\frac{{\pi}^{2}m}{8(R-1)^{2}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\geq e^{H_{\min}}\frac{(U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta})C^{(2)}_{R-1}}{4(1+A)}\mathbb{P}_{c+R+1}\Big(|W(1)|\leq{{\varepsilon}}^{(1)}\Big),

where for the second inequality we used the fact that
T-\tilde{{\tau}}-2>0
and (
8.54
). Finally, by the Feynman-Kac representation in (
8.1
), again together with Lemma
7.6
and (
8.35
), and then using that
x^{\prime}=x+aT
and
0\leq a\leq c
and using (
8.72
), we conclude that

\displaystyle u_{0}(t+T,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq e^{H_{\min}}\inf_{|y^{\prime}-x|\leq 1/2}u_{0}(t+T-1,y^{\prime}+a(T-1))\cdot\mathbb{P}_{x^{\prime}}\left(|W(1)-(x+a(T-1))|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq e^{2H_{\min}}\frac{(U_{\textrm{eq}}-{\delta})C^{(2)}_{R-1}}{4(1+A)}\mathbb{P}_{c+R+1}\Big(|W(1)|\leq{{\varepsilon}}^{(1)}\Big)\mathbb{P}_{0}\left(|W(1)+c|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq{{\nu}}^{*}_{0},

where for the last inequality we used (
8.57
). This completes the proof in case
(3)
.

Since in each of cases (1)-(3) we have
u_{0}(t+T,x^{\prime})\geq{\nu}^{*}_{0}
, the proof is complete.
∎

We are finally ready to prove Theorem
2.7
. Our proof is similar to the proof of
[
64
, Theorem 1.1]
.

Proof of Theorem
2.7
.

Recall the definition of
c^{*}
in (
8.52
). We will first establish an upper bound on the propagation speed for
u_{0}
, i.e. we will show that

\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\,\sup_{x\geq c^{*}T}u_{0}(T,x)=0.

(8.73)

Observe that, by Corollary
8.7
, if (
8.73
) holds, then we must have

\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\,\sup_{x\geq c^{*}T}\|u(T,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}=0.

In order to prove (
8.73
), recall from the assumptions of the theorem that there exists
R>0
such that
\supp f_{0}\subseteq(-\infty,R]
, where
f=(f_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
is the initial condition to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) satisfying Assumptions
4
and
5
. Recall the definition of
H_{\max}
in (
8.35
), and observe that

c^{*}=({2mH_{\max}})^{1/2}.

(8.74)

Recall that since the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is of Fisher-KPP type in the sense of Definition
2.3
, we have that (
8.36
) holds. Therefore, by the Feynman-Kac representation in (
8.3
), and by Lemma
7.6
, we have that for any
T\geq 0
and any
x\geq c^{*}T
,

\displaystyle u_{0}\left(T,x\right)

\displaystyle\leq e^{H_{\max}{T}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[f_{0}(W(T))\right]

(8.75)

\displaystyle\leq e^{H_{\max}{T}}\|f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\mathbb{P}_{0}(W(T)\geq c^{*}T-R).

Observe that by the standard tail Gaussian estimate (
0.A.13
) from Lemma
0.A.6
in the appendix, for
T>R/c^{*}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{0}(W(T)\geq c^{*}T-R)\leq\frac{(mT)^{1/2}}{(c^{*}T-R)\sqrt{2{\pi}}}\exp\left(-\frac{(c^{*}T-R)^{2}}{2mT}\right),

and so for
T>R/c^{*}
, (
8.75
) combined with (
8.74
) yields

\displaystyle\sup_{x\geq c^{*}T}u_{0}\left(T,x\right)

\displaystyle\leq\|f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\frac{(mT)^{1/2}}{(c^{*}T-R)\sqrt{2{\pi}}}\exp\left(\frac{(c^{*})^{2}T}{2m}-\frac{(c^{*}T-R)^{2}}{2mT}\right)

(8.76)

\displaystyle=\|f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\frac{(mT)^{1/2}}{(c^{*}T-R)\sqrt{2{\pi}}}\exp\left(\frac{c^{*}R}{m}-\frac{R^{2}}{2mT}\right).

Taking
T\rightarrow\infty
on both sides of (
8.76
), we conclude that (
8.73
) holds.

For the lower bound on the spreading speed, by Theorem
2.5
, it is enough to establish that there exists
{\nu}_{0}>0
such that for any
{\varepsilon}\in(0,c^{*})
,

\liminf_{T\rightarrow\infty}\;\inf_{x\in[0,(c^{*}-{\varepsilon})T]}\;u_{0}(T,x)\geq{\nu}_{0}.

(8.77)

To prove (
8.77
), we use the same argument as in the proof of the lower bound on the spreading speed in
[
64
, Theorem 1.1]
, replacing Lemmas 2.3 and 2.5 in
[
64
]
by Lemmas
8.9
and
8.10
respectively. Since the arguments are the same, we omit the proof and refer the interested reader to
[
64
]
.
∎

8.4
Tracer dynamics

In this subsection, we will prove Theorem
2.9
. Throughout this subsection, let
u
denote the continuous mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) given in Proposition
7.2
. We will assume that the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
defined in (
2.12
) is monostable in the sense of Definition
2.3
, and that the initial condition
f=(f_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
of the system of PDEs (
2.11
) satisfies both Assumptions
4
and
5
. Recall the system of PDEs for the labelled population (
2.25
), and suppose
f^{*}=(f^{*}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
satisfies Assumption
6
, recalling that
f^{*}
is the initial condition of the system of PDEs (
2.25
). Recall that
F^{*}=(F^{*}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}\times{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
is the reaction term defined in (
2.26
). We start by introducing the definition of mild solutions to the system of PDEs (
2.25
) analogously to the definition of a mild solution of (
2.25
) in Definition
3.5
.

Definition 8.11
.

Suppose that
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
,
q_{+}
and
q_{-}
satisfy Assumptions
2
and
3
, that
{\mu}\in(0,1)
and
m>0
, and that
f
and
f^{*}
satisfy Assumption
6
, and that the reaction term
F=(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
defined in (
2.12
) is monostable in the sense of Definition
2.3
. Let
u=(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
be the continuous mild solution to (
2.11
) given in Proposition
7.2
. We say that a Lebesgue-measurable function
u^{*}=(u^{*}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
is a
mild solution
to the system of PDEs (
2.25
) if and only if the following conditions are satisfied:

(i)

For
{\lambda}
-almost every
(T,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
, for
{\lambda}
-almost every
t\in[0,T]
,

\Big(P_{T-t}\|F^{*}(u(t,\cdot),u^{*}(t,\cdot))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big)(x)<\infty.

(ii)

For
{\lambda}
-almost every
(T,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
,

u^{*}(T,x)=(P_{T}f^{*})(x)+\int_{0}^{T}\Big(P_{T-t}F^{*}(u(t,\cdot),u^{*}(t,\cdot))\Big)(x)\,dt.

Our first step towards the proof of Theorem
2.9
will be to state existence, uniqueness and regularity properties for solutions of (
2.25
).

Proposition 8.12
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
2.9
, letting
u
denote the continuous mild solution to the system of PDEs (
2.11
) given in Proposition
7.2
, there exists a unique function
u^{*}=(u^{*}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
such that:

(i)

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
(T,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
,

u_{k}^{*}(T,x)=(P_{T}f_{k}^{*})(x)+\int_{0}^{T}\Big(P_{T-t}F_{k}^{*}(u(t,\cdot),u^{*}(t,\cdot))\Big)(x)\,dt.

(8.78)

(ii)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|u^{*}(t,\cdot)\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}<\infty
for all
T>0
.

(iii)

u^{*}\in\mathscr{C}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
.

(iv)

u^{*}_{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{1,2}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Moreover,
u^{*}=(u^{*}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
satisfies the following inequality: for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
T\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

0\leq u^{*}_{k}(T,x)\leq u_{k}(T,x).

(8.79)

Since the proof of Proposition
8.12
follows from standard arguments, we postpone it until Section
0.A.5
in the appendix.

In the remainder of this subsection, we let
u^{*}=(u^{*}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
denote the unique solution of (
2.25
) satisfying the conditions of Proposition
8.12
. Our next step will be to write
u^{*}_{k}
in terms of a Feynman-Kac representation for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Lemma 8.13
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
2.9
, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, any
T\geq 0
, any
t\in[0,T]
and any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle u_{k}^{*}(T,x)=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[f^{*}_{k}(W(T))\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}\Big(s_{k}(1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-}\Big)\Big(\|u(T-t,W(t))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big)dt\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\mathds{1}_{\{k\geq 1\}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\Big(s_{k-1}{\mu}u_{k-1}^{*}q_{+}(\|u\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}})\Big)(T-t,W(t))

\displaystyle\cdot\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\Big(s_{k}(1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-}\Big)\Big(\|u(T-{\tau},W({\tau}))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big)d{\tau}\right)\;dt\Bigg].

Proof.

Similarly to the proof of Lemma
8.2
, the result follows directly from Proposition
8.1
, Proposition
8.12
, Lemma
7.6
and the definition of the reaction term
F^{*}=(F^{*}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
in (
2.26
).
∎

Our next result establishes an upper bound on the propagation of the labelled population that will be pivotal in the proof of Theorem
2.9
.
Before stating this result, we recall the sequence
(\hat{{\pi}}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
defined in Assumption
5
(iii), and the sequence of functions
{\phi}=({\phi}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
defined in Lemma
8.5
. We also recall the definition of
\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}
and
\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}
in (
2.17
) and that the sequence of fitness parameters
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
satisfies Assumption
2
.

Lemma 8.14
.

Suppose that the assumoptions of Theorem
2.9
hold and that
f^{*}_{0}\equiv 0
. Then
u^{*}_{0}(T,\cdot)\equiv 0
for any
T\geq 0
. Moreover, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, any
T\geq 0
and any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

u^{*}_{k}(T,x)\leq\Big(\hat{{\pi}}_{k}\exp\Big(-T\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k})(1-{\mu})\Big)+{\phi}_{k}(T)\Big)u_{0}(T,x).

(8.80)

Proof.

The fact that
u^{*}_{0}(T,\cdot)\equiv 0
for any
T\geq 0
follows directly from Lemma
8.13
and the assumption that
f^{*}_{0}\equiv 0
. For the proof of (
8.80
), we will use an induction argument on
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Since the proof of (
8.80
) is similar to the proof of the upper bound in Lemma
8.6
, we will omit some of the details. For
k=1
, since
u^{*}_{0}\equiv 0
, by the Feynman-Kac representation given in Lemma
8.13
, and by Assumptions
5
(iii) and
6
(iii), we have that for any
T\geq 0
and any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle u^{*}_{1}(T,x)

\displaystyle\leq\hat{{\pi}}_{1}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[f_{0}(W(T))\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}\Big(s_{1}(1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-}\Big)\Big(\|u(T-t,W(t))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big)dt\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\hat{{\pi}}_{1}\exp\Big(-T\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{1})(1-{\mu})\Big)u_{0}(T,x),

where for the second inequality we used (
8.25
). Hence, (
8.80
) holds for
k=1
. Now take
k\in\mathbb{N}
and suppose that (
8.80
) holds for all
T\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Then by Lemma
8.13
, Assumptions
5
(iii) and
6
(iii), and our assumption that (
8.80
) holds, and using (
8.22
) and Lemma
8.2
, we have for any
T\geq 0
and any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle u^{*}_{k+1}(T,x)

(8.81)

\displaystyle\leq\hat{{\pi}}_{k+1}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[f_{0}(W(T))\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}\Big(s_{k+1}(1-{\mu})q_{+}-q_{-}\Big)\Big(\|u(T-t,W(t))\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\Big)dt\right)\right]

\displaystyle+{\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}s_{k}\hat{{\pi}}_{k}u_{0}(T,x)\int_{0}^{T}\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k+1})(1-{\mu})-(T-t)\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k})(1-{\mu})\Big)\,dt

\displaystyle+{\mu}\mathfrak{Q}_{\max}s_{k}u_{0}(T,x)\int_{0}^{T}\exp\Big(-t\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{k+1})(1-{\mu})\Big){\phi}_{k}(T-t)\,dt.

Using (
8.28
) to bound the first term on the right-hand side of (
8.81
), and (
8.32
) to bound the sum of the second and third terms on the right-hand side of (
8.81
), we conclude that (
8.80
) holds for
u^{*}_{k+1}
. Therefore, by induction, our claim holds.
∎

We are now ready to prove Theorem
2.9
.

Proof of Theorem
2.9
.

The claimed existence, uniqueness and regularity properties of
u^{*}
follow from Proposition
8.12
. To complete the proof, it remains to show that
\|u^{*}(T,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}
converges uniformly (in space) to
0
as
T\rightarrow\infty
. Since
(\hat{{\pi}}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
by Assumption
5
(iii), and since
(s_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is monotonically decreasing with
s_{1}<1
by Assumption
2
and Definition
2.3
, by summing both sides of (
8.80
) in Lemma
8.14
and then applying Lemmas
7.6
and
8.5
, we conclude that for any
T\geq 0
,

\|u^{*}(T,\cdot)\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\leq\|\hat{{\pi}}\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\exp\Big(-T\mathfrak{Q}_{\min}(1-s_{1})(1-{\mu})\Big)+{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}(T),

(8.82)

where
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 8\relax}}}(T)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\phi}_{k}(T)
. By taking
T\rightarrow\infty
on both sides of (
8.82
) and then using (
8.12
) from Lemma
8.5
, we complete the proof.
∎

Appendix 0.A
Appendix

0.A.1
Stochastic chain rule

In this subsection, we will state a general stochastic chain rule formula. In what follows, let
(\mathcal{S},d_{\mathcal{S}})
be some complete and separable metric space, let
A\subseteq\mathscr{C}(\mathcal{S};\mathbb{R})
be some vector subspace of real-valued continuous functions defined on
\mathcal{S}
, let
\mathcal{L}\Big|_{A}:A\rightarrow\mathscr{C}(\mathcal{S};\mathbb{R})
be a (possibly unbounded) operator and let
({\eta}(t))_{t\geq 0}
be an
\mathcal{S}
-valued càdlàg process with initial distribution
{\nu}
, which is strongly Markovian with respect to its right-continuous natural filtration
\{\mathcal{F}^{{\eta}}_{t+}\}_{t\geq 0}
. We will assume that
\mathcal{L}
is the infinitesimal generator of
({\eta}(t))_{t\geq 0}
, and that
({\eta}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a solution of the martingale problem for
(\mathcal{L},A,{\nu})
, i.e. for any
{\phi}\in A
, the process
(M^{{\phi}}(t))_{t\geq 0}
given by, for all
T\geq 0
,

M^{{\phi}}(T)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\phi}({\eta}(T))-{\phi}({\eta}(0))-\int_{0}^{T}(\mathcal{L}{\phi})({\eta}(t-))\,dt

is a càdlàg martingale with respect to the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}^{{\eta}}_{t+}\}_{t\geq 0}
.

We begin by stating a general chain rule formula for Markov processes whose transition rates are not almost surely bounded. This result is classical for Markov processes with uniformly bounded transition rates. We prove the version that we state here in the companion article
[
56
, Lemma A.8]
.

Lemma 0.A.1
.

Let
T\geq 0
be fixed, and let
g\in\mathscr{C}([0,T]\times\mathcal{S};\mathbb{R})
. Suppose the following conditions hold:

(i)

\sup_{t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}_{{\nu}}\left[|g(t_{1},{\eta}(t_{2}))|\right]<\infty
.

(ii)

The map
[0,T]\times\mathcal{S}\ni(t,\boldsymbol{{\eta}})\mapsto\left(\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}t}g(\cdot,\boldsymbol{{\eta}})\right)(t)
is in
\mathscr{C}([0,T]\times\mathcal{S};\mathbb{R})
.

(iii)

For any
t\in[0,T]
, the map
\mathcal{S}\ni\boldsymbol{{\eta}}\mapsto g(t,\boldsymbol{{\eta}})
is in
A
, and the map
[0,T]\times\mathcal{S}\ni(t,\boldsymbol{{\eta}})\mapsto(\mathcal{L}g)(t,\cdot)(\boldsymbol{{\eta}})
is in
\mathscr{C}([0,T]\times\mathcal{S};\mathbb{R})
.

(iv)

\!\begin{aligned} \sup_{t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,T]}\;\mathbb{E}_{{\nu}}\Bigg[\left(\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}t}g(\cdot,{\eta}(t_{2}))\right)^{2}(t_{1})\Bigg]+\sup_{t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,T]}\;\mathbb{E}_{{\nu}}\Bigg[\Big(\mathcal{L}g(t_{1},\cdot)\Big)^{2}({\eta}(t_{2}))\Bigg]<\infty.\end{aligned}

Then the process
\Big(M^{g}(t\wedge T)\Big)_{t\geq 0}
given by, for all
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle M^{g}(t\wedge T)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=g\Big(t\wedge T,{\eta}(t\wedge T)\Big)-g(0,{\eta}(0))-\int_{0}^{t\wedge T}\left(\left(\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}t^{\prime}}g(\cdot,{\eta}(t^{\prime}-))\right)(t^{\prime})+\Big(\mathcal{L}g(t^{\prime},\cdot)\Big)({\eta}(t^{\prime}-))\right)\,dt^{\prime}

is a càdlàg martingale with respect to the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}^{{\eta}}_{t+}\}_{t\geq 0}
.

We also need an expression for the predictable bracket process of the martingale
M^{g}
introduced in Lemma
0.A.1
. This will be our next result. A similar claim is stated without proof in
[
21
, Theorem 2.6.3]
; we provide a proof here for completeness.

Lemma 0.A.2
.

Let
T\geq 0
be fixed, and let
g\in\mathscr{C}([0,T]\times\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R})
. Suppose that, in addition to the conditions (i)-(iv) of Lemma
0.A.1
, the following conditions hold:

(i)

For any
t\in[0,T]
, the map
\mathcal{S}\ni\boldsymbol{{\eta}}\mapsto g^{2}(t,\boldsymbol{{\eta}})
is in
A
, and the map
[0,T]\times\mathcal{S}\ni(t,\boldsymbol{{\eta}})\mapsto(\mathcal{L}g^{2})(t,\cdot)(\boldsymbol{{\eta}})
is
\mathscr{C}([0,T]\times\mathcal{S},\mathbb{R})
.

(ii)

\!\begin{aligned} \sup_{t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,T]}\;\mathbb{E}_{{\nu}}\Bigg[g^{2}(t_{1},{\eta}(t_{2}))+g^{2}(t_{1},{\eta}(t_{2}))\left(\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}t}g(\cdot,{\eta}(t_{2}))\right)^{2}(t_{1})+\Big(\mathcal{L}g^{2}(t_{1},\cdot)\Big)^{2}({\eta}(t_{2}))\Bigg]<\infty.\end{aligned}

Then the process
M^{g}
introduced in Lemma
0.A.1
is a square-integrable martingale, and its predictable bracket process
\Big(\langle M^{g}\rangle(t\wedge T)\Big)_{t\geq 0}
is given by, for all
t\geq 0
,

\langle M^{g}\rangle(t\wedge T)=\int_{0}^{t\wedge T}\Bigg(\Big(\mathcal{L}g^{2}(t^{\prime},\cdot)\Big)({\eta}(t^{\prime}-))-2g(t^{\prime},{\eta}(t^{\prime}-))\Big(\mathcal{L}g(t^{\prime},\cdot)\Big)({\eta}(t^{\prime}-))\Bigg)\,dt^{\prime}.

(0.A.1)

Proof.

Since the proof is a straightforward generalisation of the classical argument for Markov processes with uniformly bounded transition rates, we will omit the details and only point out how to adapt the usual proof to our scenario. Let
(A^{g}(t))_{t\geq 0}
denote the process given by the right-hand side of (
0.A.1
). Condition (iv) of Lemma
0.A.1
and condition (ii) of this lemma imply that the process
M^{g}
is a square-integrable martingale. Hence, to prove our claim, it will suffice to verify that the process
\left(Q^{g}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
given by, for all
t\geq 0
,

Q^{g}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Big(M^{g}(t\wedge T)\Big)^{2}-A^{g}(t\wedge T)

is a martingale with respect to the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}^{{\eta}}_{t+}\}_{t\geq 0}
. By observing that for all
T\geq 0
and càdlàg functions
a,b:[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
we have

\left(\int_{0}^{T}a(t-)b(t-)\,dt\right)^{2}\leq\left(\int_{0}^{T}a(t-)^{2}\,dt\right)\left(\int_{0}^{T}b(t-)^{2}\,dt\right)\leq T^{2}\sup_{t\leq T}a^{2}(t)\cdot\sup_{t\leq T}b^{2}(t),

we conclude from condition (iv) of Lemma
0.A.1
and condition (ii) of this lemma that
\left(Q^{g}(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}
is integrable. Also, observe that by the definition of the process
M^{g}
given by Lemma
0.A.1
, we have, after rearranging terms, for all
t\geq 0
,

\Big(M^{g}(t\wedge T)\Big)^{2}=A^{g,(1)}(t)+A^{g,(2)}(t),

(0.A.2)

where

\displaystyle A^{g,(1)}(t)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=g^{2}\Big(t\wedge T,{\eta}(t\wedge T)\Big)-g^{2}(0,{\eta}(0))

\displaystyle-2g\Big(t\wedge T,{\eta}(t\wedge T)\Big)\int_{0}^{t\wedge T}\left(\left(\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}t^{\prime}}g(\cdot,{\eta}(t^{\prime}-))\right)(t^{\prime})+\Big(\mathcal{L}g(t^{\prime},\cdot)\Big)({\eta}(t^{\prime}-))\right)\,dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle+\Bigg(\int_{0}^{t\wedge T}\left(\left(\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}t^{\prime}}g(\cdot,{\eta}(t^{\prime}-))\right)(t^{\prime})+\Big(\mathcal{L}g(t^{\prime},\cdot)\Big)({\eta}(t^{\prime}-))\right)\,dt^{\prime}\Bigg)^{2},

and

\displaystyle A^{g,(2)}(t)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=-2g(0,{\eta}(0))g\Big(t\wedge T,{\eta}(t\wedge T)\Big)+2g^{2}(0,{\eta}(0))

\displaystyle+2g(0,{\eta}(0))\int_{0}^{t\wedge T}\left(\left(\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}t^{\prime}}g(\cdot,{\eta}(t^{\prime}-))\right)(t^{\prime})+\Big(\mathcal{L}g(t^{\prime},\cdot)\Big)({\eta}(t^{\prime}-))\right)\,dt^{\prime}.

By Lemma
0.A.1
, we conclude that
(A^{g,(2)}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a càdlàg martingale. It then remains to analyse
(A^{g,(1)}(t))_{t\geq 0}
. For
t\in[0,T]
, let
A^{g}(t)
denote the term on the right-hand side of (
0.A.1
). Then
(A^{g}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is an increasing finite variation process. The fact that the process
\Big((A^{g,(1)}-A^{g})(t)\Big)_{t\geq 0}
is a martingale can be proved by an application of Itô’s formula as in the classical case, with the observation that one should replace the assumption in the classical case of uniform boundedness of
g
and of
\mathcal{L}g^{2}
by Lemma
0.A.1
and condition (ii) of this lemma to establish that the process

\Bigg(g^{2}\Big(t\wedge T,{\eta}(t\wedge T)\Big)-\int_{0}^{t\wedge T}\left(2g(t^{\prime},{\eta}(t^{\prime}-))\left(\frac{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}}{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 64\relax}}}t^{\prime}}g(\cdot,{\eta}(t^{\prime}-))\right)(t^{\prime})+\Big(\mathcal{L}g^{2}(t^{\prime},\cdot)\Big)({\eta}(t^{\prime}-))\right)\,dt^{\prime}\Bigg)_{t\geq 0}

is a martingale with respect to the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}^{{\eta}}_{t+}\}_{t\geq 0}
. Since the remainder of the proof follows exactly the same arguments as in the classical case, we refer the interested reader to, for instance,
[
48
, Lemma A.1.5.1]
. We then conclude that
\Big(\Big(M^{g}(t\wedge T)\Big)^{2}-A^{g}(t)\Big)_{t\geq 0}
is a martingale with respect to the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}^{{\eta}}_{t+}\}_{t\geq 0}
, which completes the proof.
∎

0.A.2
Random walk estimates

In this subsection, we will state some random walk estimates which are consequences of the local central limit theorem (see
[
55
, Chapter 2]
for a review of the local central limit theorem). These estimates are used to control the spatial and time increments of
u^{N}
in Section
4
. For
t\geq 0
and
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, recall the definition of
p^{N}(t,x)
from (
4.3
). We will need the following inequalities, most of which can be found in
[
25
]
.

Lemma 0.A.3

(Random walk estimates)
.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds. Then there exists
C>0
such that for all
{\tau}\geq 0
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{1},x_{2}\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and
T\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}p^{N}(t,0)\,dt\leq C\sqrt{T},

(0.A.3)

\displaystyle 0\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\left(p^{N}(t,0)-p^{N}(t+{\tau},0)\right)\,dt\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(p^{N}(t,0)-p^{N}(t+{\tau},0)\right)\,dt\leq C\sqrt{{\tau}},

(0.A.4)

\displaystyle 0\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\left(p^{N}(t,0)-p^{N}(t,x_{1})\right)\,dt\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(p^{N}(t,0)-p^{N}(t,x_{1})\right)\,dt\leq C|x_{1}|,

(0.A.5)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{T}\left|p^{N}(t,y)-p^{N}(t+{\tau},y)\right|dt\leq C\sqrt{T{\tau}},

(0.A.6)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{T}\left|p^{N}\left(t,y-x_{1}\right)-p^{N}\left(t,y-x_{2}\right)\right|dt\leq C\sqrt{T}|x_{1}-x_{2}|,

(0.A.7)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{T}p^{N}(T-t,y)^{2}\,dt\leq C\sqrt{T},

(0.A.8)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{T}\Big(p^{N}(T+{\tau}-t,y-x_{1})-p^{N}(T-t,y-x_{2})\Big)^{2}\,dt\leq C(\sqrt{{\tau}}+|x_{1}-x_{2}|).

(0.A.9)

Proof.

Estimates (
0.A.3
), (
0.A.4
), (
0.A.5
), (
0.A.6
) and (
0.A.7
) are stated explicitly in
[
25
]
(see inequalities (46), (47), (48), (49) and (50) in
[
25
]
, which follow from Proposition 2.4.1 and Theorem 2.5.6 in
[
55
]
). It remains to verify estimates (
0.A.8
) and (
0.A.9
). As noted in
[
25
, Section 7]
, by symmetry and then by the Chapman-Kolmogorov equation, for all
t\geq 0
, we have

\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}p^{N}(t,y)^{2}=\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}p^{N}(t,y)p^{N}(t,-y)=p^{N}(2t,0).

(0.A.10)

Hence, by Fubini’s theorem and then by estimate (
0.A.3
), for all
T\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{T}p^{N}(T-t,y)^{2}\,dt=\int_{0}^{T}p^{N}(2(T-t),0)\,dt\leq\tfrac{1}{2}C\sqrt{2T},

Hence, estimate (
0.A.8
) is proved. In order to prove (
0.A.9
), we write

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{T}\left(p^{N}\left(T+{\tau}-t,y-x_{1}\right)-p^{N}\left(T-t,y-x_{2}\right)\right)^{2}dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{T}\left(p^{N}\left(T+{\tau}-t,y-x_{1}\right)^{2}+p^{N}\left(T-t,y-x_{2}\right)^{2}\right)dt

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{T}2p^{N}\left(T+{\tau}-t,y-x_{1}\right)p^{N}\left(T-t,y-x_{2}\right)dt.

Applying the Chapman-Kolmogorov equation to the previous identity as in (
0.A.10
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L_{N}}\sum_{y\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{T}\left(p^{N}\left(T+{\tau}-t,y-x_{1}\right)-p^{N}\left(T-t,y-x_{2}\right)\right)^{2}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\left(p^{N}\left(2(T+{\tau}-t),0\right)+p^{N}\left(2(T-t),0\right)-2p^{N}\left(2T+{\tau}-2t,x_{2}-x_{1}\right)\right)dt

\displaystyle\leq C\tfrac{1+\sqrt{2}}{2}\sqrt{{\tau}}+C|x_{1}-x_{2}|,

where the last inequality is derived after adding and subtracting
2p^{N}\left(2T+{\tau}-2t,0\right)
inside the integral and applying estimates (
0.A.4
) and (
0.A.5
). This completes the proof.
∎

We also use the following version of the local central limit theorem for continuous-time random walks that can be found in
[
55
, Theorem 2.5.6]
.

Lemma 0.A.4

(Local central limit theorem)
.

For
t>0
and
x\in L_{N}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
with
|x|\leq m_{N}t/(2L_{N})
,

p^{N}(t,x)=\frac{L_{N}}{\sqrt{2{\pi}m_{N}t}}\exp\left(-\frac{L_{N}^{2}}{m_{N}}\frac{x^{2}}{2t}+O\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{m_{N}t}}+\frac{L_{N}^{3}|x|^{3}}{m_{N}^{2}t^{2}}\right)\right).

0.A.3
Brownian motion and Gaussian estimates

In this subsection, we collect some Brownian motion estimates. For
(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
, recall the definition of the Gaussian kernel
p(t,x)
in (
3.3
). We start by establishing bounds on the spatial and temporal increments of
p
.

Lemma 0.A.5
.

Suppose
m>0
, and let
p:(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow(0,\infty)
be defined as in (
3.3
). The following estimates hold:

(i)

There exists
C^{(1)}=C^{(1)}(m)>0
such that for all
t>0
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
,

\left|p(t,x)-p(t,y)\right|\leq\frac{C^{(1)}|x-y|}{\sqrt{t}}(p(2t,x)+p(2t,y)).

(0.A.11)

(ii)

There exists
C^{(2)}=C^{(2)}(m)>0
such that for all
t>0
and
t^{\prime}\geq 0
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}}|p(t+t^{\prime},x)-p(t,x)|\,dx\leq C^{(2)}\sqrt{\frac{t^{\prime}}{t}}.

(0.A.12)

Proof.

Estimate (
0.A.11
) is standard (see e.g.
[
27
, Lemma 7.7]
). For estimate (
0.A.12
), the case
t^{\prime}=0
is trivial. For
t^{\prime}>0
, observe that by the semigroup property of the Gaussian kernel, we have for all
t,t^{\prime}>0
,

p(t+t^{\prime},x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}p(t,x-y)p(t^{\prime},y)\,dy\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}.

Therefore for
t,t^{\prime}>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}|p(t+t^{\prime},x)-p(t,x)|\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}p(t,x-y)p(t^{\prime},y)\,dy-p(t,x)\right|\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|p(t,x-y)-p(t,x)|p(t^{\prime},y)\,dy\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C^{(1)}}{\sqrt{t}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|y|p(t^{\prime},y)(p(2t,x-y)+p(2t,x))\,dy\,dx

\displaystyle=\frac{C^{(1)}}{\sqrt{t}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|y|p(t^{\prime},y)(p(2t,x-y)+p(2t,x))\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle=\frac{2C^{(1)}}{\sqrt{t}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|y|p(t^{\prime},y)\,dy,

where for the third line we used estimate (
0.A.11
), and for the fourth line we used Fubini’s theorem. Using the elementary identity

\int_{\mathbb{R}}|y|p(t^{\prime},y)\,dy=\sqrt{\frac{2mt^{\prime}}{{\pi}}},

we then conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}|p(t+t^{\prime},x)-p(t,x)|\,dx\leq 2C^{(1)}\sqrt{\frac{2mt^{\prime}}{{\pi}t}}.

Therefore (
0.A.12
) holds. This completes the proof.
∎

Next, we collect some results that are straightforward modifications of the Brownian motion estimates in
[
58
,
69
,
64
]
. We refer the reader interested in the proof of each estimate to the indicated reference.

Lemma 0.A.6
.

Let
(W(t))_{t\geq 0}
be a Brownian motion run at speed
m>0
, with
W(0)=0
under
\mathbb{P}_{0}
. Then the following estimates are satisfied:

(i)

[
58
, Lemma 12.9]
For any
T>0
and
x>0
,

\mathbb{P}_{0}(W(T)>x)\leq\frac{(mT)^{1/2}}{x\sqrt{2{\pi}}}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2mT}\right).

(0.A.13)

(ii)

[
64
, Equation (12)]
For any
0\leq x\leq y
and
t>0
,

\frac{y-x}{\sqrt{2{\pi}mt}}\exp\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{2mt}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{0}(W(t)\in[x,y])\leq\frac{y-x}{\sqrt{2{\pi}mt}}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2mt}\right).

(0.A.14)

(iii)

[
69
, Lemma 5]
For
T>0
, there exists
C^{(1)}_{T}>0
such that for any
t\geq T
and
0\leq R\leq 1
,

\mathbb{P}_{0}\Big(|W({\tau})|\leq 1\;\forall{\tau}\leq t,\;|W(t)|\leq R\Big)\geq C^{(1)}_{T}\exp\left(-\frac{{\pi}^{2}mt}{8}\right)\min\left(R,\tfrac{2}{3}\right).

(0.A.15)

(iv)

[
64
, Equation (19)]
For
R>0
and
c^{*}>0
, there exists
C^{(2)}_{R}=C^{(2)}_{R}(c^{*})>0
such that for
b\in\mathbb{R}
with
|b|\leq c^{*}
,
r\in[0,R]
and
t\geq 1
,

\mathbb{P}_{0}\Big(|W({\tau})-b{\tau}|\leq R\;\forall{\tau}\leq t,\;|W(t)-bt|\leq r\Big)\geq C^{(2)}_{R}r\exp\left(-\frac{b^{2}t}{2m}-\frac{{\pi}^{2}mt}{8R^{2}}\right).

(0.A.16)

Next, we prove that, analogous to its action on real-valued functions, the Gaussian kernel has a smoothing effect on
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
-valued maps. Recall the definition of the Brownian semigroup
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
in (
3.4
).

Lemma 0.A.7
.

Let
T>0
. Let

f:\mathbb{R}\to{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}^{+}
and
g:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\to{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
be such that

f\in L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})\quad\textrm{and}\quad g\in L_{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}).

For
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
, define

v(t,x):=(P_{t}f)(x)+\int_{0}^{t}(P_{t-{\tau}}g({\tau},\cdot))(x)\,d{\tau}.

(0.A.17)

Then, for all
{\delta}\in(0,T)
, there exists
C_{{\delta},T,f,g}>0
such that for all
t_{1},t_{2}\in[{\delta},T]
and
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\|v(t_{1},x_{1})-v(t_{2},x_{2})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\leq C_{{\delta},T,f,g}\left(|x_{1}-x_{2}|+|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{1/2}\right).

(0.A.18)

In particular,
v\in\mathscr{C}((0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
.

Proof.

We prove (
0.A.18
) by controlling the spatial and temporal increments separately. Take
{\delta}\in(0,T)
, and then take
t_{1},t_{2}\in[{\delta},T]
and
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. The triangle inequality yields

\|v(t_{1},x_{1})-v(t_{2},x_{2})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\leq\|v(t_{1},x_{1})-v(t_{1},x_{2})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}+\|v(t_{1},x_{2})-v(t_{2},x_{2})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}.

(0.A.19)

We now bound the terms on the right-hand side of (
0.A.19
). Recall the definition of the Gaussian kernel
p:(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow(0,\infty)
in (
3.3
). For the first term on the right-hand side of (
0.A.19
), by using (
0.A.17
) and (
3.4
), and then by Jensen’s inequality,

\displaystyle\|v(t_{1},x_{1})-v(t_{1},x_{2})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}|p(t_{1},x_{1}-y)-p(t_{1},x_{2}-y)|\cdot\|f(y)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\,dy

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|p(t_{1}-{\tau},x_{1}-y)-p(t_{1}-{\tau},x_{2}-y)|\cdot\|g({\tau},y)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\,dy\,d{\tau}

\displaystyle\leq\|f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|p(t_{1},x_{1}-y)-p(t_{1},x_{2}-y)|\,dy

\displaystyle+\|g\|_{L_{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|p(t_{1}-{\tau},x_{1}-y)-p(t_{1}-{\tau},x_{2}-y)|\,dy\,d{\tau},

where for the second estimate we used the fact that
f\in L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
and
g\in L_{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
. By applying estimate (
0.A.11
) from Lemma
0.A.5
and using the fact that
t_{1}\in[{\delta},T]
, we conclude that there exists
C^{(1)}=C^{(1)}(m)>0
such that

\displaystyle\|v(t_{1},x_{1})-v(t_{1},x_{2})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}

(0.A.20)

\displaystyle\leq C^{(1)}\|f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|}{\sqrt{t_{1}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(p(2t_{1},x_{1}-y)+p(2t_{1},x_{2}-y))\,dy

\displaystyle+C^{(1)}\|g\|_{L_{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|}{\sqrt{t_{1}-{\tau}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(p(2(t_{1}-{\tau}),x_{1}-y)+p(2(t_{1}-{\tau}),x_{2}-y))\,dy\,d{\tau}

\displaystyle\leq C^{(1)}\left(\frac{2\|f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}}{\sqrt{{\delta}}}+4\|g\|_{L_{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\sqrt{T}\right)|x_{1}-x_{2}|.

For the second term on the right-hand side of (
0.A.19
), by using (
0.A.17
), (
3.4
) and (
3.3
), and then by Jensen’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\|v(t_{1},x_{2})-v(t_{2},x_{2})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}|p(t_{1},x_{2}-y)-p(t_{2},x_{2}-y)|\cdot\|f(y)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\,dy

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t_{1}\wedge t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|p(t_{1}-{\tau},x_{2}-y)-p(t_{2}-{\tau},x_{2}-y)|\|g({\tau},y)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\,dy\,d{\tau}

\displaystyle+\int_{t_{1}\wedge t_{2}}^{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}p((t_{1}\vee t_{2})-{\tau},x_{2}-y)\|g({\tau},y)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\,dy\,d{\tau}

\displaystyle\leq|t_{1}-t_{2}|\cdot\|g\|_{L_{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}+\|f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|p(t_{1},x_{2}-y)-p(t_{2},x_{2}-y)|\,dy

\displaystyle+\|g\|_{L_{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\int_{0}^{t_{1}\wedge t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|p(t_{1}-{\tau},x_{2}-y)-p(t_{2}-{\tau},x_{2}-y)|\,dy\,d{\tau}.

By applying estimate (
0.A.12
) from Lemma
0.A.5
and using the fact that we chose
t_{1},t_{2}
such that
t_{1},t_{2}\in[{\delta},T]
, we conclude that there exists
C^{(2)}=C^{(2)}(m)>0
such that

\displaystyle\|v(t_{1},x_{2})-v(t_{2},x_{2})\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}

(0.A.21)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{T}\|g\|_{L_{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{1/2}+C^{(2)}\|f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\sqrt{\frac{|t_{1}-t_{2}|}{t_{1}\wedge t_{2}}}

\displaystyle+C^{(2)}\|g\|_{L_{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\int_{0}^{t_{1}\wedge t_{2}}\sqrt{\frac{|t_{1}-t_{2}|}{(t_{1}\wedge t_{2})-{\tau}}}\,d{\tau}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sqrt{T}(1+2C^{(2)})\|g\|_{L_{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}+\frac{C^{(2)}\|f\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}}{\sqrt{{\delta}}}\right)|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{1/2}.

Estimate (
0.A.18
) then follows from applying (
0.A.20
) and (
0.A.21
) to (
0.A.19
). Since (
0.A.18
) implies that
v\in\mathscr{C}((0,T]\times\mathbb{R};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
, the proof is complete.
∎

0.A.4
Topological properties of
L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})

In this subsection, we will prove Lemma
6.3
and some properties regarding the Banach space
L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
introduced in (
6.2
). For this purpose, we need to state a general criterion for compact subsets of
L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
. Recall the definition of the measure
\hat{{\lambda}}
in (
6.1
), and the definition of the functional space
L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})
in (
6.4
). Let
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{\infty}
denote the space of bounded real-valued sequences, and recall that
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{\infty}
can be identified with the dual space of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
. Define for every
w\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{\infty}
, the map

\displaystyle{\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}_{w}:L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})

\displaystyle\rightarrow L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\mapsto{\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}_{w}(v),

where, for any
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
,

{\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}_{w}(v)(t,x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}w_{k}v_{k}(t,x).

(0.A.22)

Observe that for every
w\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{\infty}
and
v\in L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})
,

\|{\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}_{w}(v)\|_{L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})}\leq\|w\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{\infty}}\|v\|_{L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})},

(0.A.23)

and so
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}_{w}
is well defined. We are now ready to state a version of Díaz and Mayoral’s compactness theorem from
[
22
]
which is adapted to our specific setting.

Theorem 0.A.8
.

(Special case of
[
22
, Theorem 3.2]
)

Let
T>0
and
r\in[1,\infty)
be fixed. A subset
\mathscr{K}\subset L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
is relatively compact if and only if the following conditions are satisfied:

(i)

\mathscr{K}
is
\|\cdot\|_{L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}
-bounded, i.e.

\sup_{v\in\mathscr{K}}\,\|v\|_{L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}<\infty.

(ii)

\mathscr{K}
is
r
-uniformly integrable, i.e.

\lim_{{\gamma}\rightarrow\infty}\;\sup_{v\in\mathscr{K}}\Big|\Big|v\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{\|v\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}>{\gamma}\}}\Big|\Big|_{L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\;=0.

(iii)

\mathscr{K}
is uniformly tight, i.e. for every
{\delta}>0
, there exists a compact subset
\mathcal{A}_{{\delta}}\subset{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
such that

\sup_{v\in\mathscr{K}}\;\hat{{\lambda}}(\{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}:\;v(t,x)\not\in\mathcal{A}_{{\delta}}\})\leq{\delta}.

(iv)

\mathscr{K}
is scalarly relatively compact, i.e. for every
w=(w_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{\infty}
, the set

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}^{*}_{w}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Big\{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}_{w}(v):v\in\mathscr{K}\Big\}

is a relatively compact subset of
L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})
.

In order to apply Theorem
0.A.8
, we recall a characterisation of relatively compact subsets of

L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})
,
which follows from a straightforward adaptation of the
Kolmogorov–Riesz–Fréchet compactness theorem to the weighted measure
\hat{{\lambda}}
defined in (
6.1
) (see e.g.
[
19
, Theorem 4.26]
). Recall, for
i\in\{1,2\}
and
{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}
, the definition of the shift maps
{\theta}^{(i)}_{{\gamma}}
in (
6.5
).

Theorem 0.A.9

(Special case of Kolmogorov-Riesz-Fréchet’s compactness theorem)
.

Let
T>0
and
r\in[1,\infty)
be fixed. A bounded
subset
\mathscr{K}\subset L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})
is relatively compact in
L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})
if and only if the following limits hold:

\displaystyle\lim_{|{\gamma}|\downarrow 0}\;

\displaystyle\sup_{v^{*}\in\mathscr{K}}\;\left(\|{\theta}^{(1)}_{{\gamma}}v^{*}-v^{*}\|_{L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})}+\|{\theta}^{(2)}_{{\gamma}}v^{*}-v^{*}\|_{L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})}\right)=0,

(0.A.24)

\displaystyle\textrm{and }\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}\;

\displaystyle\sup_{v^{*}\in\mathscr{K}}\;\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus[-R,R]}\frac{|v^{*}(t,x)|^{r}}{1+|x|^{2}}=0.

(0.A.25)

Remark 0.A.10
.

For each
i\in\{1,2\}
, the translation operators
{\theta}^{(i)}_{\gamma}

are strongly continuous on

L_{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})
, that is,
for all
g\in L_{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})
,

\lim_{|{\gamma}|\downarrow 0}\|{\theta}^{(i)}_{\gamma}g-g\|_{L_{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})}=0.

This property follows, for instance, by applying
Theorem
0.A.9
to the singleton set
\{g\}
,
or directly from the fact that
\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R})
is a dense subset of

L_{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})
.

We are now ready to prove Lemma
6.3
.

Proof of Lemma
6.3
.

It will suffice to establish that
\mathscr{K}=\mathscr{K}(C_{1},C_{2},(k_{h})_{h\in\mathbb{N}},(D_{h})_{h\in\mathbb{N}},{\beta},J,T)
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
0.A.8
. We will divide the proof into steps corresponding to each of the conditions (i)-(iv) of Theorem
0.A.8
.

Step
(1)
: Boundedness

Since
\hat{{\lambda}}
, defined in (
6.1
), is a finite measure on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
, we may apply Hölder’s inequality with exponents
2r/q
and
2r/(2r-q)
to conclude that for any
q\in[1,2r)
,

\sup_{v\in\mathscr{K}}\;\|v\|_{{L}_{q}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\leq\hat{{\lambda}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R})^{(2r-q)/2rq}\sup_{v\in\mathscr{K}}\;\|v\|_{{L}_{2r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\lesssim_{T,r,q}C_{1},

(0.A.26)

where the last inequality comes from the fact that
\mathscr{K}\subset\mathscr{K}_{(1)}(C_{1},T)
, and that, by definition, the set
\mathscr{K}_{(1)}(C_{1},T)
is
\|\cdot\|_{{L}_{2r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}
-bounded by
C_{1}
. Hence, applying (
0.A.26
) with
q=r
we conclude that
\mathscr{K}
is bounded.

Step
(2)
:
r
-Uniform integrability

We need to show that

\lim_{{\gamma}\rightarrow\infty}\;\sup_{v\in\mathscr{K}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\|v(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}^{r}\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{\|v(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}>{\gamma}\}}}{1+|x|^{2}}\,dx\,dt=0.

(0.A.27)

Observe that by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, for every
{\gamma}>0
and every
v\in\mathscr{K}
, we can bound the left-hand side of (
0.A.27
) by

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\|v(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}^{r}\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{\|v(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}>{\gamma}\}}}{1+|x|^{2}}\,dx\,dt

(0.A.28)

\displaystyle\leq\Bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\|v(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}^{2r}}{1+|x|^{2}}\,dx\,dt\Bigg)^{1/2}\hat{{\lambda}}\Big(\left\{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}:\;\|v(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}>{\gamma}\right\}\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\sup_{v^{\prime}\in\mathscr{K}}\;\|v^{\prime}\|_{L_{2r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\Big)^{r}\cdot\Bigg(\frac{\sup_{v^{\prime}\in\mathscr{K}}\;\|v^{\prime}\|_{L_{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}}{{\gamma}}\Bigg)^{1/2},

where for the second inequality we applied Markov’s inequality. Applying (
0.A.26
) with
q=1
and using the fact that
\mathscr{K}\subset\mathscr{K}_{(1)}(C_{1},T)
, and hence
\mathscr{K}
is
\|\cdot\|_{{L}_{2r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}
-bounded, and then taking the limit as
{\gamma}\rightarrow\infty
, we conclude that (
0.A.27
) holds, which completes the proof of step (2).

Step
(3)
: Uniform tightness

We must establish that for every
{\delta}>0
, there exists a compact subset
\mathcal{A}_{{\delta}}\subset{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
such that

\sup_{v\in\mathscr{K}}\;\hat{{\lambda}}(\{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}:\;v(t,x)\not\in\mathcal{A}_{{\delta}}\})\leq{\delta}.

(0.A.29)

We first recall (see, for instance,
[
23
, Exercise I.6]
) that
\mathcal{A}\subset{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
is relatively compact if and only if the following conditions hold:

(a)

\mathcal{A}
is
\|\cdot\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}
-bounded;

(b)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\;\sup_{z=(z_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in\mathcal{A}}\;\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}|z_{j}|=0
.

Recall the sequences
(k_{h})_{h\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(D_{h})_{h\in\mathbb{N}}
introduced in the statement of Lemma
6.3
. Observe that since
D_{h}\rightarrow 0
as
h\rightarrow\infty
and
(k_{h})_{h\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
is strictly increasing, by passing to a subsequence of
(k_{h})_{h\in\mathbb{N}}
, we can assume without loss of generality that
D_{h}\leq 2^{-h}
for every
h\in\mathbb{N}
. For
{\delta}>0
, let
\mathcal{A}_{{\delta}}\subset{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
be given by

\mathcal{A}_{{\delta}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Bigg\{z=(z_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}:\;\|z\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\leq\frac{2}{{\delta}}\sup_{v\in\mathscr{K}}\;\|v\|_{{L}_{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R};\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\;\textrm{ and }\;\sum_{j=k_{2h}}^{\infty}|z_{j}|\leq\frac{1}{{\delta}2^{h-1}}\;\forall\,h\in\mathbb{N}\Bigg\}.

(0.A.30)

Since, using (
0.A.26
) with
q=1
,
\mathcal{A}_{\delta}
satisfies conditions (a) and (b) above, we have that
\mathcal{A}_{{\delta}}
is a compact subset of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}
, for every
{\delta}>0
. Therefore, it will suffice to establish that for any
{\delta}>0
, (
0.A.29
) holds with the choice of
\mathcal{A}_{{\delta}}
given in (
0.A.30
).

Observe that by Markov’s inequality, for every
v\in\mathscr{K}
,

\hat{{\lambda}}\left(\left\{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}:\;\|v(t,x)\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}>\frac{2}{{\delta}}\sup_{v^{\prime}\in\mathscr{K}}\;\|v^{\prime}\|_{{L}_{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}\right\}\right)\leq\frac{{\delta}}{2}.

(0.A.31)

Recall the definition of
\mathscr{K}_{(2)}((k_{h})_{h\in\mathbb{N}},(D_{h})_{h\in\mathbb{N}},T)
in the statement of this lemma, and recall our assumption that
D_{h}\leq 2^{-h}\;\forall\,h\in\mathbb{N}
, which is possible as explained before (
0.A.30
). Hence, using Markov’s inequality, we conclude that for every
v=(v_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in\mathscr{K}
and
h\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\hat{{\lambda}}\left(\left\{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}:\;\sum_{j=k_{2h}}^{\infty}\;|v_{j}(t,x)|>\frac{1}{{\delta}2^{h-1}}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq{\delta}2^{h-1}\sum_{j=k_{2h}}^{\infty}\|v_{j}\|_{L_{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})}

(0.A.32)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{{\delta}}{2^{h+1}}.

Combining (
0.A.31
) and (
0.A.32
), and then by the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{{\delta}}
in (
0.A.30
) and a union bound, we have that (
0.A.29
) holds, which completes the proof of step (3).

Step
(4)
: Scalarly relative compactness

We must establish that for every
w\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{\infty}
, the set
\mathscr{K}^{*}_{w}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Big\{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}_{w}(v):\;v\in\mathscr{K}\Big\}
is relatively compact in
L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})
. Fix
w=(w_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{\infty}
, and note that by (
0.A.23
) and (
0.A.26
),
\mathscr{K}^{*}_{w}
is
\|\cdot\|_{L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})}
-bounded. Therefore, by Theorem
0.A.9
, it will suffice to prove that the limits (
0.A.24
) and (
0.A.25
) hold with
\mathscr{K}
replaced by
\mathscr{K}^{*}_{w}
. Starting with (
0.A.25
), observe that since
w\in{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{\infty}
, and then by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have for every
R>0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{v=(v_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in\mathscr{K}}\;\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus[-R,R]}\frac{\Big|\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}w_{k}v_{k}(t,x)\Big|^{r}}{1+|x|^{2}}\,dx\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\|w\|^{r}_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{\infty}}\sup_{v\in\mathscr{K}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\|v(t,x)\|^{r}_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1}}\cdot\mathds{1}_{\{|x|>R\}}}{1+|x|^{2}}\,dx\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\|w\|_{{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{\infty}}^{r}\sup_{v\in\mathscr{K}}\|v\|_{L_{2r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})}^{r}\hat{{\lambda}}([0,T]\times(\mathbb{R}\setminus[-R,R]))^{1/2}.

Since
\mathscr{K}_{(1)}(C_{1},T)
, and hence
\mathscr{K}
, is bounded in
L_{2r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};{\mathord{\hbox{\char 96\relax}}}_{1})
, by taking the limit as
R\rightarrow\infty
and then applying the definition of
\hat{{\lambda}}
in (
6.1
), by the definition of
{\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}_{w}(v)
in (
0.A.22
) we obtain (
0.A.25
) with
\mathscr{K}
replaced by
\mathscr{K}^{*}_{w}
.

We now proceed to the proof of (
0.A.24
) with
\mathscr{K}
replaced by
\mathscr{K}^{*}_{w}
. It will suffice to establish that there exists
C_{r,T,\mathscr{K},w}>0
such that for any
{\varepsilon}>0
, there exists
{\delta}={\delta}(r,T,{\varepsilon},\mathscr{K},w)>0
such that for
|{\gamma}|<{\delta}
and
i\in\{1,2\}
,

\sup_{v\in\mathscr{K}}\;\|{\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}_{w}(v)-{\theta}^{(i)}_{{\gamma}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}_{w}(v)\|^{r}_{L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})}\leq C_{r,T,\mathscr{K},w}{\varepsilon}.

(0.A.33)

For any
{\gamma}\in(-1,1)
and each
i\in\{1,2\}
, for any
H\in\mathbb{N}
, by applying the elementary inequality

|a+b|^{r}\leq 2^{r-1}(|a|^{r}+|b|^{r})\quad\forall\,a,b\in\mathbb{R},

(0.A.34)

we have

\displaystyle\|{\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}_{w}(v)-{\theta}^{(i)}_{{\gamma}}{\mathord{\hbox{\char 9\relax}}}_{w}(v)\|^{r}_{L_{r}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2^{r-1}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{1+|x|^{2}}\Bigg(\sum_{k=0}^{H}|w_{k}v_{k}(t,x)-w_{k}({\theta}^{(i)}_{{\gamma}}v_{k})(t,x)|\Bigg)^{r}\,dx\,dt

(0.A.35)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+2^{r-1}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{1+|x|^{2}}\Bigg(\sum_{k=H+1}^{\infty}|w_{k}v_{k}(t,x)-w_{k}({\theta}^{(i)}_{{\gamma}}v_{k})(t,x)|\Bigg)^{r}\,dx\,dt.

(0.A.36)

We will bound the integrals (
0.A.35
) and (
0.A.36
) separately. Starting with (
0.A.36
), observing that

\displaystyle\Bigg(\sum_{k=H+1}^{\infty}|w_{k}v_{k}-w_{k}({\theta}^{(i)}_{{\gamma}}v_{k})|\Bigg)^{r}

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\sum_{k=H+1}^{\infty}|w_{k}v_{k}-w_{k}({\theta}^{(i)}_{{\gamma}}v_{k})|\Bigg)^{1/r}\Bigg(\sum_{k=H+1}^{\infty}|w_{k}v_{k}-w_{k}({\theta}^{(i)}_{{\gamma}}v_{k})|\Bigg)^{(r^{2}-1)/r},

and then by applying Hölder’s inequality with exponents
r
and
r/(r-1)
in the case
r>1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{1+|x|^{2}}\Bigg(\sum_{k=H+1}^{\infty}|w_{k}v_{k}(t,x)-w_{k}({\theta}^{(i)}_{{\gamma}}v_{k})(t,x)|\Bigg)^{r}dx\,dt

(0.A.37)

\displaystyle\leq\Bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{1+|x|^{2}}\sum_{k=H+1}^{\infty}|w_{k}v_{k}(t,x)-w_{k}({\theta}^{(i)}_{{\gamma}}v_{k})(t,x)|\,dx\,dt\Bigg)^{1/r}

\displaystyle\cdot\Bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{1+|x|^{2}}\Bigg(\sum_{k=H+1}^{\infty}|w_{k}v_{k}(t,x)-w_{k}({\theta}^{(i)}_{{\gamma}}v_{k})(t,x)|\Bigg)^{r+1}dx\,dt\Bigg)^{(r-1)/r}.

We will bound each factor on the right-hand side of (
0.A.37
) separately. Starting with the second factor, recall the definition of
{\theta}^{(i)}_{\gamma}
in (
6.5
), and observe that since
|x+{\gamma}|^{2}\leq 2|x|^{2}+2|{\gamma}|^{2}
, we have

\frac{1+|x+{\gamma}|^{2}}{1+|x|^{2}}\leq 3\quad\forall\,{\gamma}\in(-1,1)\textrm{ and }\forall\,x\in\mathbb{R}.

(0.A.38)

Hence, by applying the elementary inequality (
0.A.34
), and then a suitable substitution, we conclude that for all
{\gamma}\in(-1,1)
,
q\geq 1
,
g\in L_{q}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})
and
i\in\{1,2\}
,

\|{\theta}^{(i)}_{{\gamma}}g-g\|_{L_{q}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})}^{q}\leq 2^{q}\cdot 3\cdot\|g\|_{L_{q}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R},\hat{{\lambda}};\mathbb{R})}^{q}.

(0.A.39)

Using (
0.A.39
) with
g=\sum_{k=H+1}^{\infty}|w_{k}v_{k}|
and
q=r+1
